```
set_type => array ^ { ordered?: = false }                  ; constructor refinement (§4.2), in a meta-schema
id      => uuid                                            ; type reference (§8.3)
scores  => [integer; 1..]                                  ; array type (§5.3)
point   => [number, number]                                ; tuple type (§5.3)
contact_method => (email | phone | address)                ; choice type (§5.4)
translations   => {text => text}                           ; map type sugar (§5.3)
```


### 5.2 Field Definitions

A field in a record definition answers three independent questions, and the spelling has one slot for each:

```
name?: type? ~ default
name?: type? = fixed
name:  type  =?
```

1. **May the key be omitted?** `?` on the **name**. Unmarked, the key MUST be written; marked, it MAY be omitted. This is the input question and nothing else — what omission then *yields* is the modifier's business (below). Required by default is the right choice for a typed notation, and the one structured-output validation wants.
2. **May a written value be `_`?** `?` on the **type**. It means at a field exactly what it means at every other position that admits it — an array element, a map entry value, a tuple position (§5.3, §7.6): the void sentinel is admitted here. A field whose type carries `?` is **voidable**. The record field is the one position where an earlier revision's `?` also answered the omission question, and that overload was the source of four states no spelling could reach.
3. **What may a written value be?** The **modifier**. No modifier — the value is **free**; `~ token` — the value is a **default**, supplied when the key is omitted and overridable by a written value; `= token` — the value is **fixed**, and a written value MUST equal it; `=?` — the value is **pinned by the members** of a discriminated family, a selector, and is discussed below. Whitespace around `~`, `=` and `=?` is optional.

```
config => {
  host:     text                  ; key written, _ refused, any value
  port?:    integer ~ 8080        ; key may be omitted; omission yields 8080
  debug?:   boolean = false       ; key may be omitted; omission yields false; a written value must be false
  label?:   text                  ; key may be omitted; _ refused
  timeout:  duration?             ; key written; _ admitted — "no timeout" is a thing this document can say
  proxy?:   uri? ~ "http://gw"    ; omitted: the default; _: none; a value: the value
  version:  text = "2.0"          ; a marker the document must write, and must write as "2.0"
}
```

**What omission yields is derived, never stored.** A field whose name is unmarked MUST have its key written — a missing key there is a validation error. A field whose name is marked yields, where its key is missing, its value if it has one (`~ v` or `= v`, injected) and nothing otherwise: the field stays missing. So the three slots read as one table:

| Spelling | Key in the data | `_` in the data | Value in the data | A missing key yields |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `a: T` | written | refused | any | — (an error) |
| `a?: T` | may be omitted | refused | any | nothing |
| `a: T?` | written | admitted | any | — (an error) |
| `a?: T?` | may be omitted | admitted | any | nothing |
| `a?: T ~ v` | may be omitted | refused | any | `v` |
| `a?: T? ~ v` | may be omitted | admitted | any | `v` |
| `a?: T = v` | may be omitted | refused | `v` only | `v` |
| `a: T = v` | written | refused | `v` only | — (an error) |
| `a?: void?` | may be omitted | admitted | none | nothing |
| `a: void?` | written | admitted | none | — (an error) |
| `a?: void` | may be omitted | refused | none | nothing |

The last three are the `void` type in the type slot rather than field states of their own: `void` admits nothing but `_` (§4.2, §7.3), so a `void`-typed field is one that may carry no value. `a?: void?` is a field that may be missing or written as `_` and nothing else — the spelling an earlier revision wrote `= _`. `a?: void` is a field that may be omitted and never written, which empties the field and leaves the record inhabitable; it is legal, since a declared field still participates in closure (§7.2) and a removal clause can name it (§5.9). `a: void` (key written, `_` refused, no value admitted) empties the *record* — no document satisfies it — and is a resolver error under §5.10.1's productivity rule.

**Four spellings are refused, as rules rather than omissions:**

- **A default on an unmarked name** — `a: T ~ v`, `a: T? ~ v`. An unmarked name says the key is always written, and `~ v` is a value only omission can reach; the diagnostic SHOULD suggest `a?: T ~ v`.
- **A pin on a voidable type** — `a: T? = v`, `a?: T? = v`. A pin names the only value the field admits, and `_` is not it: the `_` question is settled before the pin is consulted, and admitting the pair would let question 2 admit a value question 3 refuses. Nothing in any measured corpus asks for the cell.
- **A modifier on a `void`-typed field**, and the sentinel as a modifier value (`~ _`, `= _`). `void` has nothing to default or pin, and `_` is what the type slot spells.
- **The selector `=?` on anything but an unmarked name and a non-voidable type** (below).

**Which fields may carry a value.** A `~` or `=` value is admitted only on a field whose declared type resolves, after following its reference chain (§8.3), to an **atom-family instance or an enum** — the types a single scalar token denotes directly. On a field of any other declared type — a record, container, tuple, choice, `void`, or a `scoped` instance — a value modifier is a resolver error at the declaration, whatever token stands beside it. The positional form (§5.6) is a spelling rule for data values and does not make a record a token: admitting `p?: point ~ 3` would make a field's eligibility for a default depend on another declaration's field count, so that adding a second field to `point` silently invalidated a default written elsewhere. The refusal costs two spellings and buys a rule that fits in one line. Value modifiers are restricted to scalar tokens — quoted or unquoted — covering strings, numbers, booleans and enum members; complex modifier values (arrays, records, maps) are not supported in v1 (§12.1).

**Value conformance.** A fixed or default value MUST conform to the field's declared type — the dependency the kernel's `record_field.value` slot cannot express structurally is stated here normatively. The check is the field's own parsing contract (§7.4): a value is accepted exactly when a read would accept the same token at that position. It applies wherever the value came from: written literally (`{ first?: int32 ~ "nope" }` is refused at the declaration), or routed from a template parameter and checked at materialisation (`retry => <N> { attempts?: int32 ~ N }` applied as `retry<text>` is refused identically, §5.10). The same rule is where a routed parameter's type comes from: a parameter standing in a value slot is recorded with the field's declared type, so conformance is checked at the application, against the parameter list, before anything is substituted (§5.10).

**Eager resolution.** Default and fixed value tokens are resolved and validated at schema-load time. The token is parsed by the field's type — by the atom's parser; a `value`-typed field is read by the atom the position stands for once that atom is in scope (§7.4) — and stored as the resolved host value. A default or fixed value that fails parsing or the type's constraints is a resolver error, reported at schema load. This matches §7.4's eager-conversion rule for constraint-field values. Inside a template body a value slot may instead hold a parameter (§5.7), which is resolved when the template closes.

**Injection.** When an optional field with a value — `a?: T ~ v` or `a?: T = v` — is omitted, the decoder injects the value into the output: decoded values are fully populated, and consumers do not consult the schema to retrieve defaults. **A pin on an unmarked name is never injected**: `a: T = v` is a marker a document states itself — a `"jsonrpc": "2.0"`, an `apiVersion`, a format `version` — and exists for a reader that does *not* hold the schema, which is the reader injection cannot serve; omitting it is the missing-field error. A pin whose only information is presence — present and pinned, missing and not injected — is spelled where §5.11 says that information earns its keep: a restated member of a field group, which the group governs and never injects. A written `_` at a field that is not voidable is a validation error whatever the modifier; at `a?: T ~ v` the fix is to omit the field, and omission remains the injection route (§7.6). At `a?: T? ~ v` — the one declaration where omission, `_` and a value are three different outcomes — a written `_` is void and a missing key is `v`, so `timeout: _` means *no timeout* and an omitted `timeout` means *the default*.

**Encoders.** Encoders SHOULD write values for defaulted fields — a document that states its defaults reads without its schema; omitting a field whose value equals its default is a wire-size optimisation an encoder MAY offer, lossless only because the decoder injects the value back on read. Resolver output is exempt from this SHOULD: it omits fields at their default values (§8.1). Two declarations bind an encoder that has the schema, because a field with no value has one spelling to choose and the right one is the declaration's: at `a: T?` a void value MUST be written as `_`, omission being the missing-field error; at `a?: T? ~ v` a void value MUST be written as `_`, omission decoding to the default. An encoder without the schema leaves out a field that has no value, which is right wherever the two spellings mean the same and is why those two declarations need the schema to be written at all ([TSON-DATA] §2.9).

In data, a FIXED field may be provided with a value matching the fixed value, or — where its name is marked — omitted. **A written value at a FIXED field MUST be verified against the fixed value**, as values (§5.5): a contradicting value is a validation error, never a value the decoder silently overwrites — an implementation that seeds fixed fields and skips the check produces decoded output that differs from the bytes with no diagnostic.

**Delivery is the mode's, and only admission is the field's.** The three slots decide what a document may say and what the schema supplies. What a reader then hands over is the mode's: a bound value has one null, so `a: T?`, `a?: T` and `a?: T?` deliver alike and differ only in what they refuse; a tree keeps which spelling arrived — a field written `_` is present with a void value, a field never written is missing ([TSON-DATA] §2.9) — in every encoding. Patch semantics (set, clear, leave unchanged) need the three outcomes a tree has and a bound value does not, and are a capability of the mode, not of any declaration.

**Positional form.** The single-required-field positional form of §5.6 counts fields whose **name is unmarked**, whatever their modifier: a voidable field must still be written, and so must a marker. A record has a positional form exactly when one field's name is unmarked.

**Field names: `name_type`.** A record's field names are values of its **name type**, the kernel's `record.name_type?: identifier_kind` — an identifier family (§7.4), and the same mechanism an enum's members are held to by `enum_type.name_type`. The three rules are one on both constructors: the type is an identifier family, every declared name is a value of it, and no two are one value under its equality. The default is the series' own `field_name`, the kernel's identifier, so nothing changes unless a record or a supertype says so; a schema translated from JSON-LD, OpenAPI or JSON Schema — whose objects are records with members such as `@context`, `@id` or `$ref` — names a relaxed type the translation declares for itself, and the series ships none:

```
json_name   => !identifier_type { start_add: "@$" }
json_record => !record { name_type: json_name  extension: ABSTRACT  fields: {} }
node        => json_record & { "@id": text  label: text }
```

- **The brace form states a type by composition, and has no syntax of its own.** A relaxed name type is a translation's tool, not the go-to form for a TSON record, so there is no brace-form spelling for it: a translation declares its type once, on a fieldless abstract base, and its records compose it. The cost is an IS-A edge to the base, which asks nothing of a position typed by a member — `node` reads untagged. A declared field name is any single-line token (§12.1), quoted where it is no identifier, and is judged at link by the record's name type: a brace-form `{ "first name": text }` under the default is a resolver error, not a parse error.
- **Inheritance keeps one name type, as the constructor's rule.** A record's `name_type` is set where it is declared and a subtype cannot change it: a record with supertypes takes their one type when it states none; one that states a type beside supertypes MUST state theirs; and supertypes with different name types — `json_record & address`, where `address` says nothing and so is `field_name`'s — do not compose and are a resolver error (§5.8). The rule is the constructor's and not `&`'s: the kernel's `name_type?: identifier_kind` carries no `~ field_name` default, since a default cannot depend on supertypes, so the explicit `!record { … }` and a template's closed application are held to it exactly as `json_record & { … }` is. Taking the non-default type instead would re-judge `address`'s names by a type they were never declared under; the record to compose is one declared over the same type, `address => json_record & { city: text }`. A refinement keeps its source's, so the brace form can restate an inherited relaxed name (`tightened => node ^ { "$ref": text }`). Two name types are one by the entry they resolve to, not by spelling: `jn => json_name` is `json_name`, and the kernel's `field_name` is its `identifier`, so a record stating `identifier` composes with one stating nothing. A name type a record states resolves in the author's own namespace first, as an enum's does (§7.4), and resolved output writes `name_type` exactly where it was stated.
- **A name type's form implies NFC.** A reader matches a document's name against the declared ones in the type's `normalization` (§5.5), as an enum matches its members: under a case-folding name type `NAME` is the field `name`, the two spellings in one record are a duplicate field, and a family's selector is found however its name is cased. Under the default, NFC, nothing changes. So `NONE` is refused at load — two canonically equivalent spellings would be two names that render alike — and `ASCII_CASEFOLD`, which leaves text beyond ASCII as written, is admitted only for a type whose every character is ASCII; `NFKC_CASEFOLD` is the fold for any other (§7.4).
- **Hygiene follows the type, over a floor no profile lifts.** Every field name and enum member meets [TSON-DATA] §8.2's rules under its type's own profile, whose added characters are its own (`json_name`'s `@` and `$`, a `medial: " "` for words), and the collision relation over the scope (§11.4). An `identifier_type` MUST NOT add a Default_Ignorable_Code_Point, a control, or a space other than U+0020 (§7.4): without the floor a soft hyphen in `continue_add` makes two names no rule tells apart, and a profile derived from a hostile source's names would carry it in.
- **A schema's own references to its fields match exactly.** A group's members, a refinement's or removal's restated field and a `discriminators` entry name a declared field by its declared spelling, under a folding name type too: the schema is one author's text, so a reference spelled differently is a mistake to report, and only a document's names, which another party writes, are matched in the type's form. `record.discriminators` is therefore `[text; 1..]` and `field_group`'s member slots are `text`, each judged by the record's name type (§5.11).
- **A document's field names are judged by the record that reads them.** A name the record does not declare is an unrecognised field (§7.2), at its own pointer, after the name type has judged it: one that is a value of the name type is a name, and meets the identifier policy ([TSON-DATA] §8.2) under the type's profile before it is reported as unrecognised, so a look-alike of a declared name is refused and never reported as an unknown field; one that is not can be no declared field, since every declared name is one, and is unrecognised without reaching the policy. Both encodings report an unrecognised field with the same code. Under the default the message is the plain one any undeclared name draws; under a declared name type it names the type and its reason, since those are the rules the author has to look up. A field that is no name meets no hygiene rule, and an unrecognised field's value is skipped unjudged — no type governs it, so the one problem is the field itself, reported once. A record nested in a relaxed one judges its own names, so the relaxation does not leak into a value it holds. A lookahead does not judge a name: a sealed family's discriminator scan crosses a member's names before the member is chosen, and only the member knows its type, so a name the scan crosses is judged when the member reads it. A document that names a schema has its field names judged by that schema and only by it; a processor reading it without the schema holds them to no name rule ([TSON-DATA] §1.5, §2.5), and one that cannot obtain the schema leaves the document undetermined rather than judging it by the schemaless rule.
- **A name binds and writes as the record states it.** A host component binds a relaxed name through the name it states, as it binds any other, and a writer quotes a name that is no identifier, so the document reads back; a template's field names are judged at each application, under the closed record's name type.

**Definition marks: how a record may be realised.** A record states, by one of two words written between `=>` and its type definition — before the parameter list where there is one (§5.10) — which values it has and whether others may build on it. The fact is the kernel's `record.extension`, over `record_extension_type => !enum [ABSTRACT FINAL OPEN]`:

```
pet     => abstract { name: text  age: integer }
dog     => pet & { breed: text }
leaf    => final { id: uuid  payload: bytes }
```

- **OPEN** — the record has direct instances, and any schema in the closure may compose onto or refine it. Every record with no mark, and the default.
- **ABSTRACT** — no direct instances: no value's effective type is this record, and a position typed by it admits exactly its subtypes (§7.2). The tag is REQUIRED at such a position in every encoding unless the record names discriminators (below), and a tag naming the base itself is refused, since no value satisfies it.
- **FINAL** — direct instances, and no subtype: composition or refinement naming it as a source is a resolver error, in the declaring schema and in any schema that imports it. Subtraction stays admissible against a FINAL record: §5.9 empties the chain and mints no IS-A edge, and the IS-A set is the only thing FINAL constrains. A record template composing or refining a FINAL record is refused at its own declaration, its written parents being closed entries (§5.10).

Two marks on one declaration are ungrammatical — the slot holds one word — and a mark on a non-record definition is a resolver error. The member is **never inherited**: `dog` above is OPEN whether `pet` is ABSTRACT or OPEN, and must be, or no concrete subtype of an abstract base could exist. There is no transition table for it; the whole of the rule is that nothing may compose or refine onto a FINAL record, and every other declaration states its own member. Inhabitance gains no case: an ABSTRACT record with no subtype in its own closure is the ordinary shape of a library schema whose importers supply the members (§3.3.4 makes `subtypes` open across schemas), and refusing it at load would make the library unpublishable for having deferred exactly what it meant to defer. A document reaching such a position gets a validation error naming the remedy — no schema in this closure declares a subtype of the base, so the schema that does is missing from the imports. `extension` participates in a declared entry's meaning as any body field does, is omitted from output at its default (§8.1), and the two words are ordinary identifiers everywhere but this one slot ([TSON-DATA] §7.7): `abstract => { … }` declares a type of that name, and `f: abstract` references it; what cannot be written is a declaration whose whole body is the bare word, which is a declaration missing its definition. The mark is grammar rather than an annotation because it changes which values conform, which §6 says no annotation may do.

**The selector `=?`: a discriminated family.** A field written `=?` reads *pinned, but not here — the members pin it*: the record is the base of a family whose members each fix the field to a distinct value, and a value at a position typed by the base is placed by reading the field rather than by a tag.

```
pet => abstract { pet_type: text =?  name: text }
dog => pet & { pet_type: = "dog"  breed: text }
cat => pet & { pet_type: = "cat"  indoor: boolean }
```

The fact is the kernel's `record.discriminators`, the base's own statement of which fields select its members, in declaration order; **whether the list is present is what says how a subtype is selected** — by reading those fields where it is, by the tag where it is not — and that reading is derived from the body where it is needed, in the manner of `choice.disjoint` (§5.4), never a member of the extension enum. `=?` **implies ABSTRACT**: a record its members are selected from has no values of its own. Writing `abstract` beside a selector asserts what the body already says and is admitted, on `@disjoint`'s terms; `final` beside one is refused, the members it selects being subtypes that could then never exist.

The base cannot pin the field itself: §5.7's identity rule forbids a FIXED restatement from changing the value, so were `pet` to write `pet_type: text = "pet"` no subtype could pin its own. The base therefore declares the field free and unpinned, each subtype tightens it to FIXED — the ordinary FREE → FIXED move — and there is no other arrangement. What the arrangement buys is that the selectors' declared types are the base's, uniform across the family by construction, so a decoder parses them with the one set of types it knows before dispatch, and the check list is short. **At the base:** the marked field's declared type resolves, after its reference chain, to an atom-family instance or an enum; its name is unmarked and its type non-voidable — `a?: T =?` is a selector that may be missing, selecting nothing, and `a: T? =?` one that may be `_`, likewise; it is not a member of a field group (a member reachable by refinement included, §5.11); it carries no value, `=?` taking none. **Over the family:** every entry in the base's `subtypes`, transitively, restates each marked field FIXED — `a: = v`, the marker that must be written, or `a?: = v`, the injected one — and the pins are **pairwise distinct as values** under the field type's own equality (§5.5): `= 255` and `= 0xFF` collide, `= 1` and `= 1.0` collide, and text pins compare in the field type's normalization form, so two that fold to one value collide. A base may mark several fields; the pins are then distinct **as tuples**, taken in the base's declaration order, and a combination no member pins is the ordinary unmatched-value error, per document. The family is judged over the closure that holds it (§3.3.4): an importing schema that adds a member which fails to pin, or whose pin collides with an imported one, is refused — in the importing schema, which is the schema that broke it — and so is one that declares no member and merely imports two schemas whose members collide. A family discriminates **one level**: a subtype of a member inherits the member's pin and §5.7 forbids changing it, so it dispatches to its parent and relies on §7.2 and the encoding's tag below that; an intermediate type meaning to dispatch its own members declares a selector of its own. A base whose members are its own applications — a record template — is §5.10's case, with the same checks, except that a selector's name mark is each member's: a selector a parameter pins may carry either mark, the base reading it required (§5.10).

**A family is open across schemas.** §3.3.4 makes `subtypes` open, so an importing schema may declare a further member under the pinning obligation above, and the family is judged over the closure that holds it: two members an import merge brings together are the importing schema's error (§3.3.4). A host language's closed-set construct generated from a family — a sealed interface over records, a discriminated union — is relative to one closure and not a promise the schema makes. The word "discriminator" names the marked field in every diagnostic and index, since a symbol is not searchable; what it names is written `=?`.

**A family member is declared; a minted record is a member of nothing.** An application nothing declares is its expansion (§8.2), and a minted record whose chain would name a declared type is a resolver error at the declaration that wrote the application: over `dog_of => <T> pet & { pet_type?: = "dog"  breed: T }`, a field written `k: dog_of<text>` would mint a member of `pet` that no declaration names, and so would a parent application a declaration owns. The diagnostic is located at the declaration that wrote the application — or, where the application sits in a template's held body, at the declaration whose closing minted the member, `kt` for `kt => kennel_of<text>` over `kennel_of => <T> { d: dog_of<T> }` — and SHOULD name the fix: `dogs => dog_of<text>`, then `k: dogs`. This is what §3.3.4 and §8.2 already imply together: a member changes what every position typed by its base does, in every schema that imports it, and is read, tagged and bound by name, while a minted name may never be written (§8.2), so a minted member could be selected and then neither reported, tagged nor mapped. The rule is drawn at membership rather than at member dispatch — a minted member of a tag-dispatched family is as unreachable as one a selector picks, an untagged value at its base needing a tag it cannot carry — and it leaves every other use-site application alone: arrays, sets, choices, applications of a family's base, which compose onto nothing, and an application of a record template written at a use site, which is a type read where it is written and no member of the template's family (§5.10). A minted record gains no IS-A edge later, and a declaration's operand still reaches it: `vip => customer & box<int32>`, beside a use site's `box<int32>`, is IS-A that synthetic and stands where `crate<int32>` is expected, the two applications being one type (§8.2).

**Resolution.** A record definition resolves to `!record { fields: { … } }` (PRODUCT-kinded by derivation, §8.1), its `fields` a `field_map` — a namespace from each field's name to its `record_field`, in declaration order, which is part of the value (§4.1). A field's name is written once, by its spelling, as the key, and the name carries no slot of its own in the `record_field`: `{ type  optional?  voidable?  role?  value? }` is the field's type and the four facts a reader consults (§8.1): `type` is a `type_kind` value, the bare name as written (a reference is a hop, §8.3); `optional` is the name's `?`, `voidable` the type's, `role` one of `FREE`, `DEFAULT`, `FIXED` for the modifier, and `value` the eagerly-resolved default or fixed value, present exactly when `role` is not `FREE`. The defaults — `false`, `false`, `FREE` — are omitted in output. A duplicate is refused by the layer that can see it: a spelling written twice is the map's duplicate key ([TSON-DATA] §2.6), refused where the map is read and before any name type is consulted; two spellings that are one value of the record's name type are the linker's resolver error. A definition mark lowers into the body's `extension` (omitted at `OPEN`); the selector fields lower into `discriminators`, the marked fields themselves staying FREE, unmarked and unpinned; a stated name type lowers into `name_type`, written exactly where it was stated. An empty record `{}` is the zero-field case, `!record { fields: {} }` — the shape of the kernel's `top`, abstract.

**Type-name resolution.** Type names used as type-refs (field positions, type arguments, choice variants, composition targets, refinement sources) and generic-application heads resolve against the type-name namespace; constructor-application targets and the desugar targets of the sugar forms resolve through the structure namespace per §3.3.1. Bare names always refer to types — there is no field-name shadowing of type names.

**Inline atom refinements and bare records are prohibited.** Atom refinements (`!number ^ { min: -273.15 max: 10000 }`) and bare records (`{ name: text }`) MUST be introduced via named declarations and referenced by name; they MAY NOT appear inline in field-type, field-group-member, tuple-element, array-element, choice-variant, type-argument, or composition positions. The container sugar forms carry no positional restriction (§5.3): a size specifier or an element/position `?` is legal wherever the bracket or map form is. Implementations MAY enforce a configurable nesting-depth limit on inline forms as a resource bound in the manner of [TSON-DATA] §9.1; where such a limit is enabled, exceeding it is an error.


### 5.3 Type Expressions

The type-definition grammar has **one form per container, legal at every type-ref position** — field types, group members, tuple elements, array elements, choice variants, type arguments, and declaration bodies alike. There is one bracket form and one map form, each admitting a size specifier after `;` and an element/position `?` wherever the form appears; nesting is the recursion already present in the element position (§12.1). Whether a form sits as a declaration's own body or inline at a use site changes what the resolver *does* with it — a declaration's body is the construction in place, everything else lifts to a synthetic entry (below) — never what may be written.

```
config => {
  tags:     [text]                 ; plain array
  meta:     {text => integer}      ; map sugar
  point:    [number, number]       ; tuple, no position voidable
  contact:  (email | phone)        ; choice
  index:    {text => [order]}      ; nested forms
  aliases?: [text]                 ; the ? on the name is FIELD omission (§5.2)
  sparse2:  [text]?                ; the ? on the type admits _ at the field (§5.2)
  scores:   [integer; 1..]         ; size specifier, legal at a field
  sparse:   [text, text?]          ; voidable tuple position, legal at a field
  batch:    order_batch            ; a named declaration remains good style
}

order_batch => [order; 1..100]     ; bounded range as a declaration body
matrix9     => [number; 9]         ; exact size
opt_items   => [text?]             ; voidable elements
```

**Array types** use `[type]` with an optional size specifier after `;`. The size specifier is a grammar production over the range token ([TSON-DATA] §7.2.4): a bound, optionally followed by `..` and an optional upper bound, or `..` followed by a bound (§12.1). Each bound is an unquoted token whose text MUST match the `decimal-natural` production of [TSON-DATA] §7.6 — a non-negative decimal integer without leading zeros — or, within a template body (§5.10), a value-parameter name. Classification is unambiguous — parameters cannot be numeric — and a non-numeric bound token that resolves to no value parameter is a resolver error. Five forms result: `N` (exactly N elements), `N..M` (bounded range), `N..` (at least N), `..M` (at most M), and absent (unconstrained). `[T; N..N]` and `[T; N]` are two spellings of the same binding and land on the same entry (§8.2); the `N` form is RECOMMENDED. A lower bound of `0` with no upper bound (`0..`) is a resolver error — every array satisfies it, and because identity is structural (§8.2) the spelling would mint an entry distinct from `[T]` that means the same thing; the diagnostic SHOULD say that the unconstrained array is written `[T]`.

**Bound coherence.** A size specifier desugars to the `min_items`/`max_items` binding pair (the table below), and one rule governs the pair wherever it arises, arrays and maps identically: when both bounds are present, `min_items` MUST be less than or equal to `max_items`, checked at the point the bounds are concrete — a resolver error at schema load where the bounds are literal, at materialisation where parameter-bound (§8.2).

The element position accepts an optional `?` suffix, producing `voidable: true` on the resolved `array`. Under `[T?]`, an element at any position MAY be the void sentinel `_` (§7.6); void elements occupy positional slots — `[a _ c]` has three elements and satisfies a `[T?; 3]` size constraint. Without the suffix `voidable` is `false`, and a void element is a validation error when a schema is in scope. An element is never *missing*: a container's part is a value or it is void, so the container constructors carry `voidable` and no `optional` (§5.2, §8.1).

**Order is a facet.** `array` carries `ordered`, `true` unless a body says otherwise, and `map` carries it too, `false` unless a body says otherwise (below). The facet says whether two values differing only in order are one value. It never changes what a document may write, and output keeps the order written either way (§7.5).

**Sets.** The kernel's `set_type` constructor refines `array` and pins three facets: `ordered: = false`, `unique_items: = true`, and `voidable: = false` — a void member has no meaning in an unordered collection of unique members. Its bounds are `array`'s: the empty set is a set, and a set that must be non-empty says `min_items: 1`. Sets have no bracket sugar of their own; the spelling an author writes is the template `set<T>`, which meta and core each declare as `set => <T> !set_type { element_type: T }` (§9) — a §5.10 application, admitted at a field position where a `!` construction is not (§5.2). The template carries no bound, so a *bounded* set at a field is a named entry — `tag_set => !set_type { element_type: text  min_items: 1 }`, then `tags: tag_set` — which is how the kernel and meta state every set they rely on being non-empty: `enum_set`, `integer_member_set`, `text_member_set`, `scheme_set`, and meta's `scope_set` and `decimal_member_set`.

**Map types** use `{key => value}`, mirroring the data notation, with an optional size specifier after `;` (`{text => order; 1..}`) desugaring to `min_items`/`max_items` under the same grammar, coherence, and diagnostic rules as arrays. The sugar's key position accepts a `type-name` optionally carrying type arguments — not a paren or bracket form: composite map key types deserve a named declaration, and the explicit `!map { key_type: … }` form remains available for them. The sugar takes exactly one `key => value` entry — a map *type* has one key type and one value type; the sugar mirrors the data's shape, not its arity, and the diagnostic for a second entry SHOULD say so. The value side admits `?`, marking the value voidable exactly as `[T?]` marks an array element: `{text => order?}` produces `voidable: true` on the resolved `map`, under which an entry's value MAY be the void sentinel `_` (§7.6) — the entry is present with a void value ([TSON-DATA] §2.9) and counts toward the size bounds like any other. Without the suffix a void entry value is a validation error. A map is unordered unless it says otherwise, as a JSON object is: two maps holding the same entries in different orders are one value. `!map { key_type: K  value_type: V  ordered: true }` declares one whose entry order is part of its value, so those two are distinct, and a host binds it to a map that keeps its order; the sugar has no spelling for it. The key side does not admit `?`: a void key is a resolver error ([TSON-DATA] §2.9), and `map-key` has no `?` to write (§12.1). Annotations inside the sugar braces are a parse error; the declaration is the annotation anchor. Brace dispatch between the record and map readings is by one consumed token plus one of lookahead, reusing [TSON-DATA] §2.8's machinery (§12.2).

**Tuple types** use `[type, type, ...]` with comma or whitespace separation between individually typed positions, and a comma may follow the last ([TSON-DATA] §2.4). A tuple requires at least two element type expressions: two or more type-refs separated by whitespace or comma inside brackets is always a tuple; a semicolon after a single type-ref introduces an array size specifier; a single type-ref with no semicolon is an unconstrained array — never a one-element tuple. `[text,]` is therefore an unconstrained array of `text`, the trailing comma following a value and changing nothing. A tuple of exactly one position has no bracket spelling and is written through two templates core declares (§9): `tuple1<T>` — `tuple1<text>` admits `[a]` and refuses `[]`, `[a b]` and `[_]` — and `voidable_tuple1<T>`, whose one position may be void, so that `voidable_tuple1<text>` admits `[a]` and `[_]`. There are two because a type argument cannot carry the `?` that would make the position voidable, and an application carries no facet an author could set instead. The need is rare in a schema written as TSON and real in one converted from another schema language, where a converter needs a spelling it can emit at a field without minting a declaration.

A tuple position answers one question — may its value be void? — recorded as `tuple_element.voidable`. A position, like an array's element, is never missing, so it has no `optional`; a record field answers both questions (§5.2). Tuples are fixed-length: every position MUST be present in the data. A voidable position may carry the void sentinel `_`, but the slot itself MUST appear — a tuple value with fewer elements than the type's position count is a validation error however many trailing positions are voidable. Given `sparse_pair => [text, text?]`: `[a, b]` and `[a, _]` are valid; `[a]` is a validation error. Authors wanting a tail that may be left off should use an array (`[text; 1..]`).

**Choice types** use `(type | type | ...)` — see §5.4.

**Arguments** use `name<arg, arg>`, binding positional arguments to the declared parameters of a template (§5.10); the head resolves through the type-name namespace only (§3.3.1). The argument count MUST match the parameter count. An argument is a type reference — which may nest (`box<[integer]>`, `pair<text, {text => order}>`) — or, for a template with value parameters, a concrete value: `vector<pixel, 1920>`. Number and quoted-string arguments are unambiguously values; an unquoted token argument that satisfies the identifier grammar — `true` and `false` included — is read as its parameter's recorded type says — a type reference where that type is `type_kind`, a literal (an enum member, for instance) where it is anything else (§5.10). Bare references to a parameterized type without `<>` are resolver errors — parameter binding is mandatory at every use site.

**Desugaring.** Every sugar form desugars to a canonical constructor application (§5.6):

| Source form                  | Desugaring                                                                     |
|------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| `[T]`                        | `!array { element_type: T }`                                                   |
| `[T; N]`                     | `!array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: N }`                       |
| `[T; N..]`                   | `!array { element_type: T  min_items: N }`                                     |
| `[T; ..M]`                   | `!array { element_type: T  max_items: M }`                                     |
| `[T; N..M]`                  | `!array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: M }`                       |
| `[T?]`, `[T?; ...]`          | the corresponding form with `voidable: true` bound directly                    |
| `[T, U, ...]`                | `!tuple { elements: [...] }`                                                   |
| `(A \| B)`                   | `!choice { variants: [A B] }`                                                   |
| `{K => V}`                   | `!map { key_type: K  value_type: V }`                                          |
| `{K => V ; spec}`            | `!map { key_type: K  value_type: V  min_items/max_items: … }`                  |
| `{K => V?}`, `{K => V?; …}`  | the corresponding map form with `voidable: true` bound directly                 |
| `set<T>`                     | the template application `!set_type { element_type: T }` through meta's or core's `set` (§9) |
| `C<args>`                    | a template application (§5.10, §8.2); `C` resolves through the type-name namespace only (§3.3.1) |

Array forms record `element_type`, `voidable` from the element's `?` suffix, and `min_items`/`max_items` from the size specifier; tuple forms record `elements` with a `tuple_element` per position, each carrying its own `voidable`; map forms record `key_type`, `value_type`, `voidable` from the value's `?` suffix, and the bounds.

**Nested forms and synthetic entries.** Every sugar form **lifts** at desugar time to a resolver-materialised entry — a **synthetic entry** — except a declaration's own body, which never lifts: it *is* the declaration (`ids => [order]` is the construction in place, not a reference to one). The lift rule's dividing line is closed versus open: a **concrete** form lifts to a *closed* synthetic entry with an ordinary constructor body; a **parameter-bearing** form — one mentioning a parameter of the enclosing declaration — lifts to an *open* synthetic entry capturing those parameters in declaration order, whose body is the constructor application as written, held until materialisation (§5.10, §8.2). The rule needs no case for whether the enclosing declaration has parameters: a concrete `[order]` inside `<T> { a: T  b: [order] }` lifts closed, like any other concrete form. Materialisation creates no synthetic entries — it closes open ones, innermost-out (§8.2). A use-site *sugar form* mints where a declared *application* does not (§8.2), and the asymmetry is the design: a non-record form — an array, set, map or tuple — is the same definition wherever it is written, nothing subtypes it and no tag dispatches to it, so a content-keyed synthetic is exactly the entry every location writing `[text]` should reach; a record takes part in IS-A, and a family's `subtypes` index and a tag both key on its entry, so a declared name has to *be* the entry for `pet.subtypes` to read `[dogs cats]` and for `!dogs` to select it. Synthetic names follow §8.2's internal-name rules: resolver-chosen, fresh by construction, disjoint from declared names, and unreachable from source — and the entries themselves are an implementation's own: their shape is unconstrained beyond producing the required resolved output, and a processor is free to mint one per schema, share them, or normalise them however it likes (§8.2). The lift targets are grammar-supplied and never kernel-declared, so desugaring stays purely syntactic and independent of the import set. Because every form lifts to an entry, nesting needs no special channel — an inner form's marks, sizes, and bindings live on its own entry, and the outer form references it by name — and the positional restrictions earlier revisions imposed on inline forms are gone: a size specifier or `?` is legal wherever the form is.

A **use-site application never resolves in place**: an inline constructor application, sugared or explicit, resolves to a reference to its (synthetic) entry, and a fully-bound application of a template resolves to a reference to its **expansion** — the closed synthetic entry the substituted form lands on, keyed on its content, which any other spelling of the same form lands on too (§8.2). Nothing records the application at the use site. `type_ref.arguments` therefore appears in resolver output only where an application is still *open* or is the identity of an entry — inside held template bodies and a record template's fields (§5.10), in a record template's `chain.parents` (§4.1), in an open alias's `reference.target` (§8.3), and in the `@application` annotation on an entry that closes an application (§8.2) — and means "an application", nothing else.


### 5.4 Choice Types

A choice type declares that a value may conform to any one of a set of alternative types:

```
contact_method => (email | phone | address)
```

A choice MUST contain at least two variants; each variant is a type reference. The choice name is then used like any other type name in field definitions, and choices MAY also appear inline in type-ref positions: `contact: (email | phone)`.

**A variant MUST NOT resolve to `void`** — judged after following the reference chain (§8.3), so an alias of `void` is caught too; the declaration is a resolver error, and the diagnostic SHOULD say why: optionality is not choice — whether a slot may hold no value is the position's own fact, so the author marks the position (§5.2's `?`), or types the field `void` outright where a slot that only ever holds the void value is genuinely meant. `(T | void)` would create a second, worse spelling of `T?`: void is the one thing the format spells one way ([TSON-DATA] §2.9), and it is a fact of a position, never a variant.

**Disjointness** is a total, two-valued fact of the declarations: **discrimination-class distinctness**. Every type has at most one **discrimination class**, derived after following its reference chain (§8.3):

| Class | Types |
|---|---|
| `boolean` | the boolean family |
| `number` | every numeric family — integer, decimal, and float tiers alike |
| `string` | every text-form family — `text`, the identifier families, `uuid`, `uri` and `uri_reference`, `iri` and `iri_reference`, `regex`, `email`, the temporal families, `bytes`, the network-address families, and their refinements |
| `brace` | records and maps (both `{...}`) |
| `bracket` | arrays and tuples (both `[...]`) |

An enum's class is read off its members under its `name_type` (§7.4), always an identifier family — `enum` itself, and an enum over a profile a schema declares: it is its members' shared class read off each member's own token (`[true false]` is boolean-class; a name of the series' own profile never begins with a digit, so no member of an `enum` is number-class, and mixed members yield none). A closed set of text that is no name is the text family's own `members` facet, `ports => !text ^ { members: ["80" "443"] }`, string-class as every text atom is, so `( ports | integer )` is disjoint where `( ports | text )` is not. A kind and a reference (§4.2) are string-class, their values being names. A map keyed by an identifier family is brace-class like any map keyed by an atom. A type with no single class — `rational` and `complex` (whose typed forms straddle classes), an approximate atom whose `allow_nan` or `allow_infinity` is true (core's `float32` and `float64` as declared: a NaN or an infinity has no number spelling in some encodings and a string one, so the family's forms straddle classes exactly as `rational`'s do), a map whose key type, after following its reference chain, is not a type a single scalar token denotes by its content — an atom-family instance or an enum, `value` and `void` excepted, `value` being read by the type its position hands it to and `void` having no key at all (no single scalar token denotes a compound key, so an encoding without a delimiter pair for such a map spells it in the bracket class, [TSON-JSON] §6.4), `value` (a token read by whatever type the position stands for), a nested choice, a `scoped` instance (§7.8), or an unresolvable or cyclic reference — has none, and a classless variant makes its choice not disjoint. `identifier` is not among them: it is a text family and string-class, as every identifier profile is, so `( identifier | int32 )` is disjoint, and `( identifier | text )`, or a choice over two profiles, is not. The two straddling kinds are what make `disjoint` **class-stable**: a choice's untagged values are the same set in every encoding, at the price that `( float64 | text )` carries a tag in text too unless the float narrows `allow_nan` and `allow_infinity` to false. **The rule:** a choice is `disjoint: true` if and only if every variant has a class and no class appears twice; `false` otherwise. The procedure is closed and normative: a resolver MUST record exactly this — it MUST NOT prove more (value-set separation such as disjoint numeric bounds or disjoint patterns does not make a choice disjoint) or less. The classes are [TSON-DATA] §4's own host-type partition plus the two delimiter forms — three scalar classes and no more, deliberately ([TSON-DATA] §4.5): a class for a text-form family such as `date` would narrow what a `text` variant admits untagged in every schema that never mentions a date — and every text-class encoding shares them, so the fact is portable across encodings by construction. Every declared choice records `disjoint`; there is no absent state for a choice.

**A narrower constructor leaves values unreachable.** Discrimination recovers the variant from the value's class and nothing else, so a value dispatched to a variant that then refuses it is refused outright — there is no second attempt at another variant. A constructor narrower than its base-type class therefore leaves values of that class unreachable in any untagged choice it appears in: core's `number` is decimal-only where [TSON-DATA] §4's `number` production admits `0xFF`, `0b1010`, `0o377`, `.inf` and `.nan`, so `( number | text )` classifies `0xFF` as number-class, dispatches it to `number`, and fails, never reaching `text`; `( int32 | text )` refuses `99999999999999` the same way, and a pattern-refined `text` beside another string-class variant refuses everything the pattern excludes. This is a consequence to know rather than a rule to add, and it is one more reason the labelled form (below) is the shape for alternatives an author expects to overlap.

**Tagging.** At the data level, a value matching a choice type carries a type annotation (`!variant`) selecting the variant. The tag is REQUIRED when the choice is not disjoint, and MAY be omitted when it is: `disjoint` means precisely that the encoding's own form resolution — for TSON text, the single base-type-resolution pass of [TSON-DATA] §4, plus the brace/bracket delimiter — recovers the variant, so an omitted tag is recovered from the value's class, never by a second, type-directed inspection of the value's form; the once-only reading of form ([TSON-DATA] §2.4) is preserved. A value omitting the tag at a non-disjoint choice is a validation error. For an emitter the rule is one sentence: *if two variants share a class, tag every value of the choice; a tag is never wrong.*

**The `@disjoint` assertion.** An author MAY annotate a choice definition with `@disjoint` (defined in `meta.tn` as a `void`-targeted marker, written bare per §6 — presence is the assertion) to assert that its variants are **distinguishable by the encoding's form resolution** — the property the derivation checks, and a weaker and more useful one than mutual exclusion of inhabitance. Overlap in inhabitance is ordinary and expected: [TSON-DATA] §2.4's *form is not meaning* makes `text` admit every token, so `42` inhabits both variants of `( int32 | text )`, and the choice is disjoint all the same because the token's class recovers the variant. The annotation carries no decode force — the resolver computes the choice body's `disjoint` whether or not it is present — and is a **checked annotation** (§6) with exactly two outcomes against the derived fact: **verified** (`disjoint: true`; silent), or a **resolver error** (`disjoint: false`): the author asserted a property the declarations lack, in the manner of `N > M` in a size range (§5.3). The derivation is total, so there is no unprovable state and no warning tier. A choice whose variants are separated only by value sets — bounded numerics, disjoint patterns, disjoint enum member sets — derives `false` and rejects the assertion; nothing operational is lost, since no encoding could drop those tags, and the labelled form (below) is the recommended model for such alternatives. A resolver never diagnoses a choice merely for being non-disjoint — overlap is often intended, and the tag serves it.

**Resolution.** `(A | B | ...)` desugars to `!choice { variants: [A B ...]  disjoint: … }` — a SUM-kinded entry when declared, and a *synthetic entry* when inline (§5.3), referenced by name from the use site, so the entry carries the `disjoint` fact wherever the choice appears. The fact sits **in the choice body**, beside the variant list it is derived over (§8.1): `choice` is the only body that has one, so "recorded on every choice and on nothing else" is structural rather than a rule a document could break. Each variant is a type name (a `type_kind` value, §4.2, §8.1) and may itself be a sugar form, which lifts to its own entry (§5.3). The resolver validates that each variant resolves to a distinct type.

A choice discriminates by variant *type name*; for labelled disjunction — mutually exclusive alternatives distinguished by field label, including alternatives of the same underlying type — see field groups (§5.11). A choice has no discriminator field: an author reaching for member dispatch — the value placed by a field it carries rather than by a tag — wants the composed family of §5.2, an abstract base whose members pin a field written `=?`, and a position typed by the base rather than by a choice over the members. The labelled form is the recommended resolution wherever the tag would otherwise be mandatory: a choice whose variants share a base-type class, or whose disjointness is unprovable, is often better written as a single-group record (§5.11), which discriminates by label and needs no derived disjointness.


### 5.5 Constructor Application and Atom Refinement

Within the type-definition grammar, the `!` prefix always takes `top` or an entry that IS-A it — a **constructor** (§4.2); the invariant the data format teaches therefore holds in every grammar of the series: `!T x` describes a value shaped by `T` — in schema source, in data documents, and in resolver output alike. Two forms follow the prefix, distinguished by the `^` operator; the name after `!` resolves per §3.3.1.

**Constructor application — `!C value`.** Produces a constructor instance filled with specific values. The core value after `!C` (§12.1) is a record of bindings interpreted against the constructor's record shape — the field list `C` declared as its narrowable vocabulary — or the positional form of §5.6. This form does NOT establish IS-A: construction transfers only the constructor's kind; the result carries `C` as its tag and no supertypes. Applying an entry that is neither `top` nor IS-A it after a bare `!` is a resolver error (§3.3.1), and so is applying one that is `abstract` — `top`, `type`, the base kinds and `namespace` (§4.1). The kernel's `reference` is applicable like any other: `!reference { target: X }` denotes the alias `X` — REFERENCE-kinded, carrying no chain — and not a value of the head's own kind, open (`<B> !reference { … }`, §5.10) and closed alike. An application of a **namespace type** (§4.1) builds a map value and not a type: `orders => !interface { … }` is read as the map `interface` describes, and the entry is a value no type position may name.

```
integer => !integer_type {}
boolean => !enum [true false]
bytes   => !bytes_type {}
```

**Kind determination.** A constructor's kind is settled at definition time by the **base kind** — `atom`, `product` or `sum`, or `namespace`, excluding `top` and `type` (§4.1) — reachable through its transitive chain, each counted only as the kernel's own entry. Zero base kinds in the chain → PRODUCT by structural default; exactly one → that kind; two or more → resolver error, since the kinds are categorically distinct, `namespace` beside a base kind included. `!C {}` simply inherits `C`'s settled kind — an instance of a namespace type is a map value and is not a type (§4.1).

**Bodies are closed.** A construction or refinement body is a record whose type is known — the constructor's own constraint-field vocabulary — and is validated as an ordinary closed record (§7.2): each member binds against a declared field, and **a member the vocabulary does not declare is a resolver error** at the declaration, naming the member and the constructor's real fields. This is stated here, where bodies are written, because the silent alternative is the dangerous one: an implementation that binds field-by-field and ignores unmatched members reports success while discarding the constraint the author wrote (`!integer ^ { minimum: 1  maximum: 100 }` — JSON Schema's spellings — would compile clean and constrain nothing).

**Atom refinement — `!I ^ { values }`.** Refines an atom-family instance by tightening values on its constructor's constraint fields. `I` MUST resolve to an atom-family instance — an entry whose body *is* an atom application, not the constructor whose body is the vocabulary record describing one (§3.3.1). The test is on the body and on nothing else: an instance and its constructor are both ATOM-kinded, so kind does not separate them, "IS-A `atom`" says the opposite of what a reader expects (true of the constructor, false of every instance, IS-A stopping at construction), and a plain record has no supertypes either — where `integer` carrying `!integer_type {}` against `integer_type` carrying `!record { … }` is one test that neither of those makes alone. The body is the braced subset of the payload a constructor application takes — a data-grammar `record` or `empty-brace` (§12.1) read against the constructor's vocabulary: a bare value, a second type-ref, an annotation, or a map in body position is a parse error, and the positional form of §5.6 does not apply. The body is data, not a record of field declarations: `size: { bits: 8  signed: true }` binds a nested value, and `min: 1` binds a number, exactly as they would after `!integer_type`. This form DOES establish IS-A: the new type carries `I`'s constructor as its tag, `chain.supertypes` holding `I` and everything `I` IS-A, and a body in the constructor's canonical form (§5.6). A refinement head admits no removal clause (§5.9).

```
age              => !integer ^ { min: 0  max: 150 }
non_empty_text   => !text ^ { min_length: 1 }
positive_integer => !integer ^ { min: 1 }
```

`age` resolves to `!integer_type { chain: { supertypes: [integer] }  min: 0  max: 150 }` and can be refined further; a refinement of a refinement carries the complete chain (`small => !int32 ^ { … }` carries `[int32 integer]`). Founding and refining are distinguished at the head, by the operator: `!integer_type {}` applies the constructor (fresh family, no IS-A); `!integer ^ { min: 0 }` refines the instance (IS-A `integer`).

**Construction creates siblings, not subtypes.** One constructor may found any number of nominally distinct families: `dogs => !integer_type {}` is a fresh atom family with the same body as `integer` and no relation to it, and `small_dog_count => !dogs ^ { min: 0  max: 5 }` refines `dogs`, not `integer`. Identity is nominal for a declaration (§8.2): `stock_id => !uuid_type {}` and `other_id => !uuid_type {}` have byte-identical resolved bodies and are two types, and `!other_id` is refused at a `stock_id` position, as is `!uuid`. The only IS-A the `!` forms ever create is the refinement's hop to its instance — recorded in the type's `chain` and deliberately nowhere else in the body: the canonical form (§5.6) erases the surface distinction, so `chain.supertypes` is the sole carrier of the atom family's IS-A fact (§8.1).

**Single-required-field positional form.** When a constructor has exactly one field whose name is unmarked, the core value after `!C` fills that field directly; see §5.6. The positional form applies to constructor application only — a refinement body is always a braced record.

**Facets with a stated meaning.** Each constraint family's facets are defined by the constructor's documentation in `meta-kernel.tn` and `meta.tn` (§9), with the tightening rules of §5.7 applying per facet kind. What every family's facets rest on, and what the artifacts state per family, is one clause:

> **A type denotes a value space. An encoding defines a lexical space over it and one canonical form per value. Equality, ordering, refinement, disjointness and content addressing are defined over value spaces only, never over spellings.**

Every rule in the series that compares two decoded values — the set duplicate rule (§7.5), map-key identity ([TSON-DATA] §2.6), the check of a written value against a FIXED one (§5.2), the identity of a value argument (§8.2), and the digest a pinned reference verifies (§10.2) — delegates to the element type's equality, and this clause is what that delegation reaches. For the numeric families [TSON-DATA] §4.3 already states it (`255` and `0xFF` are one value); for every family whose value space differs from its lexical space the companion artifacts state it in the family's own `@doc`, and the consequences that decide interoperation are these:

- **`bytes`** is an octet sequence. The RFC 4648 alphabet a text encoding spells it in is a `bytes_type` *selector* (`encoding`, defaulting to `BASE64`), which picks a spelling and can never change what two values compare as: case in hex and padding in base64 are lexical, `"abcd"` and `"ABCD"` under `HEX` are one value, and the length facets count octets — `length: 32` is a 32-byte digest whether it arrives as 64 hex characters, 44 base64 characters, or 32 raw bytes. An encoding whose values are octets ignores the selector and writes them raw, and a round trip through it is value-preserving: the alphabet is re-spelled from the schema, not recovered from the bytes. The selector is a facet rather than an annotation because a container element has no annotation position — `[hexdigest]` works only if the element's own type carries the alphabet — and it is **not refinable**: an alphabet narrows nothing, since every octet string is writable in every one of them, so `hexbytes => !bytes ^ { encoding: HEX }` would claim an IS-A carrying no narrowing at positions no base64 reader could honour, and is a resolver error (§5.7). Another alphabet is another **instance**, `hexdigest => !bytes_type { encoding: HEX  length: 4 }`; refining for length inherits the alphabet, `sha256 => !bytes ^ { length: 32 }` being a base64 sha256. `bytes_type` carries no `spec`: RFC 4648 governs spellings, not octets. Core declares one `bytes` and no spelled siblings, and [TSON-DATA] §5.3's `!bytes` is base64, so the name denotes one type in both conformance classes.
- **`number`** and the exact numeric tiers are exact values with no scale: `1`, `1.0` and `1.00` are one value, and whether a spelling's trailing zeros survive a round trip is an encoding's promise, not the type's. `rational` is the fraction — `2/4` and `1/2` are one value.
- **A text value is its token's text put into the type's form.** `text_type` carries `normalization`, over `normalization => !enum [NONE NFC NFKC NFKC_CASEFOLD ASCII_CASEFOLD]`: a value is the token's text, unquoted, unescaped, and then *put into* that form — rewritten, never refused for not already being in it — and every other facet judges the value and never the spelling (lengths, `pattern`, `members`, an identifier's profile), as does every comparison of two values: pins, map keys, set uniqueness, the duplicate and look-alike rules. This is the series' own posture, stated for text: an atom maps a spelling to a value (`0x10` is 16), and a quoted token at an identifier position is normalised before it is matched ([TSON-DATA] §7.2.1). **No comparison goes below NFC.** Two text values are one value when, each in its type's form, they are NFC-equal, so a composed and a decomposed `É` are one value under every member — `NONE` and `NFC` admit the same equality and differ in what the value is, the text as written or its composed form, which is what the other facets judge and a round trip writes. NFC can carry identity where name hygiene cannot ([TSON-DATA] §8.2): Unicode's normalization stability policy keeps two NFC-equal texts NFC-equal under every later version, so the verdict never moves, and an identifier or token policy relaxed to nothing leaves it standing. `NONE` keeps the text as written. `ASCII_CASEFOLD` maps `A`–`Z` to `a`–`z` and changes nothing else — it runs no Unicode normalisation on the value — and is the comparison URI schemes, HTTP field names and DNS names state for themselves. `NFC` and `NFKC` are Unicode's canonical and compatibility forms. `NFKC_CASEFOLD` is `toNFKC_Casefold`, UAX #31's form for names compared without case: a full case fold and not lowercasing (`ß` folds to `ss`, so a length changes), which also folds compatibility characters *into* ASCII and removes the default ignorables, the join controls among them, so [TSON-DATA] §7.7's context rule has nothing left to judge under it. NFD and NFKD are not members: each is its composed twin's equivalence stored decomposed, and would change a spelling and no verdict. The default is `NONE` on `text_type`, where quoted text is otherwise kept as written, and `NFC` on `identifier_type`; `regex_type`, `uri_type`, `iri_type` and `email_type` fix `NONE`, since a pattern put into another form matches something else (`[A-Z]` folds to `[a-z]`), and past the scheme and host a URI compares with case, as an IRI does and as a mailbox's local part does. `normalization` is **fixed where the type is constructed** (§5.7). Two consequences reach an encoding. **A round trip writes the value**: `Content-Type` under a folding type is written back as `content-type`, as `0x10` is written `16`. And a refusal SHOULD quote the token as written and then the value it was judged as — `'PUT' (read as 'put' under ASCII_CASEFOLD)` — the written spelling being what a reader, or a repair of a generated document, has to find. A schema's own members and pins are values too, decoded as data is.
- **A URI is RFC 3986's URI, and an IRI RFC 3987's.** `uri_type` states RFC 3986's distinctions as facets. `allow_relative` admits a relative reference (RFC 3986 §4.2) beside a URI (§3), so its default is the URI-reference (§4.1) and withdrawing it requires a scheme. `allow_fragment` admits a fragment (§3.5); withdrawn beside `allow_relative` it leaves the absolute-URI (§4.3). `schemes` lists the admitted schemes as a set of the kernel's `scheme_name`, an `ASCII_CASEFOLD` identifier, so a scheme is held folded as RFC 3986 §3.1 compares it, `[HTTP http]` is a duplicate, and a value with no scheme is outside every set. `uri_type`, in meta, has core's `uri_reference => !uri_type {}` and `uri => !uri_reference ^ { allow_relative: false }` as its pair, so `uri` IS-A `uri_reference`, every URI being a URI-reference. A URI is US-ASCII (RFC 3986 §2). `iri_type` has the same three facets with the same meanings over RFC 3987's grammar — `ucschar` beyond US-ASCII, and `iprivate` within the query — and core's `iri_reference` and `iri` are its pair; an IRI is judged through the URI it maps to (RFC 3987 §3.1). **`iri_type` is the kernel's**, because the kernel and meta type their own references by it: the kernel's `iri => !iri_type { allow_relative: false }` types `atom_specification.spec`, and meta's `schema_identity => !iri_type { allow_fragment: false }` the keys of `scoped.schemas` (§7.8). A schema identity is an IRI-reference ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1, §3.3) — a host or path may reach beyond US-ASCII, and a path-only reference names a library entry — and never carries a fragment; its remaining form rules — a host that is a `host` value written in its canonical text ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1), no userinfo or port, no dot-segments, an absolute path where there is no host — are identity's own and are refused where an identity is formed. Meta cannot construct an instance of a constructor it declares, its constructor heads resolving in the kernel (§3.3.1), so the family both use lives there and `uri_type`, which neither uses, lives in meta. `uri` is not IS-A `iri`: families do not relate, and §5.4 already treats every text-form family as overlapping. A relative reference where `allow_relative` is withdrawn, a scheme outside `schemes`, and a fragment where `allow_fragment` is withdrawn are validation errors — the token is inside the family's lexical space and outside the type's value space — and a character outside the family's grammar is refused by the family's parser (§7.4). The value of each is its text as written.
- **`hostname` is a domain name, and the value is the name** ([TSON-DATA] §5.5): RFC 5890's labels, LDH labels, A-labels or U-labels in any mix. ASCII case is lexical, and a U-label and its A-label are two spellings of one label, each recovered from the other by Punycode (RFC 3492), so `Example.COM` and `example.com` are one value and so are `xn--bcher-kva.example` and `bücher.example`. The canonical text is the lowercase U-label form — what a writer emits, and the form an identity's host takes ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1). `hostname_type` carries one facet beside its `spec`: `allow_idn`, a **permission** (§5.7) granted by default, which withdrawn admits only a name none of whose labels is a U-label or an A-label. It judges the value and not a spelling, so `!hostname ^ { allow_idn: false }` refuses `bücher.example` and `xn--bcher-kva.example` alike, as a validation error — the token is inside the family's lexical space and outside the type's value space — while a token outside RFC 5890's grammar (uppercase beyond ASCII, a trailing dot, a last label beginning with a digit) is refused by the family's parser (§7.4).
- **`host` is a host name or an IP address, never both** ([TSON-DATA] §5.5): RFC 3987's `ihost` profiled to DNS, whose lexical space is the union of `hostname`'s, `ipv4`'s and `ipv6`'s plus an IPv6 address in brackets. Each member keeps its own equality — a name compares as a `hostname` value, an address numerically — and a name never equals an address, nor an IPv4 address an IPv6 one, its IPv4-mapped form included. The brackets are a spelling and not a value, so `[::1]` and `::1` are one host. The canonical text is the member's: lowercase U-labels, a dotted quad, or RFC 5952 text unbracketed. `host_type` has no facets beyond its `spec`: a position that wants only names, or only addresses, is typed by the member, `hostname`, `ipv4` or `ipv6`.
- **`media_type` is a type, a subtype and a map of parameters** ([TSON-DATA] §5.5): an RFC 6838 media type, its parameters RFC 9110 §8.3.1's. The type, subtype and parameter names compare without ASCII case, parameter order carries no meaning, a quoted parameter value equals its token form, and a parameter value keeps its case except `charset`'s, which folds. The canonical text is the lowercase type, subtype and parameter names with the `charset` value folded, parameters sorted by name, each value a token where it is one and quoted otherwise, joined by `;` with no whitespace: `text/html;charset=utf-8`. `media_type_type` carries three facets beside its `spec`. `allow_parameters` is a **permission** (§5.7), withheld by default; withheld, a value carrying a parameter is a validation error. Net's `media_type` admits none, and since a refinement may withdraw a permission and never grant one, a position that takes a `Content-Type` value declares its own instance, `content_type => !media_type_type { allow_parameters: true }`. `types` lists the admitted top-level types, and `suffixes` RFC 6839's structured-syntax suffixes, a suffix `s` admitting a subtype ending `+s` and the subtype `s` itself; each list, where it is given, must admit the value, and a value outside one is a validation error. Both are **member sets** (§5.7) whose entries are media-type names and compare as the type and subtype do, without ASCII case — so `suffixes: [JSON]` admits `application/ld+json` and `application/json`, and `types: [Text]` refines `types: [text image]`: the family's own value identity that §5.7's subset rule compares by is the media type's, not that of the lists' element type `text`. Each entry MUST be an RFC 6838 `restricted-name`, a suffix holding no `+`, and a list MUST NOT be empty, an empty one admitting nothing; a body breaking either is a schema-load coherence error.
- **`time` and `datetime` are instants.** RFC 3339 makes the offset mandatory, and the offset is a *spelling*: a `datetime` is the instant on the UTC timeline and a `time` is the time of day in UTC on `[00:00:00, 24:00:00)`, so `2026-01-01T10:00:00+01:00` and `2026-01-01T09:00:00Z` are one value, `-00:00` (offset unknown, RFC 3339 §4.3) is the same instant as `Z`, and `23:30:00-02:00` is `01:30:00Z`. A leap second — second 60, which RFC 3339's grammar admits — is neither a time of day on that interval nor an instant on that timeline, and both families' parsers refuse it ([TSON-DATA] §5.4). Both families are totally ordered — the mandatory offset is what makes them so; XSD's partial order is the cost of an optional one. A wall clock with no offset, and an appointment for which the zone is data, are other value spaces and not these types. TSON text preserves the offset as written; an encoding that stores the instant may drop it.
- **`duration` is a signed exact decimal number of seconds, and `period` a signed integer number of months.** The split is what gives each a total order: a month has no fixed length beside a second that has one, so the two are two value spaces rather than one partially ordered one, and every ordered-bound facet on them is enforceable (§5.7). `duration`'s lexical form is RFC 3339 Appendix A's `duration` production with an optional leading `-`, a fraction on the seconds component only, any subset of `D`/`H`/`M`/`S` in order with at least one present, and no `Y` or month-`M`; `PnW` stands alone, a week is 7 days and a day 86400 s, so `PT90M`, `PT1H30M` and `P0DT5400S` are one value and `PT0S`, `P0D` and `-PT0S` are one value. `period`'s is `P` with a `Y` component, an `M` component, or both, and nothing else. Both ends of `duration`'s space are fixed at a signed 64-bit count of nanoseconds: **`time-secfrac` is `"." 1*9DIGIT`** — stated once, for `duration`, `time` and `datetime` alike, since no host runtime represents finer than a nanosecond and a value below that floor is not exactness anyone can use — and **a `duration`'s magnitude does not exceed 2⁶³ − 1 ns** (about 292 years), as a magnitude so that negating an admitted value yields an admitted one; a processor MUST represent every value in that range and MUST reject one outside it whether or not its own representation could hold it. A span of centuries is a calendar span and is a `period`; a quantity finer or wider than the range is a `number` in the unit the schema names. The floor is lexical (a fraction reaches the value through one component and is capped at the token) and the ceiling is a value rule (the seconds count is summed from `D`, `H`, `M` and `S`, so `P400000D` overflows with no single component long enough to catch).
- **`precision`** on `time_type`, `datetime_type` and `duration_type` is a constraint on the *value*, not on a spelling: `precision: N` admits a value that is a whole number of 10⁻ᴺ seconds — `fraction_digits` on the seconds count — so `precision: 3` is millisecond resolution, `precision: 0` whole seconds, and `precision: 9` the nanosecond grid; reading admits any spelling of an admitted value, trailing zeros included, so `12:00:00.500` is admitted under `precision: 1`, and a text encoding writing the value writes at most N fractional digits, `12:00:00.5`. The atom is exact — nothing is truncated; a value off the grid is rejected. `precision` may not exceed 9, which falls out of the fraction production rather than being stated twice; a larger value is a schema-load coherence error. The temporal families carry no timezone facet: RFC 3339 `full-time` and `date-time`, which the atoms' `spec` pins, already make the offset mandatory, so a facet requiring it would be vacuous and one relaxing it would widen the atom against its own pin. All five temporal families state their bounds as §5.11 groups, `( min | exclusive_min )?` and `( max | exclusive_max )?`, so a half-open interval is spellable on a date exactly as on an integer.
- **The network families' `within`/`excluding` pair MUST admit a value**, and the rule is exact. `ipv4_type`, `ipv6_type`, `cidr4_type` and `cidr6_type` each carry `within` (a value must lie inside some listed network; a network must be a subnet of one) and `excluding` (an address must lie inside none; a network must not *overlap* any — overlap, not containment, so a wider value cannot smuggle an excluded block). A body whose two lists between them admit nothing is invalid at the schema level, in the voice the family already uses for a prefix outside its range: `{ within: ["10.0.0.0/8"]  excluding: ["10.0.0.0/8"] }` is `{ min: 10  max: 3 }` in another spelling, and one an author reaches by editing. The check is decidable exactly — CIDR blocks are nested or disjoint and never partly overlap, so an exclusion meeting a permitted block either contains it or lies wholly inside one of its halves, and the walk terminates at the address width — and a resolver MUST implement it exactly rather than pairwise, since `10.0.0.0/9` and `10.128.0.0/9` cover `10.0.0.0/8` by counting and only the tiling case arises from a real edit. **For a network family the prefix bounds participate**: a value must be a subnet of a `within` block, so its prefix is at least that block's, and `max_prefix` caps it from above; `{ within: ["10.0.0.0/24"]  excluding: ["10.0.0.5/32"]  max_prefix: 24 }` admits almost every *address* and no *network* at all, since the one block the prefix bounds leave overlaps the hole. The diagnostic SHOULD say which cause it found — an exclusion covering everything permitted, or the largest block the bounds leave — since the two want different edits.


### 5.6 Canonical Form and Desugaring

All type-definition bodies ultimately take a single canonical form:

```
!C { bindings }
```

where `C` names a constructor and `bindings` is a record literal filling the constructor's fields. Every other form — inline type expressions, positional constructor forms, atom refinements — is syntactic sugar that desugars to this form during resolution; resolver output always records the fully expanded canonical form as the entry's value.

**Positional form.** When a constructor has exactly one field whose name is unmarked — a field the document must write, whatever its modifier (§5.2) — the core value after `!C` may be that field's value directly:

```
!enum [true false]    →  !enum { members: [true false] }
!array text           →  !array { element_type: text }
!choice [a b]         →  !choice { variants: [a b] }
```
