Fields whose name carries `?` do not count, whether or not they carry a value. The positional form is invalid when the type has zero, two, or more unmarked names — the resolver MUST reject such uses with a clear error. This restriction applies to the positional form only.

**The positional form is general over schema-backed data.** At any data position whose declared type is a record with exactly one unmarked-name field, a non-brace-delimited value fills that field directly; a braced value is always the explicit record (so record- and map-valued fills cannot go positional — the same exclusion as above). The two spellings denote the same value; which one canonical output uses is a per-type convention (§8.1 for `type_ref`, whose bare-token spelling is this rule). Type-definition bodies canonicalise the other way — toward the explicit bindings record, per the end state below — a deliberate pair of conventions: bodies uniform, references minimal. **In a schema document the positional form keeps a bare token where the positional field is atom-typed** — a type name at a `type_ref` slot, an enum member, a literal — **and loses it where that field is product- or sum-typed**: there the explicit record is the only spelling, since a bare token at a record- or choice-typed position in a schema document would otherwise be indistinguishable from a name, and a value written by name stands at a slot typed `ref<T>` and nowhere else (§4.2, §5.12); a bare token at a record-typed slot whose positional field is product- or sum-typed, or at one whose record has no positional form, is refused as no record. So `fields: { p => text }` fills `record_field.type`, an atom-typed positional field, in a schema document as in a data document (§8.1). A data document has no namespace to resolve a name in and reads every bare token positionally, whatever the positional field's type, so a data document's positional fills read as they always have. The `name<args>` **application sugar is schema syntax only**: at a `type_ref`-typed slot in *data* — a `parents` list in resolver output, say — `page<order>` is a parse error, `<` never being data syntax, and an application with arguments is written in `type_ref`'s explicit record form, `{ name: page  arguments: [ { name: order } ] }` (§8.1). At a `type_kind`-typed slot of a value body — a field of a namespace entry's map (§4.1) — a braced application is refused outright: nothing lifts or mints in a value body, so the application would have no entry to be the name of. The spelling that gives such an application a name is the alias, `order_page => page<order>` (§8.2).

**Record-bindings form.** `!C { ... }` is the explicit form, valid for any constructor as long as the bindings cover every field whose name is unmarked. Empty bindings `!C {}` are valid whenever the constructor has no unmarked-name field.

**Atom refinement.** `!I ^ { values }` desugars by retargeting to the instance's source constructor, **with `values` merged over `I`'s own already-bound field values**: a field named in `values` overrides `I`'s value for it, and every field `I` itself bound that `values` does not mention keeps `I`'s value — the atom counterpart of §5.7's body-materialisation rule, under which inherited constraints survive a refinement that does not mention them:

```
!integer ^ { min: 0  max: 150 }   →  !integer_type { min: 0  max: 150 }
!text ^ { min_length: 1 }         →  !text_type { min_length: 1 }
```

For a fresh instance (`integer => !integer_type {}`) the merge is a no-op — every inherited field is absent — so the single-hop desugar above is the general rule's degenerate case. Chained refinement is where the merge matters:

```
int8      => !integer ^ { size: { bits: 8  signed: true } }
bigNumber => !int8 ^ { min: -500  max: 5000 }
          →  !integer_type { size: { bits: 8  signed: true }  min: -500  max: 5000 }
```

`bigNumber` retargets to `integer_type` (an instance's tag is always the base constructor) and keeps `int8`'s `size` binding, which nothing in its own body mentions — replacement rather than merge would silently discard the width and yield an unconstrained-width integer with bounds. Recognition is syntactic — the `^` declares the intent — and the resolver verifies it: the target MUST resolve to a non-constructor atom-family instance, and the retarget follows the instance's tag. The result carries `I`'s constructor as its tag and `I`, with everything `I` IS-A, in `chain.supertypes` — `[int8 integer]` for `bigNumber`.

**End state.** After desugaring, every *closed* non-reference type-def body in resolver output is a binding record `!C { bindings }`, where `C` is the constructor the form applies — with the container constructors parameterless (§4.2), the nearest constructor in the source chain for every container closure is the container constructor itself: `!array` for every array form, `!map` for the map sugar, `!set_type` for an application of `set<T>` — and `bindings` supplies values for the vocabulary's unmarked-name fields, plus the fields the application or refinement bound. Pins, defaults, and routes divide by where their values live: a pin or default whose value is *concrete in the head's own declaration* (`set_type`'s `ordered: = false`) comes from the vocabulary and does not appear in the binding record, while a value routed by a user-template parameter (§5.10) is application-supplied information and does appear — so a validator reads any closed body with a one-hop lookup of the head, never substitution. A type slot is an ordinary required `type_kind`-typed field (§4.2), and the bound name appears in the binding record as a bare token (which, after lifting, is what every closed form holds; §5.3). Binding records are closed-world: they never contain parameter references. A definition whose parameters remain open is either a **record template** — a `!record` with `parameters`, read as the vocabulary, a parameter standing in a field's `type` or `value` or in an application's arguments (§5.10) — or a **held** constructor application, the same `!C { … }` text, unread against `C`'s vocabulary until materialisation substitutes the parameters away (§5.10, §8.2); so constructors carry `!record` vocabulary bodies, record templates carry records with parameters, every other open entry carries a held application, and every other non-reference entry carries a binding record. The surface abbreviations exist only in source text — except the one that names a reference: a use site names whatever entry the author named, an alias included, and output states the chain as written (§8.3).

**Top-level constructor applications are constructions.** A declaration whose body applies a constructor — the explicit form (`lookup => !map { key_type: text  value_type: integer }`) or its sugar spelling (`lookup => {text => integer}`) — resolves as a construction: kind from the constructor's family, the constructor as the value's tag (§8.1), the binding record as the value, and no supertypes (construction transfers kind, not IS-A). Two such declarations are nominally distinct entries with structurally equal bodies. A declaration whose body is a fully-bound application of a non-constructor *template* resolves on the same footing: the declaration **is** the entry — the substituted binding record its value, closed in place, the canonical application recorded as `@application` on its key — and no parallel entry is minted beside it (§5.10, §8.2). Two such declarations are two entries, as two hand-written records with one field list are.

**Named definitions required.** The refinement form (`!I ^ { values }`) and constructor application (`!C value`) are valid only as the top-level body of a declaration — the inline prohibition of §5.2. A constrained atom must be introduced with its own declaration and referenced by name.


### 5.7 Refinement

Refinement copies an existing definition and tightens it — binding values, fixing defaults, restricting ranges — producing a new definition with its own identity that IS-A the source. It never changes the field set: no field is added and none removed. It is expressed with the `^` operator between a source type name and a record body:

```
config => { host: text  port?: integer ~ 8080  debug: boolean }
production => config ^ { host?: = "prod.example.com"  port?: = 9090 }
```

The operator carries the operation: `^` always means *refine, preserving IS-A*, at every rung of the ladder — record and map types (`config ^ { ... }`), constructors (`array ^ { ... }` in a meta-schema, §4.2), and atom instances (`!integer ^ { ... }`, §5.5). A source type name followed directly by a braced body, with no operator, is a parse error; the diagnostic SHOULD suggest `^` (refinement) or `&` (composition). A refinement head admits no removal clause: `T ^ { ... } - { ... }` is a parse error — an operator that promises IS-A cannot host the operation that revokes it (§5.9).

In a refinement, only existing fields may be modified: fields in the body MUST exist in the source definition, and adding fields is a resolver error. The guiding rule is that refinement can only restrict, never expand. The source name resolves in the type-name namespace; the same syntax refines local and imported types. A refinement whose source is a FINAL record is a resolver error (§5.2).

**Field facts move along three orders, and never backwards.** A field's three questions (§5.2) each carry an order from least to most determined, and a restatement refines its source exactly when no question moves backwards:

- **omission** — *nothing* → *required* → *injected*: what a missing key yields, read off the declaration as `?` without a value, an unmarked name, and `?` with a value;
- **voidable** — *true* → *false*;
- **role** — `FREE` → `DEFAULT` → `FIXED`, a default overridable until it is fixed.

So `a: T` → `a?: T ~ v` (required → injected, FREE → DEFAULT) is allowed; `a?: T ~ v` → `a: T` is refused, a default not being removable; `a?: T ~ v` → `a?: T = v` is allowed; `a?: T?` → `a?: T` (voidable true → false) and `a?: T` → `a: T` (nothing → required) are allowed, and each reverse is refused. The third of §5.2's invariants shapes one move: `a?: T? ~ v` reaches a pin only as `a?: T = v`, voidable and role moving forward together, since a pin on a voidable type is refused. One consequence is worth stating: a marker written in a base, `a: T = v`, may be relaxed by a subtype to `a?: T = v` — required → injected — exactly as `a: T` may be relaxed to `a?: T ~ v`; a family that needs its marker written in every member restates it unmarked in each. Nothing stores the omission answer, and nothing needs to: the order is checked on the derived answer, which is `optional` and `value` read together (§8.1).

**Restatement.** Each field may be restated as itself. A restatement may change a **default** (defaults are overridable, §5.2) and MUST NOT change a **pin** — the identity case exists so a body may restate a fixed field without error, not so fixed values can be replaced. This is what forces a discriminated family's arrangement (§5.2): the base cannot pin a selector, since no member could then pin its own.

**Value tightening is per facet kind.** "Refinement can only restrict" is checkable per constraint field, and each field's rule follows its facet's kind, declared once here for every family: an **ordered bound** (`min`, `max`, `min_length`, `max_items`, exclusive bounds, and kin) may move only inward — a lower bound may rise, an upper bound may fall, never the reverse — and requires its family's value space to be **totally ordered**, which every family carrying one now is (§5.5; the temporal split is what made `duration`'s so); a **step** (`multiple_of`, wherever it appears) is strictly positive, tests the value's magnitude with the sign ignored, and may tighten only to an integer multiple of the inherited step (`15` under `5` tightens; `10` under `15` is an error); a **permission** facet (a boolean granting latitude: `float_type`'s `allow_nan` and its three siblings, the `allow_relative` and `allow_fragment` of `uri_type` and `iri_type`, `hostname_type`'s `allow_idn`, and `media_type_type`'s `allow_parameters`) may go from granted to withdrawn, never the reverse; a **member set** (an enum's `members`, the `members` of the exact numeric tiers, the `schemes` of a URI or IRI family, the `types` and `suffixes` of a media-type family) may shrink to a subset, never grow or replace, compared by the family's own value identity (§5.5, §7.4); a **settable-once** facet (`text_type.pattern` and `text_type.members`) may be set by a refinement where the source left it unset, restated verbatim, or left alone, and never changed — the two text facets occupy one logical position, both specifying the admitted value set, and `pattern` cannot be narrowed without a regular-language containment oracle the series decides nowhere, so giving the pair two rules by which spelling the author reached for would make the narrowing relation an artifact of notation; that `text_type.members` is settable-once where the numeric tiers' member sets shrink is the one departure from the member-set rule, stated so that a reader does not infer it from a diagnostic; a facet **fixed at construction** is pinned where it is constructed — a refinement of the *constructor* may pin it, which is how `enum` states its `name_type` — and a refinement of an *instance* restates it verbatim or leaves it alone, and never sets or moves it, because moving it would change what a token *means* rather than narrow which values are admitted — `text_type.normalization` (turning a fold on in a refinement re-reads every token the source admitted); the profile facets of `identifier_type`, `start`, `continue`, `start_add`, `continue_add`, `medial` and `exclude`, for which settable-once would be unsound, since setting `start_add` where the source left it unset *widens* the profile (`!identifier ^ { start_add: "_" }` would admit `_x` without `_x` being an `identifier`); and an enum's or a record's `name_type` (§5.2, §7.4) — so that a different form, profile or name type is a fresh construction and never a refinement; a **selector** facet — a discriminant among members of a closed vocabulary (`integer_type.size`, `complex_type.component`, `bytes_type.encoding`, `float_type.format`) — may move under refinement **only along the narrowing relation its members carry**, which each family states: a width chain for `size` (`int8` is inside `int16`, so `!int8 ^ { size: { bits: 16 } }` widens and fails, whatever the arithmetic direction); a partial order for `component` (`INTEGER ⊂ NUMBER ⊂ RATIONAL` and `FLOAT32 ⊂ FLOAT64`, the exact and approximate families incomparable since binary64 carries ±inf and NaN that no exact decimal represents, so `!complex ^ { component: INTEGER }` narrows and `!complex ^ { component: FLOAT64 }` is refused, a float complex being its own instance); and **no relation at all** for `encoding`, which a refinement may neither set nor change — an alphabet is a spelling of one value space, so a change would produce an IS-A carrying no narrowing, and another alphabet is another instance of the constructor (§5.5). A selector at the constructor's default is not thereby free: the relation is over effective values, so an unwritten default and a written one refine identically, and there is no "set-from-default" permission apart from the relation. A **fixed** value follows §5.2's rules for a pin, and a written value is compared against it as a value (§5.5). A refinement value violating its facet's rule is a resolver error at schema load, in both record refinement and atom refinement (§5.5, §5.6). Constructor refinement in a meta-schema is the meta-level exception and MAY replace fixed values (§4.2).

**Three ways to name a type after another, and what each buys.** An author writing `user_id => uuid` and expecting a `stock_id` to be refused at a `user_id` position gets no protection, and nothing else tells them the other two forms exist:

| Spelling | Relation to `uuid` | `!uuid` at its position | a sibling's tag |
|---|---|---|---|
| `user_id => uuid` — **reference** (§8.3) | the same type, under another name | accepted | accepted |
| `user_id => !uuid ^ {}` — **refinement** | IS-A `uuid`; usable at a `uuid` position | refused | refused |
| `user_id => !uuid_type {}` — **fresh instance** (§5.5) | unrelated in either direction | refused | refused |

Only the first is a rename, and it is what identity dereferences (§8.2): `box<user_id>` and `box<uuid>` are one application. The **empty refinement is legal** — `!uuid ^ {}` narrows nothing and is the language's one nominal-subtype spelling, which is why "a refinement tightens" is a description of what a refinement may do and not a MUST that a body change something.

**Open modifiers.** In a template body (§5.10) the value of a `=` or `~` modifier may be a parameter: `bounded => <N> { attempts?: integer = N }` routes, and `retry_policy => <N> { attempts?: integer ~ N }` defaults. **The name's mark is the author's, exactly as beside a literal.** The `?` on a name says whether the key may be missing and says nothing about the value (§5.2), so it does not wait for the value to exist: `a?: T ~ P` is a default, `a?: T = P` an injected pin, and `a: T = P` a marker every document must write — and `a: T ~ P` is refused for the reason `a: T ~ v` is, a default on a key that is always written being a value nothing can reach. An earlier revision had a parametric modifier written on an unmarked name and the mark supplied when the template closed; the mark is now written where it is meant, as it is on every other field. What the declaration does not settle is the *value*: it arrives at application, and every application MUST bind every parameter (§5.10), so it is always supplied. While the entry is open the parameter rides the ordinary `value` slot of the `record_field` (§8.1) — no label distinguishes it from a literal: a token there is a parameter exactly when its text resolves into the enclosing record's `parameters` (the shadowing rule, §5.10), in resolved output as in source. **The role is the modifier's, as beside a literal**: `= P` is `FIXED` and `~ P` `DEFAULT` where they are written, with the parameter in `value`, and materialisation substitutes the argument into `value` without changing a fact — so the materialised entry enforces what the literal form `status?: int32 = 201` one line down would (a document writing `status: 999` is a validation error, and an omitted `status` injects `201`), and the bound value is checked against the field's type by the value-conformance rule (§5.2). A FREE field never carries a value, open or closed. In a *fresh* record template a field pinned to a value parameter is a **selector** whatever its name's mark (§5.10): one value per application is one pin per member, the base reading the field required with the pin dropped and each member FIXED to its argument, optional as the name was marked. In a *record* template body the modifier spellings are the only way to bind a field's value to a parameter — `min_items: S` in a record body is a field declaration whose type is `S`, not a value binding; in a held *constructor* body (`<S> !array { element_type: text  min_items: S }`) the slot is a value slot of `array`'s own vocabulary and the parameter stands in it directly (§5.10).

**Elided type-refs.** In a refinement or supertype-composition body, the type-ref in a field definition MAY be elided: when only a modifier is present (`field: = value` or `field: ~ value`), the field's type is inherited from the source declaration and only the value state changes. A modifier-only entry is always a tightening — it names no type, so it cannot declare a new field — and a modifier-only entry whose name matches no inherited field is a resolver error. Restating the type-ref remains necessary when the tightening also narrows the field's type. In a fresh record definition there is no inherited declaration to elide toward: every field MUST have an explicit type-ref, and the resolver MUST reject modifier-only entries there.

**Refinement requires a vocabulary body** (§4.3 states the rule for both operator families). The source of `^` — after following its reference chain (§8.3) — MUST be a definition whose body is a `!record`: a fresh or refined record, a composition, a constructor (in a meta-schema, §4.2), or an *applied* record template (a record template named bare at an operand is a resolver error, §5.10). A definition whose body is a binding record — a top-level constructor application (§5.6), or an alias resolving to one — is **finished**: its bindings are set, and `^` on it is a resolver error; a choice is likewise inadmissible (§4.3). A record instantiation is not finished: it closes to a `!record` with fields, and whether its source was written as `box<text>` inline or as a name for that application is provenance, which never gates an operator (§4.3). There is no refinement of an application head: `{text => text} ^ { min_items: 1 }` has no head to refine, and the use case is the size specifier on the sugar itself (`{text => text; 1..}`, §5.3). Record refinement chains remain open because record refinement re-emits a `!record` body; a declared construction (`lookup => {text => integer}`) is terminal — authors wanting a narrower relative write the bounds on the form (`strict_lookup => {text => integer; 1..}`). The `refined-def` grammar's optional `<type-args>` head serves user-template heads only (§5.10). Atom refinement (`!I ^`, §5.5) is a distinct form with its own rule and is unaffected.

A refined record definition remains a definition: it can be refined further or instantiated. A refinement that narrows a field's type to `void` — `a?: void?`, or `a: void?` at a restated group member (§5.11) — forbids the field's value in the refined type while keeping the field in the contract, the IS-A-preserving counterpart of removal (§5.9); `void` refines every type at a voidable position, the value set `{_}` being a subset of every voidable position's. Individual map entries cannot be refined because map keys are data, not definition fields.

**Body materialisation.** The refined body re-emits the complete inherited field set in source order; each field carries either its inherited state and value or the tightened ones. The materialised body is self-describing — consumers of resolver output do not walk the supertype chain to learn the field set. Inherited defaults and pins appear with their values even when the refinement did not refer to them.

**Resolution.** The refined entry's `chain.supertypes` records the source and the IS-A chain through it (§8.1), its value carries the source's constructor as its tag, and tightened fields appear in the materialised body. A refinement keeps its source's `name_type` (§5.2).


### 5.8 Supertype Composition

Supertype composition (`&`) is a construction tool: it combines one or more parent types with new fields into a new definition, declaring an IS-A relationship with each listed supertype. New fields are permitted; existing fields may be tightened.

```
address => { street: text  city: text  postcode: text }
contact => { name: text  email: text }
customer => address & contact & { loyalty_tier: text }
```

**Supertype field conflicts.** The supertypes MUST contribute disjoint field sets — a field name appearing in more than one supertype path is a resolver error, including diamond cases where the field traces to the same originating type through both paths. Two names are one field under the composed record's name type (§5.2), and **supertypes with different name types do not compose**: `json_record & address`, where `address` states nothing and so is `field_name`'s, is a resolver error, and the record to compose is one declared over the same type.

**The trailing body.** The trailing `& { ... }` body is optional (`customer => address & contact` is valid). Body fields that match an inherited field are tightening entries and follow the refinement rules of §5.7 (including elided type-refs); body fields that match no inherited field are new fields.

**Field ordering.** Supertypes contribute fields in left-to-right order as listed; each supertype's fields appear in their declared order. Tightening entries replace inherited fields in place; new fields are appended after all inherited fields.

**Constructorness is inherited through composition.** Composition propagates the supertype chain, so an entry composing with something IS-A `top` is IS-A `top` and is therefore a constructor (§4.2): `iri_type => text_type & atom_specification & { ... }` is a constructor because `text_type` is. A record mixin such as `atom_specification` lends vocabulary without conferring the level, and a composition reaching no base kind — `box => <T> base & { value: T }` over a plain record `base` — is an ordinary template, not a constructor, whatever its author intended. Placement (§2.2.2) is what keeps the level inside the meta layer.

```
atom_specification => { spec: iri }
iri_type => text_type & atom_specification & {
  spec?:           = "https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3987"
  schemes?:        scheme_set
  allow_relative?: boolean ~ true
  allow_fragment?: boolean ~ true
  normalization?:  = NONE
}
```

`iri_type` is a constructor, IS-A `text_type` and `atom_specification` directly. Its fields, in order: `text_type`'s six constraint fields, the last of them — `normalization` — tightened in place from a default to the pin `NONE`; `atom_specification`'s `spec`, tightened in place to an injected pin via an elided-type modifier; and the three new fields. Composition onto a FINAL record is a resolver error (§5.2), and a supertype that is ABSTRACT confers nothing on the composed entry, which states its own extension and is OPEN unless it says otherwise. A restated field that is a **group member** stays a member, in its own option: a `?` on its name is the in-option mark, which a restatement may drop and may not add (§5.11). A declared application of a template whose body is not a record (`string_triple => vector<text, 3>`) closes to a binding record and has no vocabulary body to compose with; a record template's declared application is a `!record`, which composes like any other (§4.3, §5.7).

**A restated field's annotations are the restatement's own followed by the inherited ones.** §6 puts an annotation on a `record_field` and §8.1 preserves it; composition copies inherited fields whole and §5.7 lets a body entry restate one, and the two paths must agree. The rule: a restated field — in a composition body or a refinement body, including §5.7's modifier-only entry — carries **the restatement's own annotations, in source order, followed by the inherited field's, in source order**. Nothing is dropped and no name is treated as a key, because [TSON-DATA] §3.1 already makes an annotation name repeatable on one value and annotations are a list; and the restatement leads because order is already the precedence mechanism — every first-occurrence lookup reads it that way, so the nearer declaration wins at every such site without any of them knowing about composition. A restatement adds and never removes: a subtype rewriting an inherited `@doc` leaves the field carrying both, which is what "annotations are not removable" buys. Per-name replacement would need a cardinality on the declared annotation, which v1 does not have. The rule is what makes a count along a composed chain checkable, and it has read-side force wherever an annotation directs reading: a field that tightens presence and writes no annotation of its own inherits its source's, rather than silently losing it. It also fixes which fields carry a selector over a chain (§5.2): `record.discriminators` is the base's statement and the member restates the field pinned, so no per-field mark travels and none has to be cleared.

**Parameterized references.** Both `&` composition and `^` refinement operate on type-refs, which may carry arguments. A refinement of a parameterized type must re-declare its open parameters in its own `<>` slot (§5.10); composing with a parameterized supertype works the same way: `ok => <T> result<T> & { note: text }`. An operand may carry arguments, which may name the declaration's parameters; **an operand that is itself a parameter is a resolver error** — `<T> T & { name: text }` and `<T> T ^ { … }` are refused under the shadowing rule, even where the schema declares an entry named `T`, since a parameter has no field set to flatten until the template is applied (§5.10). A record template's `chain.parents` is a list of **`type_ref`**, not `type_name`, because a parent may be written as an application: while the composing declaration is open, `result<T>` stands there with its arguments, and is substituted and closed with every other reference the record holds (§5.10) — so `ok<text>` reaches an IS-A edge to `result<text>` and not to `result<int32>`, and a head name, which is the name of no type a position is ever typed by and holds of every instantiation at once, is never asked to carry the distinction. The closed chain `supertypes` records names, being computed once every parent is a type; the closed parent's name and its own chain are folded into it when the instantiation closes. A closed type keeps no `parents` and carries no arguments in its chain, so a schema with no open parent produces a chain of bare names. The edge a closed application mints is to the entry its own arguments name, so §7.2 needs no second check and no comparison of bodies. An application standing at a composition operand is **subsumed where it stands**: its arguments are the member's own contribution (`dog => pet<"dog", dog_type> & { … }` is the hand-written `dog => pet & { pet_type?: = "dog"  pet: dog_type }` with one IS-A edge to `pet`), and it mints no entry (§5.10). A record template named *without* arguments at an operand is a resolver error: a position may name the base, but an operand is flattened, and a template has fields only once its parameters are bound (§5.10).

**Resolution.** The composed entry's `chain.supertypes` records the listed parents and, transitively, their own chains (§8.1), complete to a fixed point; inherited fields are copied into the body's `fields` map in the order above, so the body is flat and a consumer never repeats the merge. Which operands were written is not recorded for a closed record: direct parents are the chain's minimal members, and operand order is in the flattened `fields`.


### 5.9 Subtraction

Subtraction removes fields from a construction and deliberately breaks IS-A — the resulting type is no longer source-compatible. Taxonomically it is a construction operation, not a refinement: like composition, it computes a new field set; unlike composition, it disclaims the contract. It is expressed as a trailing **removal clause** on a construction head:

```
removal-set = "-" ws "{" ws field-name *( separator field-name ) ws "}"
```

```
account => { name: text  email: text  password: text }
account_public => account - { password }
account_view   => account & { email?: text ~ "n/a" } - { password }
staff_public   => account & user & { badge: text } - { password  ssn }
```

The clause is head-level: a reader of the declaration line knows the contract is broken without scanning the body. It attaches only to construction heads — a bare source, or an `&` chain with or without a trailing body; a refinement head admits no removal clause (§5.7, §12.1).

Rules:

1. **Resolution order.** Supertypes merge first — §5.8's disjointness rule fires here unchanged, so subtraction cannot be used to resolve diamond conflicts — the body applies second, and removals apply last.
2. **Removing a nonexistent field is a resolver error** — symmetric with refinement's existing-fields-only rule.
3. **Source path does not restrict removal.** Removal operates on the merged field set, not on field provenance; since IS-A is already broken, there is no contract to violate.
4. **The body and the removal set are disjoint.** A removal naming a field the body itself introduces is a resolver error — adding and removing a field in one declaration is incoherent — and a body entry tightening a removed field is a resolver error. Body entries tightening the remaining inherited fields follow §5.7's rules.
5. **Removal and narrowing to `void` are distinct.** `- { field }` removes the field from the contract and breaks IS-A; `field?: void?` (§5.2, §5.7) keeps the field in the contract, forbids its value, and preserves IS-A.
6. **Empty subtraction does not exist**, by grammar: the removal set requires at least one name, and `source & {}` is composition-with-no-additions and preserves IS-A.
7. **Groups.** A removal may name a field-group member; the member leaves its option, an emptied option leaves the group, and a group the declaration rules would then refuse is rewritten as the spelling it equals (§5.11).
8. **Constructors and parameters.** A subtracted construction in a meta-schema whose head is a constructor is not one — it has no supertypes, so it is IS-A nothing and may not be applied — and a parameterised subtraction declares a record template whose field set is the merged set minus the removals, with no parents.
9. **FINAL does not reach subtraction.** A removal off a FINAL record is admissible: FINAL constrains the IS-A set (§5.2), which subtraction never joins, so the result is an unrelated type that happens to share a field list. Composition and refinement, which both mint an IS-A edge, are the two that FINAL refuses.

**Resolution.** A subtracted record is a record with no supertypes: `chain.supertypes` is empty — the subtracted type is not source-compatible — and nothing records what it was subtracted from, provenance being what was written and the source document having it (§4.1). A removed field's annotations are lost with the field. For ingest (§8.1), the empty `supertypes` is read as stated rather than inferred by diffing field sets against the parents.


### 5.10 Templates and Parameters

A type definition may declare parameters using `<>` immediately after `=>`. Parameters are local names referenced in the body and bound to concrete arguments when the definition is referenced with `<args>`. Each parameter has a type, derived from how the body uses it, and may be written with one — `<T: text, N: int8>` (below). A definition with parameters is a **template** — it cannot be instantiated directly, and references to it MUST supply arguments for all its parameters:

```
container => <T> { items: [T] }
pair      => <T, U> { first: T  second: U }
vector    => <T, N> !array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: N }
matrix    => <T, M, N> [[T; N]; M]
```

`container<text>`, `vector<pixel, 1920>`, and `matrix<pixel, 1080, 1920>` are concrete types; bare `container` or `vector` references are resolver errors. A template application's head resolves through the type-name namespace only (§3.3.1); parameters and templates are an authoring-side feature exclusively — resolved output governing data carries none of it (§1.3, §8.2).

**Template forms, and the two kinds of template.** A template declaration's body may be a record (`container`), a container or sugar form (`matrix`), a reference (a pure application body, below), or — the fourth form — an **instance**: a constructor application whose bindings mention parameters (`vector` above). The instance form is the route to a constructor with no sugar of its own (`bounded_set => <N> !set_type { element_type: text  min_items: N }`) and the target the sugar desugars into; for the sugared constructors the compact spelling exists alongside it (`vector` could equally be written `<T, N> [T; N]`). The forms divide in two, and the division is the one C++ (alias template / class template), Haskell (`type` / `data`) and ML (abbreviation / `datatype`) all make. A **record template** — a fresh record, a composition or a refinement whose body is a `!record` — is a *family*: a type a position may name, whose open form is a base and whose applications are its members (below). Every other template — over a container, an atom constructor, a choice, a `scoped`, or a reference — is an *abbreviation*: its open form denotes nothing, and an application of it is its expansion (§8.2). The kernel states the split: a record template is a **`record` with `parameters`**, an ordinary record body read as the vocabulary, with a parameter standing only in the places below; every other open entry is a **`template`** — `template => top & { parameters: [template_param]  template: text }` — the constructor application as written, held as text and *unread* until materialisation substitutes its parameters away. One substitution rule serves both (below).

**Held bodies, and where a parameter stands.** A `template`'s body is not a typed quotation of the constructor's vocabulary, and it is not read against that vocabulary while a parameter remains: a sugar form is desugared per §5.3's table and held; an explicit `<T, N> !array { … }` is held as written. Because a held body is not classified by slot kind, a parameter needs no channel of its own: a parameter in a value slot (`min_items: N`), in a type slot (`element_type: T`), inside a collection (`variants: [T error]`, `members: [a b M]`), or at any depth is a token like any other, and **collection-valued slots are parameterizable** — `result => <T> ( T | error )`, the sum-typed result envelope, resolves and closes to an ordinary `choice` body. A **record template is not held**: `<T> { x: T }` is the record `!record { fields: { x => !record_field { type: T } }  parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind }] }`, read as the vocabulary, and a composition or refinement template is resolved against its namespace first — a tightening entry states a modifier and no type-ref, so it is not a `record_field` until the inherited field supplies one — and the *flattened* record is the entry, its `chain.parents` the parents as written (§5.8). In a record template a parameter stands in **three places and no other**: a field's type (`v: T`), a field's default or fixed value (`w?: int32 ~ N`, `type: text = N`), or an argument of an application (`items: box<T>`, `result<T> & { … }` — sugar inside a field, `[T; N]`, lifts to an application and so is the third). A parameter anywhere else in a record body — as a field name, a group member, `record_field.optional`, `voidable` or `role`, `record.extension`, `name_type` or `discriminators` — is a resolver error at the declaration naming the slot, since the family base (below) would then have no shape its members share. A parameter is never an operand of `&` or `^` (§5.8). The cost of the one-rule substitution is shadowing's usual one: inside a template, a literal spelled like a live parameter is unreachable, so an author renames the parameter.

**Substitution is one rule at any depth.** Binding a template's parameters rewrites the body — the held text, or the record's fields and parents — by replacing every token whose text resolves into the entry's parameter list with the corresponding argument — a reference argument as a `type_ref` (a bare name, or the record form when the argument is itself an application), a literal argument as written — and nothing else is classified. An argument is **read as its parameter's recorded type** (below) and then substituted: where that type is `type_kind` it must resolve as a type, where it is a value type it must be a value of it, and in an enum's member list it is a member — `e => <M> !enum [a b M]` applied as `e<c>` admits `c` because `M` is recorded as a text value, not because of how `c` is spelled. When every parameter of an entry closes, a held body is read, once, against the constructor's own vocabulary — materialisation binds against vocabulary exactly once, at the only moment binding is decidable — and a record template's fields take their arguments in place; the result is an ordinary closed body (§8.2).

**One spelling.** A held body is carried as text and compared as the **parsed form** — never as the text, and never as bound values — and an open synthetic's identity and content-derived name are computed over that parsed form with the parameters renamed positionally (§8.2), so the open form MUST have **one spelling** however many phases produce it: no-argument type names as bare tokens, `type_argument` and `type_ref` records only where an application is open, and parameter tokens unquoted — a producer that quotes every token has written a body that references no parameters at all. Whitespace is free: comparing the parsed form is what keeps "one spelling" from becoming a canonical-whitespace requirement the series has no emitter contract to support. Two of the template shapes cannot be normalised syntactically (composition and refinement need a namespace to flatten against), so "one phase" is not achievable and "one spelling" is the requirement.

**A parameter's type is derived from the positions it stands in, and recorded.** Every parameter of an open entry carries the type an argument for it is read as — the kernel's `template_param => { name: param_name  type: type_kind  bound?: type_kind }` (§8.1) — and the type is *derived*, never required of the author: it is the declared type of the slot the parameter stands in, in the vocabulary of the constructor the body applies. No parameter lacks one, since a parameter the body never references is refused (below) and every position has a declared type:

- **A type slot** — a field declared `type_kind`, such as `array.element_type`, `map.key_type` or `record_field.type` — gives `type_kind`.
- **A value slot** gives the slot's declared type: `min_items: N` gives `non_negative_integer`, and an enum's member list gives `text`.
- **A routed default or fixed value** (`w?: int32 ~ N`) gives the *field's* declared type, `int32` — §5.2's value conformance, read off the field rather than off `record_field.value`, whose own type is `value` and says nothing. Where the field is itself typed by a parameter, `box => <T, N> { w?: T ~ N }`, the derived type is that parameter: `N`'s `type` is `T`. That is the one scoping rule a parameter list needs: **a `type` may name a parameter of the same template declared before it**, and nothing else in the schema's namespaces under that name; at an application the argument for `N` is then read as the type `T`'s argument names.
- **An argument to another template** gives that template's recorded type for the position, so derivation is a **fixed point** across templates that apply each other. A parameter the fixed point leaves undetermined is given `type_kind`: a reference template's body *is* its application (`reference.target` is a `type_ref`, §8.1), so `loop => <T> loop<T>` has no slot for a concrete use and its `T` is a type parameter — what is wrong with that declaration is that it applies itself forever and denotes no type, and that is what its diagnostic names.
- **A payload in §5.6's positional form** is walked as the one field it binds: `names => <M> !enum [a b M]` puts `M` in `enum.members` exactly as `!enum { members: [a b M] }` does. The kernel's own `kind => <T> !kind_type { of: T }` records `T` as `type_name`, `of`'s declared type, and is a value parameter: its argument is a constructor's name (§4.2).

**The kind follows from the type.** A parameter whose `type` is `type_kind` is a **type parameter**; any other is a **value parameter**. Nothing records the kind apart from the type, since a separate field would state one fact twice. Value parameters bind **scalars only** — numbers, booleans, identifiers, and quoted strings; type parameters bind references, simple or compound. There is no parameter arithmetic: every value argument is used as given.

**Several uses must agree.** A parameter's `type` is the use type that IS-A every other; where the use types are not ordered by IS-A the declaration is a resolver error. `<N> !array { min_items: N  max_items: N }` derives `non_negative_integer` twice and is fine; `both => <T> { a: T  b?: int32 ~ T }` derives `type_kind` and `int32`, which no argument could satisfy, and is refused — a parameter standing in both kinds of position is this rule's commonest case and needs none of its own. The rule follows IS-A edges only and proves nothing about value sets: two sibling refinements such as `int8` and `int32` are refused together though a small integer satisfies both, and the author declares the narrower type by name and uses it in both places. That is the price of a total, two-valued rule checked once at the declaration, where a list of use types checked one by one would move the verdict to every application. **A declared parameter the body never references is a resolver error** — `<T> !array { element_type: text }` is a mistake, not a degenerate template, and the rule holds for every template form (`box => <T> { v: text }` likewise).

**A declaration may narrow what it derives.** A parameter may be written with a type after its name — `<T: text, N: int8>`, one production for every template form (§12.1) — and the written type is read by the kind the positions give, never the reverse, so an annotation cannot contradict the uses:

- **On a value parameter it narrows `type`.** `<N: int8> { w?: integer ~ N }` records `type: int8`, and the written type MUST be IS-A the type the uses derive, or the declaration is a resolver error: `<N: text> !array { element_type: text  min_items: N }` is refused, `text` not being a `non_negative_integer`.
- **On a type parameter it is `bound`.** `boxed => <T: text> { a: T }` records `type: type_kind  bound: text`, and an argument MUST name `text` or a type that IS-A it, judged after following its reference chain (§8.3); `boxed<int32>` is refused at the application. `bound` is present only where `type` is `type_kind`, and is its own field because a type parameter carries two facts — its argument is a reference, and the reference is restricted — where narrowing `type` to `text` would lose the first and leave `<T: text> { a: T }` and `<M: text> !enum [a b M]` recording alike.
- **An unwritten parameter keeps what it derives**, so a template that writes no types resolves as it always did.

**A bound is inherited** through another template's argument list, as a type is: `rebox => <T> boxed<T>` records its `T` with `bound: text`, so `rebox<int32>` is refused at its own application rather than deep inside `boxed`. Several inherited bounds agree by the rule for several types, and a written bound MUST be IS-A every inherited one — `<T: currency_code> boxed<T>` narrows it where `currency_code` refines `text`, and `<T: int32> boxed<T>` is refused at the declaration. A bound follows **IS-A edges only**: `date` has a text form without being `text`, and a bound that followed a type's form or its discrimination class would admit it. **A bound names a type, and only a type**: it resolves in the schema's type-name namespace (§3.3.2), so a name found only among the governing meta's constructors — `<T: text_type>` — admits nothing and is refused at the declaration. Construction transfers kind and no supertypes (§5.5), so a nominal bound cannot say "any text-valued atom": `identifier`, a profile a schema constructs, and `stock_code => !text_type {}` are IS-A nothing and fail `<T: text>`. That separation is right for values and is a limit for bounds, recorded among the boundaries below.

**A value conforms to where it is defined, and a template's form follows from it.** A value parameter's type is read where the slot it binds into is defined: a constructor's own slot (`array.min_items`, `scoped.schemas`' keys) is the meta's, so the value is a meta value; a field the schema types (`w?: integer ~ N`) is the schema's. A type parameter names a local type — the argument is a schema type captured into a `type_kind` slot, as `set<T>` captures one into `element_type` — so its `bound` is the schema's whatever slot it lands in. Since a constructor template's value parameters can only bind into the meta's own slots, and a record template's only into fields the schema types, the rule reads off the template's form. A template whose held body applies a constructor of the governing meta other than `record` — `vec => <N> !array { element_type: text  min_items: N }`, core's `extern_type => <S, T> !scoped { … schemas: { S => [T] } }` — binds its values into the meta's own structure, so each value parameter's `type` is a type of the **structure namespace** (§3.3.1): `N` is the meta's `non_negative_integer` and `T` the kernel's `type_name`, read, recorded and checked there. A written type on such a parameter names a type there too, so `<N: non_negative_integer>` narrows within the meta's vocabulary and `<N: int8>` is a resolver error, `int8` not being in it; and a schema's own entry under a structure name is a different type that never stands in for it — a schema declaring `non_negative_integer => !integer ^ { min: 1 }` still has `vec<0>` as a valid application, `min_items: 0` being what the constructor admits. A record template's fields are typed by the schema, so its parameters — a routed default's type and a written type alike — read in the **schema's own namespace**: `<N: int8> { w?: integer ~ N }` records `int8`. A template whose body applies another template takes that template's form, and the entry's value says which form it is — a `!record` with `parameters`, or a `!template` whose text's first token names the constructor — so a consumer of resolved output reads a recorded `type` without the held body (§1.3). Uses of one parameter can still come from both namespaces — a record template passing `N` to `vec` and routing it into a field — and entries under one name in the two have no IS-A edge between them. **Two same-named entries with the same resolved body are one type wherever this section compares parameter types** — a written type against a derived or inherited one, the several uses of one parameter against each other, and an argument against a parameter's type or bound — and for nothing else: everywhere else a declared entry's identity is its name (§8.2), and a core sibling remains a distinct type from its kernel original (§3.3.5).

**An application is checked at the call site.** Each argument is read as its parameter's `type` before anything is substituted, against the parameter list alone: `vector<pixel, 1920>` reads `pixel` as a type reference and `1920` as a `non_negative_integer`, and `vector<pixel, "two">` is refused at the argument, not inside the body. A type argument must name a type that IS-A the parameter's bound; a value argument must parse as the parameter's type, or as the type an earlier parameter's argument names. An argument the check cannot judge on its own — an application, a name that does not yet resolve — is left to the substituted body, whose verdict is the same one. The check is what lets a consumer of resolved output — an importing schema, a second resolver comparing output, a host binder generating a generic type — verify an application from the entry, without the held text (§1.3).

**Scoping and shadowing.** Parameters are local to the declaring definition; they do not escape and do not compose across `&`, and two definitions can independently use the same parameter name. Two positions that must agree share a parameter (`homogeneous_pair => <T> { first: T  second: T }`) — sharing the name is the link. Within a held body, parameter names take precedence over the schema namespace, and **a parameter that shadows a schema type is a resolver error** — renaming a parameter is free, and silent capture of a type the body also means to use is the confusability hazard [TSON-DATA] §8.2 exists for. A template's parameter list is not one of §11.4's hygiene scopes: one author writes it whole on one line, and a deliberate confusable pair there is not a population the list should grow for.

**Declaration-time checking splits by what the parameter list obscures, and nothing more.** Two questions are answered at the declaration from the held body's own field names, needing no stand-in values and so unable to fabricate a verdict: that each binding key names a field the constructor declares (`<T> !array { elemen_type: T }` is a typo, not a template), and that every unmarked-name field is bound. The typing of every *concrete* binding is checked there too (`min_items: "two"` is not an integer whatever `T` becomes), as is the unreferenced-parameter rule, from the tokens the held body names, and so are the parameter types themselves: the derivation, the agreement of several uses, and a written type or bound against what the uses derive (above). Everything value-shaped that a parameter obscures waits for materialisation, where the whole body binds through the constructor's own reader (§8.2). An **unapplied** template is checked no further and receives no verdict: a resolver MUST NOT check it by substituting stand-in values, since that manufactures false errors on exactly the slots the mechanism exists for (`<N> !integer_type { min: N  max: 3 }` is correct for every argument anyone passes and fails under a stand-in of 10). Deferring everything would make the template the one form validated at use rather than at declaration, shipping broken templates to fail at their first user's application site; deferring only what the parameters obscure is the line.

**Where a deferred diagnostic lands.** A materialisation diagnostic MUST be located at the declaration whose text wrote the offending name, with the application as context — deferred checking is survivable only if the author is sent to the line they can edit. The rule is over the *name*, not over the template: `m => <T> !map { key_type: T  value_type: no_such_type }`, a held body whose names are read when it closes, applied by `holder` belongs to `m`, which wrote the name — a record template is read at its declaration, so a name it writes is reported there — while `box => <T> { v: T }` applied as `box<3>` belongs to `holder`, which wrote the `3` — and is found there by the call-site check, before substitution. One defect earns one diagnostic, however many declarations apply the template.

**Sugar inside templates.** A sugar form inside a template body desugars to the same construction it would outside one — §5.3's table unchanged — and lifts to its own synthetic entry, **open** if the form mentions a parameter (§5.3). Nesting needs no special member: an inner form lifts to its own open synthetic, and the outer body holds an ordinary open application of it. At its smallest, `box => <T> { a: [T] }` desugars as if written

```
array_t => <T> !array { element_type: T }
box     => !record { fields: { a => !record_field { type: { name: array_t  arguments: [ { name: T } ] } } }  parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind }] }
```

with `array_t` an internal name, held, and `box` a record with `parameters`. The application in `box`'s field rides `type_ref.arguments` — the channel that means "an application" and nothing else — at a `type_kind` slot that, while the record is open, holds an application a parameter leaves unresolved. Applications inside a held body close before its entry is named: desugar lifts innermost-first, so a form it writes already names the entry its inner form became, and a form closed at materialisation MUST agree — `[[pixel; 3]; 3]` written out and `grid<pixel, 3>` closed land on one entry for one type. Because a closed lift hashes its binding record at desugar, before inner applications are rewritten, while an open lift hashes the closed record at materialisation, the two channels can produce two candidate entries for one type (`[box<text>]` written directly, and `[box<T>]` closed with `T := text`); §8.2's merge pass, which re-derives every synthetic's identity from its *resolved* record after Pass 2, is therefore **required**, not an optimisation.

**A record template is a family base, and may be named at a type position.** "A template is not a type" is right for `vector<T, N>` and wrong for a base, and the difference is *elimination*: a position typed `vector` would have to read a value against an element type nothing has fixed, leaving a reader to infer arguments from the payload — ill-defined, since `[]` determines nothing and two instantiations can accept one document. None of that reaches a base, because **no value is ever read against it**: a value there is a value of some member, selected by a tag or by reading the selector fields, and every member is a closed entry with every argument fixed. So a **record template** — a fresh record, a composition, or a refinement whose body is a `!record` — may stand at a field, element, variant or argument position as a base, and the record template entry *is* the base: a type, a member of `kind<record>` (§4.2), what the position names, what `subtypes` indexes, what its own parents' `subtypes` list, and what a host sealed type binds to, with no second entry minted beside it. **Its members are the applications a declaration names** — `bt => box<int32>`, or a composition `dog => pet<"dog", dog_type> & { … }` — and only those. An application written at a use site (`k: box<text>`) is a type, read structurally where it is written, and no member: no declaration names it, so it is never a candidate at a position typed by the template and never needs a name a document cannot write (§8.2). This is §5.2's "a family member is declared", kept by leaving the minted application out rather than by refusing the schema; an author who wants it in the family declares it. **The base's `extension` is ABSTRACT by construction** — never OPEN, nothing being able to write a value whose type is the template rather than one of its applications, and never FINAL, its applications being subtypes by construction — and is not stated: the record's own `extension` is **each application's**, OPEN unless the template says otherwise, which is how a second-level base is spelled, `result => abstract <T> { payload: T }` making `result<text>` abstract over `ok<text>` and `err<text>`; `final` written on a template is a resolver error. Its `discriminators` are the base's selector list: whatever selectors survive erasure of the parameters — a field written `=?`, or, in a *fresh* record template, a field pinned to a value parameter, whatever its name's mark, one value per application being one pin per member (`pet => <N, T> { type?: text = N  pet: T }`); the base reads such a selector required with the pin dropped, and each member is FIXED to its argument, optional as the name was marked (§5.7), exactly as a hand-written member restating `pet_type?: = "dog"` is. A refinement or composition template derives none: `<T> array ^ { element_type: = T }` pins a constructor's own facet and states nothing about a family. The one **condition** is on a selector: its declared type MUST mention none of the template's parameters, at any depth — not as its head and not in an argument, a value parameter as much as a type one — because a decoder parses the selectors *before* it knows the member and their pins are exactly what the parameters supply; `type: text = N` satisfies it; over `capped => <M> !integer_type { max: M }`, `kind: capped<9> = N` satisfies it (a parameter-free application is the entry it expands to, in the base as in every member), and `badge => <M, N> { kind: capped<M> = N  name: text }` is refused at the template, since `badge<9, 3>` and `badge<5, 4>` would give the selector two types. A reference template (`id => <T> T`, a partial application), a container template (`arr => <T> [T]`), a constructor-application template (`vector`) and an atom template (no form, §12.1) are **not** bases and are refused at a type position: a reference is indirection with no contract for a base to hold, abstractness has nowhere to live on a non-record body, and `vector<float32, 3>` and `vector<float32, 4>` erase to one host type no reader could place a value within. What a base carries is what survives erasure — a value parameter's pin dropped and the field kept, a field whose type mentions a type parameter omitted — so a base's field list may be smaller than its members' and may be **empty**; member dispatch alone has a floor, at least one selector, and a base claiming dispatch with none is refused. The base's facts do not reach a member. The base's ABSTRACT is the base's alone, a member's `extension` being the record's own; and a declared member states no `discriminators` — closing pins each selector a parameter pins to its argument, and a `=?` selector is pinned by no argument, so closing does not pin it. A resolved-output consumer recognises the base by its `parameters` and dispatches on its `discriminators`, and never reads a field that carries a parameter (§1.3), which is why the selectors are stated on the record; the base is ABSTRACT by construction, and the record's `extension` is each application's. A record template named *bare* at an operand of `&` or `^` is a resolver error: a position may name the base, but an operand is flattened, and a template has fields only once its parameters are bound (§5.8).

**Templates are not directly instantiable.** An entry with a non-empty parameter list cannot validate data: a template with any parameter is a resolver error as a data annotation, without exception (§7.2), and a family base is reached by a *position* naming it, never by a tag naming it — the tag names a member.

**Open bodies in output.** While parameters remain unbound, an entry's resolved form is one of two values (§8.1). A **record template** is `!record { fields: { … }  parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind } …] }` with its parameters in place — a field's `type` or `value` holding a parameter token, or a `type_ref` application an argument of which names one — its `discriminators` stated as the family's, its `extension` each application's, and its `chain.parents` the parents as written. Every other open entry is an instance of the kernel's `template` constructor — `!template { parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind } { name: N  type: non_negative_integer }]  template: "!array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: N }" }` — carrying the parameters, each with its type, and, as **text**, the held application under the one-spelling rule: a container as `!array`/`!map`/`!tuple`/`!choice`, an atom constructor as `!integer_type`, an alias as `!reference` with its partial application in `target` (§8.1, §8.3). Text is what "held" means: a parameter stands wherever a token stands — `min_items: N` as readily as `element_type: T` — so a body carrying one is not a value of any constructor's own record shape until it closes, and writing it as though it were leaves the two halves disagreeing, a value parameter refusing to read at all and a type parameter reading as a reference to a type nobody declared; a record body has no such slot once a parameter stands in three places only, which is why it is not held. The `template` constructor composes with `top` directly and carries no chain, `extension` or `discriminators`: it is not a type, its open form denotes nothing, and an application of it is its expansion, with whatever chain the expansion's own `^` or `&` gives it. Openness is one question with one answer — an entry has `parameters`, as a `!record` or a `!template`, or it is closed. **A parameter reference appears only inside an open entry's own body**: a held text, or a record template's fields and parents. Closed bodies are binding records and never contain parameter references (§5.6).

**Materialisation.** When every parameter closes, the resolver materialises the result (§8.2): materialisation is total and closes **innermost-out** — each open synthetic the application reaches becomes closed as its bindings go concrete, a held body whose last parameter is substituted being read against its constructor's vocabulary. Given `grid => <T, N> { x: [[T; 1..N]; 2..N] }`, closing `grid<pixel, 3>` yields

```
c1 => !array { element_type: pixel  min_items: 1  max_items: 3 }
c2 => !array { element_type: c1     min_items: 2  max_items: 3 }
c3 => !record { fields: { x => !record_field { type: c2 } } }
```

`c1` and `c2` are closed synthetics keyed on body structure — `c1` is the same entry an independently written `[pixel; 1..3]` produces anywhere in the schema — and `c3` is the expansion — a closed synthetic keyed on its content where a use site wrote the application; where a declaration did, the declaring entry itself, which carries `@application:{ name: grid  arguments: [{name: pixel} {value: 3}] }` on its key (§8.2). Deferred value checks land here: substituting `N := "two"` yields `min_items: "two"`, and *that* is the error, located per the rule above. A recursive reference inside a nested form denotes, at materialisation, the entry under construction — the open synthetic's binding references that entry by its internal name before the entry is complete.

**A declaration naming a fully-bound application is that application's entry.** `string_triple => vector<text, 3>` resolves to `!array { element_type: text  min_items: 3  max_items: 3 }` under the author's name, with `@application:{ name: vector  arguments: [ { name: text }  { value: 3 } ] }` on its key — the closed instantiation, in place, with no minted `vector_text_3_…` beside it and no `!reference` hop: the axis is construction against reference, and an application is a parameterised reference syntactically and a construction semantically, filed by what it is (§8.2). A use-site application resolves to a declaration that owns it where one exists, and to its **expansion** otherwise — the closed synthetic the substituted form lands on, which any other spelling of the same form lands on too, minting no instantiation entry (§8.2). **Minting is keyed on naming, not on the mark**: an application at a composition operand is subsumed there and mints nothing (§5.8), and `result<text>` written at a position and `ok<text>` composing onto `result<T>` coexist under one rule. A declaration whose body is a fully-bound application of a *constructor* resolves as a construction in place on the same footing (§5.6).

**Partial application.** When a reference to or refinement of a parameterized type leaves parameters open, it MUST re-declare the open parameters in its own `<>` slot — implicit parameter inheritance is not permitted; every parameter has a visible declaration site:

```
text_keyed_map => <V> {text => V}
uuid_pair      => <B> pair<uuid, B>
```

A partially-applied reference is itself a template, and — being a reference — never a family base (above). The sugar case is an open synthetic-backed template whose held body is `!map { key_type: text  value_type: V }`; the alias case is the held application `!reference { target: { name: pair  arguments: [ { name: uuid }  { name: B } ] } }` under a one-entry parameter list for `B` — the application in `type_ref`'s record form, as every application inside a `!C` payload is written (§12.1); `reference.target` is a `type_ref`, so an alias to an application states the arguments it already binds *inside the held body* (§8.1, §8.3), and the partial application is an ordinary open entry rather than the one shape that had to keep its arguments elsewhere. Reference-kind templates compose during substitution — no intermediate entry per alias hop; a use site states the name it named (§8.3). `reference` is applicable because it IS-A `top` (§3.3.1): it describes no *value*, but it describes a hop, which is the question a construction site asks; its entries take the REFERENCE kind from the alias form rather than from a base kind (§4.1). A resolver tells the open cases apart by the value — a `!record` with `parameters` closes to a record, the declaration's own where a declaration names the application and a content-keyed synthetic otherwise; a held `!reference` composes and mints nothing; every other held constructor closes to its expansion, a synthetic (§8.2).

**Heads are not parameters.** Parameters bind *arguments of a named head*; the head itself — the type being refined, composed, or applied — is always a concrete name. `<A, N> A ^ { max_items: = N }`, abstracting over the refinement source, is not expressible, and a generic-application head that resolves to a parameter (`weird => <map> map<text, text>`) is a resolver error, diagnosed rather than applied (§3.3.1). Generic derivation over arbitrary heads is a deliberate v1 boundary, as is a parameterized atom refinement: `<N> !integer ^ { min: N }` is no form at all (§12.1) — the constructor spelling `<N> !integer_type { min: N }` is the open route.

**What a parameter does not carry** — a second deliberate v1 boundary, narrower than it was. A parameter carries a type and, where it is a type parameter, a nominal bound (above). **It carries no bound on the constructor an argument's type was built with, by design.** A type parameter names a local type, so its bound is a local type, and a constructor is the meta's vocabulary: `<T: text_type> set<T>` would judge a schema's type by how the meta built it, the crossing the paragraph above refuses. "Any text-valued atom" is not a type, because construction creates siblings, not subtypes (§5.5): `stock_code => !text_type {}` founds a family of its own so that a stock code never passes where `text` is declared, and an author who wants a type admitted by `<T: text>` refines into the family — `stock_code => !text ^ { pattern: … }` — rather than constructing beside it. "Any scalar" is likewise unneeded: a set of records is a set, and a record map key is a compound key (§7.7) that [TSON-JSON] writes in pairs form; a schema that wants scalars states the element or key type. A parameter carries no default argument either. A type slot cannot be bounded at a *field* by a nominal bound as it can at a template; what a slot can say of a name it holds is which constructor built the type (`request?: kind<record>`, §4.2) or which type the value named is of (`handler: ref<method>`, §5.12), and a dependency between two fields of one constructor — `record_field.value` on `record_field.type` (§5.2) — stays a rule stated in prose rather than structure. Each is additive: it can be introduced later without changing the template model, so its absence is a recorded deferral, not a design claim.

**Closed entries are parameter-free**, and the rule is structural rather than a MUST over two fields that have to agree: a closed entry is one with no `parameters` — its value is a binding record, a `!record` without `parameters`, a `!reference` or a namespace value, and contains no parameter reference at any depth, there being no parameter list anywhere for a token to resolve into — and an open entry is a `!record` with `parameters` or a `!template`, with its parameters declared once, in the value that holds them (§8.1). Ingest verifies the one thing left to verify: that no token inside a closed body names a parameter of any enclosing template, which is a resolution failure like any other. Only entries with `parameters` may be open.

The `_` token is not valid in field-type positions (§7.6); authors expressing "type to be filled later" use parameters.


#### 5.10.1 Self-Referential Types

Types may reference themselves:

```
node => { value: text  children: [node] }
linked_list => <T> { value: T  next?: linked_list<T> }
tree => <T> { value: T  children?: [tree<T>] }
```

The resolver MUST detect and handle cycles in type references. In a template body, a recursive reference is an open application like any other — `tree`'s `children` carries a reference to the open synthetic for `[tree<T>]`, whose binding applies `tree<T>` (§5.3, §5.10) — and needs no special representation.

**Regular recursion.** Within a template body, a recursive application — direct or mutual — MUST pass each parameter through **unchanged**: `tree<T>` inside `tree`'s own body is well-formed; `weird => <T> { next: weird<[T]>? }`, whose recursive application rebuilds the argument, is a resolver error at the declaration. This is the standard regularity restriction of recursive-type theory, and it belongs beside this section's other v1 boundaries (no head abstraction, no constructor bounds): without it, one application could demand an unbounded family of distinct instantiations, and no portable depth limit distinguishes that from legitimate depth. The check is static, at declaration time.

**Productivity.** A type MUST admit at least one finite value; a definition with no finite member — a required direct or mutual self-reference with no terminating alternative — is a **resolver error** at schema load, not a warning: `item => { inner: item }` describes no data, and validation against it can only ever fail, so rejecting it at the schema is the earlier, better diagnostic. A recursive reference is **guarded**, and the definition productive, when every cycle through it passes at least one position that can terminate: a field whose key may be omitted or whose type admits `_` (`?` on the name or on the type, §5.2), a voidable tuple position, an array or map position whose floor admits emptiness or whose elements may be void, or a choice with at least one non-recursive variant. `a: void` — a field that must be written and admits nothing — is the one declaration this rule refuses on its own, the record having no member at all (§5.2); `a?: void` is legal, emptying only the field. An ABSTRACT record with no subtype in the declaring schema's closure is **not** a productivity error (§5.2): its members are its importers' to supply. A group that must be chosen (§5.11) terminates when at least one of its options can be chosen without recursion — one option is chosen, so one terminating option suffices — and an option terminates when every member it requires does. Every entry is judged, synthetic entries included; a template is judged with its parameters assumed inhabited (a bound names a type that is itself judged, §5.10, so an argument can always be chosen productive), and its materialisations need no re-check the entry did not already pass. Productivity is structural: whether an atom's *constraints* admit any value (a `min > max` interval) is a separate, value-level question (§5.3, §7.4).

**Template materialisation.** When a template application closes, it resolves to the declaration that names it where one exists, and to its expansion otherwise (§8.2). A self-reaching expansion is the one case an application nothing declares keeps an **instantiation entry** of its own: `nested => <T> ( T | [nested<T>] )` closed at `nested<text>` names itself inside its own body, so its content cannot be written without a name, and the knot is tied on the application — a `!reference` to the synthetic, keyed on the canonical application and carrying `@application` (§8.2). A self-reaching application keeps one instantiation entry per canonical application — arguments compared after following reference chains (§8.2) — so `tree<text>` and `tree<integer>` are two entries and `tree<user_id>` over `user_id => text` is `tree<text>`'s, and every further occurrence of the same application anywhere in the schema reuses the existing one. The entry's name is internal (§8.2). Recursive references within the body, once bound, denote the entry being materialised; the resolver ties the knot by resolving them to the entry's own name (§5.10). A non-recursive template's application is its expansion alone (§8.2).


### 5.11 Field Groups

A record body may declare a **field group**: a parenthesised presence rule over fields of the record. A group lists **options** separated by `|`, each holding one or more labelled members separated as fields are, and at most one option may be chosen in conforming data. The field names are the discriminator; the wire form of instances is unchanged by grouping.

```
integer_type => atom & {
  size?: integer_size
  ( min: integer | exclusive_min: integer )?
  ( max: integer | exclusive_max: integer )?
  multiple_of?: non_negative_integer
  members?:     integer_member_set
}

endpoint => { ( host: text  port: uint16 | socket: text ) }
contact  => { name: text  ( email: email | phone: text )+ }
```

Each member is a `field-name`/`type-ref` pair, the type optionally voidable (`?` on the type, §5.2) and the name optionally marked (`?` on the name, below). Member labels share the enclosing record's field namespace and its name type (§5.2): a label MUST be unique across the record's plain fields and all groups' members, including fields contributed by supertypes (§5.8's disjointness rule extends to member labels) — so options are disjoint, and a field sits in one option of one group.

**Three rules decide validity.**

- An option is **chosen** when any of its members is present.
- A chosen option MUST contain every member not marked `?`. A `?` on a member's *name* marks it optional **within its option**: it may be missing once its option is chosen.
- A bare group admits **exactly one** chosen option. A `?` after the closing parenthesis makes the group **optional** — it may be missing as a whole, as an optional field's key may — and it then admits at most one. A group has no default or fixed form.

`( include: I | name?: N  type?: T )` therefore admits `include`, `name`, `type`, and `name` with `type`; it refuses an empty record, and `include` beside either of the others.

**`+` is sugar for "at least one of".** `( email: E | phone: P )+` desugars to `( email?: E  phone?: P )` — a group of one option, not optional, whose members are all marked. That option must be chosen, and it is chosen when any of its members is present, so the group admits every non-empty subset of them. `+` is allowed only where every option is one unmarked member; the desugared form is the kernel's, and `+` is the only way to write it. Like `?`, it is adjacent to the `)` it marks, and it is the one use the series has for a bare `+` ([TSON-DATA] §7.2.5).

| Rule | Spelling |
|---|---|
| exactly one of `a` and `b` | `( a: A \| b: B )` |
| at most one of `a` and `b` | `( a: A \| b: B )?` |
| at least one of `email` and `phone` | `( email: E \| phone: P )+` |
| `include` alone, or at least one of `name` and `type` | `( include: I \| name?: N  type?: T )` |
| both `a` and `b`, or neither | `( a: A  b: B )?` |
| `a` requires `b`; `b` may appear alone | `( b: B  a?: A )?` |
| `a` and `c` each require `b` | `( b: B  a?: A  c?: C )?` |
| either `host` and `port`, or `socket` | `( host: H  port: P \| socket: S )` |

**Each presence rule has one spelling.** A group is refused, at schema load, wherever it restates what plain fields or another group already state, and the diagnostic SHOULD name the spelling it restates:

- **The only member of an option takes no `?`.** It is present exactly when its option is chosen, so the mark changes nothing: `( a?: A | b: B )` is `( a: A | b: B )`.
- **A written group of one option is optional, with at least two members and one of them unmarked** — `( b: B  a?: A )?`. Every other group of one option restates something. Bare with an unmarked member, it is plain fields: `( b: B  a?: A )` is `b: B  a?: A`. Bare with every member marked, it is what `+` desugars to, and is written that way. Optional with every member marked, it is optional fields: `( a?: A  b?: B )?` is `a?: A  b?: B`, and `( a: A )?` is `a?: A`.
- **`+` takes options of one unmarked member each.** `( a?: A | b: B )+` and `( host: H  port: P | socket: S )+` are refused.

A declared group therefore has at least two members, and every option can be chosen on its own, so a declared group is never unsatisfiable and loading needs no search. There is no form for any number of options: options free to be chosen together are independent, and such a group is always its options written as separate groups or plain fields.
