Groups do not nest, and a field under two presence rules has no spelling — it sits in one option of one group.

**Member positions are deliberately bare.** A member takes a name, optionally marked, and a type-ref, optionally voidable, and nothing else: the `~`, `=` and `=?` modifiers are parse errors on a member — selection belongs to the label, presence belongs to the group, and a group never injects. A voidable member written as `_` is **present** and chooses its option; at a member that is not voidable a written `_` is the ordinary refusal (§7.6). A group is not a type-ref: it cannot appear in field-type, element, argument, or variant positions, so multiplicity around a group (`[( a: T | b: U )]`) is not expressible; repetition of alternatives is written as an array of a named choice type (§5.4).

**Resolution.** Groups flatten. Each member becomes an ordinary `record_field` in the body's `fields` map — in source order, contiguous with its sibling members — with `optional: true` and `role: FREE` whatever the group says, and `voidable` as written. The grouping is recorded in the body's `record.groups` list as a `field_group`:

```
field_group => {
  members:           [[text; 1..]; 1..]
  optional_members?: [text; 1..]
  optional?:         boolean ~ false
}
```

`members` holds one list per option, options and their members in source order; each slot is `text` because a member names a declared field by its declared spelling, judged by the record's name type and never a different one (§5.2). `optional_members` names the members marked `?`, and is absent rather than empty where none is marked, so a group has one spelling. `optional` is the group's own `?`, omitted in output at its default (§8.1). So `( min: integer | exclusive_min: integer )?` resolves to `!field_group { members: [[min] [exclusive_min]]  optional: true }`, and `( email: E | phone: P )+` to `!field_group { members: [[email phone]]  optional_members: [email phone] }`. The in-option mark is recorded on the group and not on the field, for three reasons: `record_field.optional` keeps meaning "the key may be missing", which stops being true of a member the moment its option is chosen; a consumer that ignores groups reads every member as optional, which is loose but never refuses a valid document, where the alternative would have it refuse one; and a member's presence stays the group's alone to state. The flattened fields-plus-groups form is canonical: group membership is fully derivable from `groups` in one local pass, and implementations SHOULD compile it into a per-record lookup at schema-load time, per the eager-resolution convention of §5.2 and §7.4 — the output form is canonical, not operational.

**Validation.** For each group of a record type, after ordinary field validation, the validator finds the chosen options, reports each chosen option's missing members, and then counts: a group that is not optional errs with no option chosen or with two or more; an optional group errs only on two or more. A present member is validated as an ordinary field of its declared type. Diagnostics stay local, and SHOULD read in the group's own terms: "`port` chose (host, port), which needs `host`"; "`include` and `type` choose two options; exactly one is allowed"; "none of (email, phone) is present; at least one is required".

**Refinement and composition.** In a refinement or composition body, a restated member **stays a member**, in its own option. It may drop its `?`, which narrows, and may not add one; a member of a `+` group is restated as it is written there, without a `?`, and that changes nothing. Its type slot may move voidable true → false or narrow the type, as at any field. A restated member narrowed to `void` and not voidable (`a: void`, which admits no value and refuses `_`) can no longer be present: an *unmarked* one makes its option unchoosable, and a *marked* one drops out of its option. A group left with no choosable option is unsatisfiable, as a group that must be chosen with every member so narrowed always was. Narrowed to `void?` a member can still be written, as `_`, and written so it is present and chooses its option — a member that carries presence and nothing else. A restated member may take `= v`, which is checked when written and **never injected** — the one pin that does not inject, because an injected member would be present, and presence is what chooses the group's option. `~ v` stays refused on a member: a default is a value only a missing key reaches, and whether a member's key may be missing is the group's to say. A member reachable by refinement may not acquire a selector (§5.2). A body entry may also restate a group: the restated group MUST have the same options, with the same member labels in the same order (member type-refs restated verbatim), and may tighten it from optional to not optional; the reverse is a resolver error, and changing membership is a resolver error. Supertypes contribute their groups whole; the composed entry's `groups` lists inherited groups in supertype order followed by the body's own. A field belongs to at most one group — guaranteed by label disjointness.

**Removal.** A removal clause naming a member (§5.9) removes it from `fields` and from its option; an emptied option leaves the group, and a group with no option left is removed outright, with nothing left to occupy its logical position. A group the declaration rules above would then refuse is **rewritten as the spelling it equals** — plain fields or an accepted group, one of which always exists. The simplest case is a group reduced to a single one-member option: it is dissolved, the surviving field taking the group's `optional` as its own (an unmarked name where the group had to be chosen, `?` on the name where it was optional; the field's own voidability is unchanged). The declaration rules are thus an invariant of resolved output as well as of source: no resolved record carries a group they refuse.

**The labelled-sum pattern** (informative). A record whose entire body is a single group that must be chosen, each option one field, admits exactly one field — a labelled sum in record clothing:

```
timestamps => { ( created: timestamp | modified: timestamp | accessed: timestamp ) }
```

An instance is `{ modified: "2026-05-21T13:05:00Z" }` — quoted, since a datetime contains colons ([TSON-DATA] §7.1); the label is both the semantic role and the discriminator. Where `choice` (§5.4) discriminates by variant type name — via a `!variant` annotation, required except where the choice is disjoint under the encoding's discrimination — and requires distinct variant types, a single-group record discriminates by label and permits variants of the same underlying type, needing no disjointness and no tag. The pattern's kind is PRODUCT; host bindings MAY recognise the shape (one such group, no other fields) and lower it to a native sum, and where an option holds several fields the variant it lowers to is record-shaped. [TSON-GUIDE] discusses the pattern and the design history.


### 5.12 Values by Name

A schema may hold a value declared elsewhere and write it by its name. The slot that holds such a name is typed by a **reference**, `ref<T>` (§4.2), and the name is a **qualified name**: segments joined by `.`, the first a type name and each further one a selection inside the value the name has reached so far. The need arises in a meta-schema's vocabulary — an HTTP binding naming the method it serves, a service naming its interfaces — and the rule is one gap closed once: *a slot that holds a value declared elsewhere, written by its name.*

```
!!meta:"https://tson.io/2026/38/m/meta-kernel.tn"
!!import:"https://tson.io/2026/38/m/meta.tn"
{
  method_name => identifier
  method      => { request?: kind<record>  response?: type_kind  errors?: [kind<record>] }
  interface   => namespace & { key_type: = method_name  value_type: = method }
  http_verb   => !enum [GET POST]
  binding     => { method: ref<method>  verb: http_verb  route: text }
  bindings    => namespace & { key_type: = identifier  value_type: = binding }
}
```

```
orders => !interface { place_order => { request: place_order_request  response: order } }
routes => !bindings { post_orders => { method: orders.place_order  verb: POST  route: "/orders" } }
```

**The qualified name.** A qualified name is one unquoted token by [TSON-DATA] §7.1's token profile — `.` is a token character and the lexer emits `orders.place_order` as one token — parsed into segments by the slot's type, each segment a [TSON-DATA] §7.7 identifier. The first segment resolves as a `type_ref` does: in the writer's type-name namespace, locally and through imports, alias hops followed (§8.3), and names an entry. Each further segment selects **in a value**: a key of a **namespace value** (§4.1), or a field of a **record value**; a map that is no namespace is not entered, and nothing else is. Every hop is written — nothing is skipped and nothing is looked up lexically, so a name means the same wherever it stands in a document — and the grammar is names only: no `[n]` (an element of a list is not a thing a schema declares, and `[` is structural in the lexer), no quoted segment (`api."/x".request` is three tokens, and a text key is data, declared nowhere), and no empty segment (`orders..place_order` is three tokens under the range rule, and a leading or trailing `.` is refused by the segment grammar). A name that wants a position rather than a named thing is a JSON Pointer in a quoted string, not a widening of this grammar. A segment is judged by §7.7's grammar wherever it stands, when the name is resolved; a map is entered only where it is a value of a namespace type, whose `key_type` is an identifier family, so a key outside §7.7 under a wider key profile cannot be written as a segment.

**The judgement.** The slot declares the type: a value of `ref<T>` names a value whose type is `T` or IS-A it, judged after following reference chains and compared by canonical identity — declaring schema and entry — each type named in the namespace of the meta governing the schema that declares it. The referent is **found first and judged after**, never chosen among candidates, and its type is judged before its value, so a name wrong by type is refused for its type whatever the value holds. A **type entry admits no further segment**: projecting into a record type's fields (`order.sku`) is a different capability and is not this one. An open entry is no referent, and is refused at a kind-typed slot on the same terms although a kind lists a record template (§4.2): `request: box` over `box => <T> { v: T }` is refused, a template denoting no value until applied, and what a document names there is the application a declaration gives it, `bt => box<int32>` then `request: bt`. A framed name (`@doc:"x" orders`, `!record orders`) is refused: a name's type is its referent's, and an annotation on it would have nowhere to go. A quoted token is text, never a name. Each refusal names its own step:

| Written | Result |
|---|---|
| `method: orders.place_order` | accepted: `orders` is an `interface` entry, `place_order` a key of its map, the value a `method` |
| `method: orders` | refused: an `interface` value, where a `method` is wanted |
| `method: orders.cancel_order` | refused: `orders` has no key `cancel_order`; the keys are listed |
| `method: orders.place_order.nope` | refused: a `method` value has no field `nope` |
| `method: place_order` | refused: no entry `place_order`; a member is reached through its entry |
| `method: place_order_request` | refused: a `record` type, where a `method` value is wanted |
| `method: hs.h.headers.accept`, `headers: {text => method}` | refused: a map that is no namespace is not entered |
| `method: shop.reads.fallback`, `service.fallback?: method` absent in the value under `reads` | refused: a field the value does not state names nothing, as a key the map lacks does |
| `response: orders` | refused: a namespace entry is a value, where a type is expected (§4.1) |
| `request: { … }` at `request?: kind<record>` | refused: a kind-typed slot of a value body holds one bare name (§5.6) |
| `r: ref<record>` | refused at the declaration: `of` names a type, never a constructor; `kind<record>` is the spelling for the names of the record types |

**A field of a record value reached past a key** selects in that value as a key does in a map: the name reaches what the field holds, and is judged by the field's declared type. `shop.orders.methods.place_order`, where `service.methods` is typed by `interface`, reaches the method the interface value holds under that key.

**Where the rule stands.** A value by name stands at a slot typed `ref<T>` and nowhere else: a slot typed by a record holds the record's value inline (`method: { response: order }` is plain data of `method`), and a bare token at a record-typed slot in a schema document fills the record's positional field where that field is atom-typed, and is refused as no record otherwise (§5.6). A reference is kernel vocabulary, declared only in a kernel-governed schema (§4.2): a schema that governs data declares only types, so a reference there would name nothing. **The rule does not reach a data document.** Its store is a schema's namespace, and a data document has none, so a bare token at a record position in a data document fills the positional field whatever its type (§5.6), exactly as before, and a host component bound to a reference there receives a name, unresolved. An imported entry's names are judged once, when its own schema links, in its own namespace. Resolved output writes a name **as written** (§8.1): the referent is judged at link and delivered to a host with the name, and the two readings a later revision might give a name — substitution, where the value replaces the name and nothing downstream can tell the spellings apart, and identity, where the name's referent is the value's identity and what a coverage check reads — both stay available. Neither is decided here: this section is the lookup, the judgement and the grammar, which both share.

**What it is, against what it replaces.** `type_ref`, `type_name`, a kind's value and `ref<T>`'s are one thing — a reference that must resolve — and differ by slot only in the judgement: at a kind-typed slot the name is one segment and must resolve to a member of the kind; at a `type_ref` slot it is one segment with arguments and must resolve to a type; at a `ref<T>` slot it may carry segments and must resolve to a value IS-A `T`. `type_ref` is, in this light, a reference to a type with one segment, because types live only in the schema map; `ref<method>` takes segments because methods live in collections. The dotted-names family this spelling belongs to is the **qualified name** — Java's, XML's QName, Python's dotted name, protobuf's fully-qualified name, TOML's dotted keys — where each segment is a name and nothing is a position; the address family (RFC 6901 JSON Pointer, RFC 9535 JSONPath) answers *which position* and is the one the series' diagnostics already use.


## 6. Annotations as Types

[TSON-DATA] §3.1 defines annotation syntax and preservation. This section defines annotation semantics: an annotation is a typed metadata attachment, resolved and validated against a type reachable through the schema chain.

Annotation values are always data values — concrete values, not type definitions — both in data values and within the type-definition grammar. Placement follows [TSON-DATA] §3.1; the resolver preserves annotations in their authored positions. The type-definition grammar adds one position: in `field-def` (§12.1), annotations precede the field name and annotate the field itself, mapping to the `record_field` in resolver output.

In schema declarations, an annotation may stand at two positions with two meanings, and the resolver does not hoist between them. An annotation immediately preceding the declared name binds to the name — the schema-map key — and is **metadata about the declaration**: documentation of the entry, provenance, lifecycle (`@deprecated name => …`), and the resolver's own derived markers (`@synthetic` and `@application`, §8.1, §8.2) live here. An annotation after `=>` binds to the entry's value and is metadata *inside* the definition. Both positions are preserved in resolver output at their authored places (§8.1): a key annotation on the output schema-map key, a value annotation on the definition value. A **checked** annotation (below) is an assertion in either position and is honoured at both: a processor MUST consult both spellings, and MUST NOT ignore one with no diagnostic.

**Annotations are types.** An annotation `@T` (or `@T:value`) names a type `T` and attaches it as metadata to the surrounding value. Resolution is one-hop against the governing target's namespace (§3.3.3): the `!!meta` target for a schema document, the `!!schema` target for a data document. `!!import` entries and local entries of the document being authored are NOT part of the annotation namespace. The value is validated against `T`'s contract:

- For `void`-targeted `T` (a type whose resolved body, after following its reference chain, is `void` — such as `annotation` or `numeric`), the annotation form is `@T` with no colon and no value. Bare `@T` is shorthand for `@T:_`; the resolver fills the implicit `_` and validates against `void`'s contract (§4.2) — presence is the information.
- For any non-`void` `T`, the form is `@T:value`, where `value` is a single data-value conforming to `T`: `@doc:"User's full name"`, `@ordering:TOTAL`, `@title:"Order"`.

Any type in the governing target's namespace can be used as an annotation — there is no separate annotation namespace. **An annotation whose name does not resolve in that namespace is a resolver error**, for the valueless form as much as the valued one: `@doc` in a schema whose meta does not supply `doc` fails exactly as `@doc:"..."` does — the bare form is shorthand for `@T:_`, and `_` still needs a `void`-targeted `T` to validate against. Annotations are not free-form markers under a governing schema; the preserved-uninterpreted treatment belongs to schemaless processing alone ([TSON-DATA] §3.1). The one exception is structural: the meta-kernel's own bootstrap self-reference (`annotation => @annotation void`), resolvable because the kernel's governing target is itself and the kernel is pre-loaded before any document is parsed. An annotation typed by an abstract entry — `top`, `type` or a base kind (`@top`, `@type`, `@sum`, §4.1), or any other abstract record, a meta layer's own included — is a schema error in either position, a value at an abstract position naming no subtype.

The one-hop rule is what allows that self-reference to work; it also fixes where annotation types must live (§3.3.3).

**What an annotation may do.** An annotation never changes a value, its type, or whether it is valid: validation is over values, and a schema with every annotation erased admits exactly the same values. That is the criterion for the home of a fact — *an annotation is the right home exactly when the mark changes no value's validity* — and it is what keeps an encoding's projection concern out of every processor's type model. Within it an annotation may do two further things; the series has one of the first kind and none of the second:

- It may carry **load-time force**: a *checked* annotation is verified against a fact the resolver derives, with two outcomes and no third — verified, or a resolver error at schema load — and the section defining it states the check. `@disjoint` (§5.4) is checked against derived disjointness, and is the category's one member.
- It may be a **representation directive**: it directs how a *class* of encodings represents a value, and force is confined to the encodings that claim it — in every other encoding, and in the model, it changes nothing. A directive's **class** is named, not inferred: it is the set of encodings whose encoding-rules documents declare, by the directive's name, that they consume it, and each such document MUST say so where it states its rules. A class is never defined by the behaviour the directive directs — "the encodings that flatten" names no class — and no encoding is privileged, TSON text being one encoding beside [TSON-JSON]. An encoding-rules document MAY bind projection behaviour to a schema-side annotation declared for it on these terms; no such annotation is declared in this revision, and the category stands empty.

**Two facts that are not annotations, and why.** How a record may be realised (`abstract`, `final`) and which fields its members are selected by (`=?`) each change which values conform — erase the first and a direct instance becomes readable at every position typed by the record; erase the second and every untagged document at a member-dispatched position stops validating, in every encoding, so there is no other encoding for the mark to change nothing in. Both are therefore §12.1 syntax lowering into the kernel's own `record.extension` and `record.discriminators` (§5.2), never annotations: the resolver consumes them into the body, they appear in no output annotation channel, and their names are reserved at exactly one position — a definition mark between `=>` and the type definition — and are ordinary identifiers everywhere else ([TSON-DATA] §7.7). `meta.tn` declares neither, so `@abstract`, `@final`, `@sealed` or `@discriminator` written as an annotation is the ordinary unresolved-name error above. An earlier revision spelled a discriminator as an annotation on a *choice*, where the mechanism it names cannot reach, and argued that it changed no value's validity by counting variants rather than values; that claim is withdrawn with the spelling.

Everything else an annotation can be is *advisory*: `@doc`, `@title`, `@comment`, `@examples`, the void presence markers `@deprecated`, `@read_only` and `@write_only` (a bare mark is the whole statement — absence is the default, so there is no `false` to write — and `@read_only` with `@write_only` on one field is a schema-load error), and the `@annotation` marker itself, which signals "intended as annotation metadata" so tools can surface available annotations, carries no runtime force, and leaves a type without it no less usable as an annotation. Each has a stated reading:

- **`@doc`'s text is CommonMark** (version 0.31.2), with no extensions. CommonMark is versioned where "markdown" has no specification, so two renderers agree on what a doc says, and a doc that uses one vendor's tables, task lists or autolinks reads as their literal text. Raw HTML in a doc is never executed, and a renderer need not render it: a doc is written by whoever published the schema, and rendering one fetched from elsewhere must not become a way to run its author's code. The rule refuses nothing — every string is valid CommonMark, so no schema stops loading and no processor checks anything; it says how the text is displayed, which is all an advisory annotation may do.
- **`@title`** is plain text, being a name.
- **`@comment`** is a note for a schema's maintainers rather than its readers (JSON Schema's `$comment`): where `@doc` is what a documentation tool shows, `@comment` is what it leaves out. Its format is left unstated.
- **`@examples`** is a list of *text*, each an example as a reader is shown it — conventionally the value written in TSON notation, so a record's example is a string such as `"{ x: 1 }"`. Nothing parses an example or checks it against the type it illustrates: an annotation's position hands its value no type to be read by, and a check would need an annotation value typed by its target, which this section does not have.
- **`@deprecated`** is a yes-or-no fact; a reason, where there is one, is `@doc`'s to give.

**Resolver-attached annotations.** Two annotations are attached by the resolver rather than written by the author, and an author's spelling of either is a resolver error. Every synthetic entry's key in resolver output carries the bare marker `@synthetic` (§8.1, §8.2), so tooling can fold materialised entries back into nested display; it is **derived**, and on ingest it is discarded and recomputed (§8.1) — it carries no decode force and cannot be forged into a resolved document to change its meaning. An entry that closes a template application carries `@application:{ name: box  arguments: [{ name: int32 }] }` on its key — the application the entry is the instantiation of (§8.2), a fact about the entry and not about a type, so an entry of any kind can carry it: a record template's declared member, a namespace entry closed from a templated instance, the alias a template's application closes to, a self-reaching application's instantiation entry. It is derived, as `@synthetic` is, and never written by a declaration; unlike `@synthetic` it is **read as stated** on ingest and not recomputed, being the one fact the entry's value does not state, and what lets an imported schema's instantiations unify with a later schema's (§8.1, §10.1). Both are defined in the meta-kernel and follow the same resolution rules as user-written annotations. No annotation records a reference: a use site names what the author named, and the chain is stated as written (§8.3).


## 7. Text Encoding Rules: Data Values Under a Schema

This section is the text format's **encoding rules**: how the type system's values are carried in TSON text. Every encoding of the type system owns a section shaped like this one — its atom lexical forms, its container syntax, its treatment of void, and its discrimination predicate over the derived disjointness fact (§5.4). This section states TSON text's rules; every other encoding states its own against the same encoding-independent model in its own encoding-rules document — [TSON-JSON] is the first, and the two agree on every rule the model states, differing in spelling alone.

[TSON-DATA] defines the syntax of data values and their schemaless interpretation. This section defines the `!!schema` directive (§7.1) and how an active schema changes interpretation: type-annotation resolution (§7.2), atom parsing in place of base type resolution (§7.3, §7.4), sets (§7.5), the void sentinel (§7.6), resolver behaviours at typed positions (§7.7), and cross-schema references (§7.8).


### 7.1 The `!!schema` Directive

`!!schema` identifies the schema whose types are available for `!name` references in the value it governs. The directive value is an IRI-reference identifying a published schema.

```
!!schema:"https://example.com/people.tn?sha256=c4d5e6f7a8b9c0d1e2f3a4b5c6d7e8f9a0b1c2d3e4f5a6b7c8d9e0f1a2b3c4d5"
!person { name: Alice age: 30 }
```

**The referent is a schema document.** A `!!schema` URL resolves to a schema document — never to a resolved-schema data document, whose resolver-derived fields cannot be verified from the document alone. Resolved-form documents enter the schema library only through the explicit ingest path (§8.1, §10.1).

On a data document's header, `!!schema` binds the schema for the entire document. On a scoped value — a record field value, map entry value, or array element ([TSON-DATA] §2.3) — it binds the schema for that value alone: the referenced schema becomes the active scope for all `!name` annotations within that value and its descendants, reverting to the enclosing scope when the value ends. Directives remain excluded before map keys, field names, and annotation values ([TSON-DATA] §3.3); §7.8 defines the typed-position rules.

**The directive names a namespace, not a root contract.** `!!schema` supplies the vocabulary against which `!name` annotations resolve; it does not assert the governed value's type, and a schema has no privileged entry point. The value names its own type by annotation, and **under `!!schema` the root MUST name its type**: an unannotated root under a bound schema is a validation error in every mode, since under a schema every value is typed by its position or by its tag (§7.3) and a root has no position to be typed by. There is no "vocabulary-only" reading of a governed document — a value read by base type resolution is a schemaless value, and a document is one thing or the other from its header. The same holds at every position whose type is a `scoped` instance: a value there names its type or is invalid (§7.8).

A scoped `!!schema` at a position whose type is constrained by the outer schema is admitted exactly when that type resolves to a `scoped` instance whose `scope` holds `EXTERN`, or a container of one. At a `scoped` position whose `scope` does not hold `EXTERN` it is a validation error, by the cell rule; at a position whose type is not a `scoped` instance at all it is a resolver error — see §7.8, where both are derived rather than listed.

Schema documents do not carry `!!schema`: a schema's governing contract is declared by `!!meta` (§2.2.2). A document with no `!!schema` directive has no type vocabulary: base type resolution ([TSON-DATA] §4) applies, type annotations are limited to the built-in vocabulary of [TSON-DATA] §5, and any other type annotation is a resolver error ([TSON-DATA] §3.2). The built-in names denote the types their libraries declare — core's for most, `net.tn`'s for the network rows — so a schema that wants them imports the library that declares them (§9), and a schemaless `!mac` keeps its meaning when its document moves under a schema that imports `net.tn`. Such a document opens no schema scope of its own (§7.8).


### 7.2 Type Annotation Resolution

In data values, a type annotation (`!name`) marks **instantiation** — the value is concrete data conforming to the named type. The name resolves against the external schema identified by the current `!!schema` directive — never against definitions within the same document (§3).

**The built-in vocabulary does not apply in schema scope.** When a schema is in scope, all type annotations MUST resolve through the schema's type-name namespace; a built-in annotation name not defined by the active schema is an unresolved-type error. Schemas wanting `uuid`, `bytes`, `datetime`, and the other built-in names import the library that declares them — core, or `net.tn` for the network names ([TSON-DATA] §5.5) — or define them locally. This is the normative statement of the scoping rule; [TSON-DATA] §5.1 restates it for schemaless processors. A document with no `!!schema` falls back to schemaless processing (§7.1).

**Records are closed under their type, with no exception.** When a schema is in scope and a record's type is known, the record MUST contain only fields defined by its type; fields not present in the type definition are validation errors — a field name that is no value of the record's name type among them, since every declared name is one (§5.2), the diagnostic under a declared name type naming the type and its reason. A document's field names are matched against the declared ones in the name type's form (§5.5, §7.7), and the record that reads a value judges its names: a record nested in one with a relaxed name type judges its own. This applies to directly-typed records (`!person { ... }`) and structurally-positioned records (a record at a record-typed field position). Nothing in a schema makes a record absorb fields its type does not declare: open-ended data is carried by a declared map-typed field (`extras: {text => text}`), whose entries are keys and not names ([TSON-DATA] §2.5, §2.6) and are written the same way in every encoding. Schemaless records have no closure rule. Closure is what makes schema evolution a discrete operation: every published schema version is a precise, immutable contract about what fields exist (§3.5).

**Type expression syntax is not available in data values** ([TSON-DATA] §3.2). To annotate an array or map value with a named type, declare a named type in the schema (`int_list => [integer]`) and write `!int_list [1 2 4 8 32]`.

**Validation follows what the name is.** `!T value` asserts that the value conforms to `T`, and conformance is determined by `T`'s own definition. An **atom instance** validates a single token by its parsing contract (§7.4): `!age 42`, never `!age { ... }`. A **product** validates a record against its field list; a **choice** validates any conforming variant. A **constructor** is a record-shaped type, so it validates a record against its constraint-field vocabulary: `!integer_type { min: 0  max: 255 }` is a record conforming to `integer_type`'s fields, receiving ordinary record validation; family coherence between bindings (e.g. `min ≤ max`) is a compilation and ingest concern (§8), not data validation.

**Subsumption at typed positions.** At a position whose declared type is `T`, a value annotated `!S` is valid if and only if, after following both reference chains to their terminal entries (§8.3), `S` is `T` or `T` appears in `S`'s `chain.supertypes` (§4.1, §8.1). A subtracted type has no supertypes and does not stand where its source is expected. An admitted value validates against `S` in full: closure, constraints, and defaults are `S`'s; the position's `T` contributed admission alone. **What an unannotated value at such a position means is `T`'s own extension fact** (§5.2), and there are three readings. Where `T` is OPEN or FINAL, the value is exactly `T`: there is no structural recovery of `S`, per the once-only reading of form ([TSON-DATA] §2.4), so the annotation carries information precisely when `S` is a proper subtype — a field typed `person` admits `!employee` where the schema declares `employee => person & { ... }`, and without the annotation the value is a `person`, full stop. Where `T` is ABSTRACT and names no discriminators, `T` has no direct instances, so the tag is REQUIRED: an untagged value is a validation error naming `T`, whose diagnostic SHOULD name the subtypes, and a tag naming `T` itself is refused, no value satisfying it. Where `T` is ABSTRACT and names discriminators, the value is placed by **reading the marked fields** at the types `T` declares them — the one set of types known before dispatch — and matching the value, or the tuple of values in declaration order, against the pins its members state; the matched member becomes the effective type and the whole value validates against it, which re-verifies each pin as an ordinary FIXED check and makes the dispatch read and the validation read agree by construction. A missing selector, or a value no member pins, is a validation error, the latter SHOULD naming the pinned alternatives; the tag is then optional and MUST name the dispatched member or a subtype of it where written — one selector per position, and the FIXED check is what forces the two to agree. This notation reads a member-dispatched position on those terms and so does every other encoding ([TSON-JSON] §6.1.5); the two differ in spelling alone, since the selectors are the model's facts and not any encoding's. A record's fields have no significant order in this notation, so a reader locates the selectors by lookahead over the record; an encoding may require them to lead. Choice-typed positions discriminate by variant membership (§5.4) and scoped positions by the namespaces the instance admits (§7.8) — the same surface gesture, an annotation selecting a more specific type than the position promises, under their own membership relations; this rule governs every other typed position. It is also what admits resolver-output entries at the `top`-typed values of `schema`: every entry's annotation head carries `top` in its chain — constructors through `type` and their base kinds, a namespace type through `namespace`, `reference` and `template` through their direct composition with `top` (§4.1, §8.1, §9) — and `top` being abstract, dispatch is by tag among its subtypes.

**There is no construction in data.** `!C { bindings }` produces a new type only in the schema grammar (§5.5); the same surface shape in a data document is a record that *describes* constraints — which is precisely what resolver output stores as an entry's value (§8). The two category errors are symmetric, in data as in schema: a constructor never types its family's atom values (`!integer_type 42` is a type error — the value type is the instance, `!integer 42`), and an instance never types records (`!age { min: 0 }` is a type error — the constraint vocabulary belongs to the constructor).

**No parameterized annotations.** Resolver-output bodies (`!array { element_type: person }`, `!map { key_type: … }`, §8.1) are annotations by ordinary parameterless constructors (§4.2), validated by ordinary record validation: each field against its declared type, a type slot against `type_kind` — a bare name judged a member when the governing schema links (§4.2). No special rule remains — a template with any parameter is a resolver error as a data annotation, without exception (§5.10).

**Schema values.** The type annotation `!schema` marks a map as a schema value — a regular type annotation; `schema` is defined in the meta-kernel as the namespace `namespace & { key_type: = type_name  value_type: = top }` (§4.1), and a value of a namespace type is read as the map it describes. It appears on data documents that carry resolved schema structure, most notably resolver output (§8); schema-document source never carries it (§2.1).


### 7.3 Atom Parsing Replaces Base Type Resolution

When a schema is in scope, base type resolution ([TSON-DATA] §4) does not apply at all: every value is typed by its position or by its tag, each declared atom type owns its own parsing contract (§7.4), and there is no third way to read a token. The tokens `true` and `false` have no special status when a schema is in scope — their meaning is determined entirely by the position's type, and at core's `boolean` they are enum members like any other (§7.4). There is no `null` in the notation ([TSON-DATA] §4.4): the token `null` at a `text` position is the string `null`, at an `int32` position a parse error, and at a `void`-typed position — any instance of `void_type`: the kernel's `void`, core's sibling of it (§3.3.5, §9), or one a schema constructs for itself (§4.2) — a validation error, since `void` admits the void sentinel `_` and nothing else. A JSON document's `null` reaches the model as void through the JSON reader ([TSON-DATA] §6), where the position decides whether a void value is admitted (§7.6); no keyword in this notation is needed to carry it.


### 7.4 Atom Token Parsing

Each atom type owns its parsing contract. When a token appears at a position whose type is an atom, the atom's parser takes the token and produces either a typed host value or a parse error; the atom's constraint record is applied as validation after parsing succeeds.

**Parsing and validation are distinct.** Parsing takes a token to a host value; validation checks the host value against the constraint record. `twelve` at an `integer`-typed field is a parse error; `300` at a field typed `age` (refining `integer` with `{min: 0 max: 150}`) parses as an integer, then fails validation. Implementations SHOULD distinguish these in error reporting ([TSON-DATA] §8.1).

**Enum member semantics.** An enum is a closed set of **names**, and the identifier family they are drawn from — the same two facts a record states of its field names through `record.name_type` (§5.2), under the same name. The kernel's constructor states both, and one tightening of it pins the family for the common case:

```
enum_type => atom & {
  name_type: identifier_kind
  members:   enum_set
}
enum      => enum_type ^ { name_type?: = identifier }
```

`!enum [OPEN ACTIVE DONE]` is a *vocabulary*: its members are names, written unquoted, and a named scope for name hygiene (§11.4). It is the positional form (§5.6) — `name_type` is pinned and injected, so `members` is the one unmarked field. `!enum_type { name_type: kebab  members: [make-tea drink-tea] }` is the general form, and an ordinary schema may write it: `name_type` is data, so a schema enumerates names of a naming vocabulary it declares itself, with no new constructor, which only a meta layer may declare (§2.2.2); `!enum_type { name_type: words  members: ["lightly active" "sedentary"] }` over `words => !identifier_type { medial: " " }` enumerates names with spaces in them, with full hygiene, `medial` placing a character only between two others and never at an edge or doubled. `members` is typed by the kernel's `enum_set` — `!set_type { element_type: text  min_items: 1 }` — so members are held as text whatever `name_type` is, every enum has `enum_type`'s shape, and one reader and one host class serve them all: a processor binds a constructor that tightens another as the one it tightens. **A closed set of text that is no name is no enum**: it is the text family's own `members` facet, `!text ^ { members: ["80" "443"] }` (below), string-class as every text atom is. An earlier revision's `text_enum` is withdrawn, its one use being that facet.

**`name_type` names an identifier family** — typed `identifier_kind` (§4.2), the kind of the types built with `identifier_type`, so a value naming any other type is refused as no member of the kind, at the declaration. Everything that distinguishes one enum from another is a property of that type, not of `enum`:

| | derived from `name_type` |
|---|---|
| members | each MUST be a value of the type, by the family's own parsing and facets — so a name under its profile — and no two may be one value under its equality; a member the type refuses is a schema-load error ("is not an identifier" under the default, "is not a value of its name_type 'words'" under an author's) |
| hygiene | §11.4's scope and all three of [TSON-DATA] §8.2's mechanisms, under the type's own profile, whose added characters are its own |
| discrimination class (§5.4) | the members' shared class, read off each member's own token |
| binding | a host enum by a mapping the binder owns (below) |

The first row is a pair of rules and not structure: members are held as `text` and are unique as text by the set's own contract, and a type whose equality is coarser than text — one that folds case (§5.5) — makes two of them one name, which is refused. The class row stays with the enum rather than following the type: it reads the members' tokens (`boolean => !enum [true false]` is boolean-class, where the type `identifier` is string-class). **A name type's `normalization` implies NFC**, on an enum as on a record (§5.2): `NONE` is refused at load, and `ASCII_CASEFOLD` is admitted only for a type whose every character is ASCII, `NFKC_CASEFOLD` being the fold for any other, since a document's name is matched against the members in the type's form and no reader could match one under a form the type never stated.

**Where a `name_type` resolves.** A `name_type` an author writes names a type in the author's own namespace. A value a constructor pinned was written in the governing meta and resolves **there**: `!enum [A B]` names the kernel's `identifier` in a schema that imports no core, however the enum is reached — directly, or through a template's held body — and an `identifier` the schema declares for itself is a different type that does not displace it. This is §2.2.3's rule that a merged entry's own references stay in its defining schema's namespace, stated for a pinned value; the same holds of a record's `name_type` (§5.2). **`name_type` is fixed at construction** (§5.7): a refinement narrows the member set and never changes the type, since narrowing it would re-judge members already admitted and widening it would admit names the source's type refuses. A `~`/`=` value at the slot is judged a member of `identifier_kind` where it is written, so a constructor pinning a non-member fails at its own meta layer's link rather than at the first schema applying it; a name type written as a template application is admitted as the instance it expands to.

**Numbers are never enums.** An enum's members are names, equal only to themselves under their type's equality; an integer's equality is a value equality (`80` and `0x50` are one value, [TSON-DATA] §4.3), and a numeric value set is the numeric tier's own facet — a contiguous range is `!integer ^ { min: 1  max: 3 }`, an arithmetic progression is `multiple_of`, a **sparse set is `members`** (`!integer ^ { members: [80 443 8080] }`, the `integer_member_set`-typed facet `integer_type` carries and the `decimal_member_set`-typed one `decimal_type` carries, §9) — and a choice is for alternatives that are not one family. A mixed source enum (`["AUTO", "MANUAL", 80, 443]`) becomes a choice over an identifier enum and a member-set integer, which is disjoint by class and tag-free. The split, in one sentence each: *a vocabulary of names* is `!enum [OPEN ACTIVE DONE]`, or an `enum_type` over the author's own identifier family where the names need a wider profile; *a value set on a type needed for its other facets* is that family's `members` — `!text ^ { length: 2  members: ["AU" "NZ"] }`, `!int16 ^ { members: [80 443] }` — where the family's own parsing still applies. What an enum has that a member set has not is an unquoted spelling, a class of its own, name hygiene, and name equality in place of value equality.

An enum MUST have at least one member (`!enum []` fails `enum_set`'s `min_items` at schema load), and its members are unique (`!enum [OPEN OPEN]` is a schema-load error). There is no carve-out for `true` and `false`: the kernel defines `boolean` as `!enum [true false]`, an ordinary enum of names whose class is read off its members' tokens by [TSON-DATA] §4, so `boolean` stays boolean-class and `[INDEX NAMED]` string-class. Parsing at an enum-typed position puts the token's decoded text into the label type's normalization form (§5.5) and matches it against the members — a typed position never consults the form, so `"true"` and `true` are one value at `boolean` (as [TSON-DATA] §5.5's `!boolean` states for the schemaless case). **How an enum binds:** to the host type of the natural parse of its members — a host boolean at `boolean`, host text everywhere else — with a host-language enum reached through a mapping the binder owns. Under an identifier family the members are names; that they are *host-safe* names is true of no host — [TSON-DATA] §7.7 admits `in-progress`, and the kernel's own `boolean` has `true` and `false` for members — so binding is a mapping in every case (`in-progress` to `IN_PROGRESS`, `"new york"` to `NEW_YORK`), and the binder reports one that collides or yields no legal constant. A host language is what a schema maps *to*, never a constraint on it. `members` describes the permitted values, not the resolved representation.

**Text member sets.** `text_type` carries `members?: text_member_set` beside `min_length`, `max_length`, `length`, `pattern` and `normalization`, and `identifier_type`, `uri_type`, `iri_type`, `regex_type` and `email_type` inherit it through the composition they already use (§9): a value set on a constrained text family, where the family's own parsing contract still applies — `!uri ^ { members: ["https://a.example/" "https://b.example/"] }` is two admitted URIs that are still parsed as URIs, exactly as `!int16 ^ { members: [80 443] }` is a sixteen-bit integer that happens to be sparse. It is a set, not an array, for three reasons — uniqueness comes from the set's own contract, non-emptiness from its `min_items`, and member identity is the value's, so two spellings of one member are a duplicate rather than two — and a plain field beside `pattern` rather than a group excluding it, because a pattern is two things nothing can tell apart — `a|b|c` is a value set and beside `members` is the same statement twice, `[A-Z]{2}` is a shape and beside `members` composes as a numeric range does. A member is a value: each is put into the type's normalization form (§5.5), and two that are one value there are a duplicate and are refused, the set's own uniqueness having compared only the text as written. **The pattern MUST admit every member**: meta's uniform `members` rule, applied, and the one member-coherence check in the series that needs a regex match rather than a comparison. `pattern` and `members` are each settable once under refinement (§5.7).

**Identifier families.** `identifier_type` is the constraint vocabulary of names: a UAX #31 identifier profile, stated as data, with the text facets applied inside it.

```
identifier_type => text_type & atom_specification & {
  spec?:          = "https://www.unicode.org/reports/tr31/"
  start?:         identifier_base ~ XID
  continue?:      identifier_base ~ XID
  start_add?:     text
  continue_add?:  text
  medial?:        text
  exclude?:       text
  normalization?: normalization ~ NFC
}
identifier => !identifier_type { continue_add: "-" }
```

The profile is UAX #31's R1 default identifier syntax — `Start Continue* (Medial Continue+)*`, with `Start = (start ∪ start_add) − exclude`, `Continue = (continue ∪ continue_add) − exclude` and `Medial = medial` — where `start` and `continue` name the base each set is drawn from (`identifier_base => !enum [XID ID ASCII NONE]`: `XID_Start`/`XID_Continue`, `ID_Start`/`ID_Continue`, XID's ASCII members — Start `A`–`Z` `a`–`z`, Continue adding `0`–`9` and `_` — or the empty set) and each of the four text facets is read as the set of code points it holds. A medial character stands only between two others and never beside another medial. **A floor no profile lifts:** an `identifier_type` MUST NOT add, in `start_add`, `continue_add` or `medial`, a character that renders as nothing or as another character — a `Default_Ignorable_Code_Point` (so every `Bidi_Control`), a control, or a space other than U+0020 — and a profile that does fails to load ([TSON-DATA] §8.2): an added character meets no restricted-character rule, so without the floor a soft hyphen in `continue_add` would make two names no rule tells apart. U+0020 stays admissible, a medial space being how a name type holds words. Where a type is a **name type** — a record's or an enum's (§5.2) — its `normalization` MUST imply NFC: `NONE` is refused, and `ASCII_CASEFOLD` is admitted only over a profile whose every character is ASCII. The profile judges the *value* — the text put into the type's `normalization` form, `NFC` unless the instance says otherwise (§5.5) — so a quoted decomposed spelling at an identifier-typed position reads as its composed value and is not refused for having been written decomposed, and a profile that folds case lists lowercase letters only and admits `Content-Type`. A join control (U+200C, U+200D) is admitted only in the contexts UTS #39 §3.1.1.1 permits, whatever the profile ([TSON-DATA] §7.7, rule 2). Because the family composes `text_type`, it takes lengths, a `pattern` — a naming convention such as snake_case, inside the profile — and `members`, a closed vocabulary of names; the member-coherence rule below counts the profile among the facets a member must satisfy, so `members: [north "2nd"]` under `identifier`'s profile fails to load.

`identifier` is [TSON-DATA] §7.7's grammar exactly: `XID_Start`-initial, `XID_Continue ∪ { - }` thereafter, in NFC. Two identifiers are equal iff their NFC texts are byte-identical; identity is case-sensitive. It is the type of every naming position in the series — `type_name`, `field_name` and `param_name` alias it, `enum` pins it as its name type, and a record whose chain states no other has `field_name` as its (§5.2) — so a rule stated on it reaches all of them, and because every identifier lies inside the unquoted-token profile ([TSON-DATA] §7.1), the positions that admit no quoted form (`!` targets, `@` names, and every schema-grammar name) lose nothing. A lexer and a schema parser hold that profile directly, the kernel's own names being read before the kernel exists, and a kernel whose `identifier` states any other is refused at bootstrap (§3.4). What follows from the family being a type:

- **A value typed by an identifier family is a name.** [TSON-DATA] §8.2's per-name mechanisms reach it under the identifier policy, at a map key and at a field's value alike, and the keys of a map keyed by one and the elements of a set of one are look-alike scopes (§11.4). The line between a name and data runs through the type, and an earlier revision's statement that `identifier` is not used in data values is reversed.
- **An ordinary schema declares its own.** `identifier => !identifier_type { continue_add: "-" }` is one line, after which a schema writes `{identifier => handler}` and refines `!identifier ^ { … }`. Core declares no sibling and leaves the name free (§9).
- **Each profile is its own type.** Every `!identifier_type { … }` is a distinct type entity: IS-A between two identifier types comes from refinement alone, never from one profile admitting a subset of another's names. All are string-class (§5.4).
- **The profile facets and `normalization` are fixed where the profile is constructed** (§5.7): a refinement narrows only the text facets, and a different profile is a fresh `!identifier_type`.
- **Only `identifier`'s profile is known to lie inside the unquoted-token profile.** A value under another profile may need quoting, which costs nothing: the positions that admit no quoted form are typed by the kernel's roles, which stay on `identifier`, and a field name or enum member under another profile is written quoted where it must be (`"@id"`, `"lightly active"`).
- **A profile's own additions are exempt from the `Identifier_Status` rule**, and a per-segment restriction level divides a name at its profile's own separators ([TSON-DATA] §8.2).
- **A record's field names and an enum's members are values of an identifier family**, their `name_type` (§5.2), so a schema translated from a JSON vocabulary declares the profile its names need — `json_name => !identifier_type { start_add: "@$" }` — and judges `@context` and `$ref` as names under it, with full hygiene.

The kernel's second instance is `scheme_name`, a URI scheme (RFC 3986 §3.1) — a letter, then letters, digits, `+`, `-` and `.`, compared without case, written over the `ASCII` base: `!identifier_type { start: ASCII  continue: ASCII  continue_add: "+-."  exclude: "_"  normalization: ASCII_CASEFOLD }`. The `ASCII` base is what lets an ASCII protocol's names be stated in one line — an HTTP field name is `!identifier_type { start: ASCII  continue: ASCII  medial: "-"  normalization: ASCII_CASEFOLD }` — where a `NONE` base would list the alphabet twice.

**Number-grammar reuse.** Atoms that parse numeric values SHOULD use the number grammar of [TSON-DATA] §7.6 for the relevant numeric form; a single shared number parser dispatching on the atom's declared form is the expected pattern.

**Constraint fields typed as `value`.** Some atom constructors declare constraint fields with type `value` because the constrained atom cannot be referenced at the point of declaration — `decimal_type.min` is typed `value` and cannot use `number`, which is a core instance of `decimal_type` (bootstrap ordering, §3.4). The kernel's `integer_type` is the exception that proves the rule: its bounds, its `multiple_of` and its `members` are typed `integer`, available in the kernel's own namespace, and every facet that counts something is typed by the kernel's `non_negative_integer` (§9). A token at a `value`-typed position is the token, uninterpreted (§4.2): **the resolver reads it under the atom it stands for**, once that atom is in scope, and stores the result. That reading is what makes a `value`-typed constraint comparable at all — `1` and `1.0` at `decimal_type.min` are one number, not an integer beside a float — and for a `value`-typed *set* it runs before the set is formed: each member of `decimal_type.members` is read under the constrained atom first, so `[1 1.0]` is a duplicate the set catches, a member that does not parse as a decimal fails at schema load, and §5.7's member-set tightening compares decimals rather than whatever host type a bare token would have become.

Each constrained atom's implementation converts `value`-typed constraint values to its internal representation. Conversion MUST occur at schema-load time, not per-validation; an atom that cannot convert a given constraint value MUST report a resolver error at schema-load time — a schema either loads cleanly or fails with a clear diagnostic, and a half-valid schema that silently mis-validates is never produced. Which constraint-value types an implementation accepts for conversion is an implementation choice; the validation semantics after conversion are the atom's contract.

**Coherence of a body's facets.** A body's facets MUST between them admit a value: the lower bound not exceeding the upper (across whichever inclusive or exclusive forms are present), a `multiple_of` strictly positive, a `precision` at most 9, a network family's `within`/`excluding` pair and prefix bounds admitting a value (§5.5), an identifier profile whose Start set is not empty and whose `medial` characters are disjoint from its Start and Continue sets, and — where `members` is present — **every member satisfying the body's other facets**, the range a family derives rather than stores included (`{ members: [443]  size: { bits: 8 } }` fails the way a stated bound outside the width does). Each is a resolver error at schema load; at materialisation the same rules run again over the operands that were parameters (§8.2).

**Lexical classes vs type names.** The categories of [TSON-DATA] §4 base type resolution (boolean, integer, float, string) are lexical classifications of tokens a schemaless read produces, not type names: the lexical class and the core type `number` are distinct namespaces that share a word. A schema cannot reference a lexical class, and base resolution never produces a declared type; when a schema is in scope, the schemaless dispatch does not apply (§7.3).


### 7.5 Sets

A **set** is an unordered collection of unique values. Sets share array syntax `[ ... ]` at the data level ([TSON-DATA] §2.7); set-ness is a schema property declared via the kernel's `set_type` constructor, most often through the `set<T>` template (§4.2, §5.3). Without a schema, every `[ ... ]` is an array.

**Duplicate handling.** When source data contains a value more than once at a set-typed position, the duplicate is a **validation error** at the repeated occurrence — a set-typed position asserts uniqueness, and silently dropping a member the author wrote would decode the document to something other than what it says (the same posture as duplicate map keys, [TSON-DATA] §2.6). Two values are duplicates if the element type's equality contract considers them equal, and for an **atom** that contract is the type's **value space** (§5.5): name identity for a set of `identifier`, value equality for a set of `integer` or `number` (`1` and `1.0` are one member), octet equality for a set of `bytes` whatever the spelling, instant equality for a set of `datetime`, and equality in the type's normalization form for a set of a text family (`Content-Type` and `content-type` are one member under a type that folds case). For an element type that is not an atom — a record, array, map or choice — two elements are duplicates if the processor's **host representation** of them is equal, and which pairs that relates is implementation-defined: a processor detects at least the textual identity of [TSON-DATA] §2.6 in a tree it builds itself, and where elements are bound to host types, those types' equality decides. The series can reduce to a value space only the atoms it implements on the host; past an atom, identity belongs to the host, and a schema that needs portable duplicate detection over a compound value keys it by an atom — a derived identifier or a canonical string — which is the only identity every encoding and every host can agree on.

**Element order.** Order is a facet a container states (§5.3): `ordered` says whether two values differing only in order are one value — `true` for an array unless it says otherwise, `false` for a set by `set_type`'s pin, and `false` for a map unless it says otherwise. It never changes what a document may write. A set is thus unordered as a *value*: uniqueness and set equality are what the type states, and element order carries no meaning at a set-typed position. **Output keeps the order written either way.** In resolved output a set's elements, like an unordered map's entries, appear in source declaration order — for `enum_type.members`, `integer_type.members`, `decimal_type.members`, `text_type.members`, `uri_type.schemes` and `scoped.scope` alike — so that two resolved documents are compared as §8 writes them, with no table of unordered positions to consult and no canonicalisation step; a reader keeps the author's order, which an ordinal-assigning encoding also needs. A refinement that shrinks a member set keeps the surviving members in their inherited order.

These rules apply uniformly to every set-typed position: the kernel's `enum_type.members`, the exact tiers' `members`, `scoped.scope`, user-defined set-typed fields, and set values produced by the positional fill rule (§5.6). A void element at a set-typed position is rejected — `set_type` pins `voidable: = false` (§5.3) — and a set may be empty unless its type states `min_items: 1`, as `set<T>` does not and every member set of the kernel and meta does.


### 7.6 The Void Sentinel Under a Schema

[TSON-DATA] §2.9 defines the void sentinel and its data-value positions. `_` is not itself a type; the type whose sole conforming value is `_` is `void` (§4.2). It is the one spelling of void: the token `null` is a string, or a parse error, or a validation error, by the position's type (§7.3).

This document extends the position rules of [TSON-DATA] §2.9:

| Position                                  | `_` permitted? | Meaning                                                                                          |
|-------------------------------------------|----------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Array element (schema in scope)           | conditional    | Permitted only when the array type is `voidable`, written `[T?]` (§5.3)                           |
| Tuple element (schema in scope)           | conditional    | Permitted only when the position is `voidable` (§5.3); the void element occupies its slot        |
| Record field value (schema in scope)      | conditional    | Permitted when the field is **voidable** — its type carries `?` (§5.2) — whatever its name's mark; the field is then present with a void value. At a field that is not voidable, a validation error, whatever the modifier — at a defaulted field the fix is omission, which injects the default (§5.2). At a voidable group member, `_` is present and chooses its option (§5.11) |
| Map key (schema in scope)                 | no             | Resolver error — restates [TSON-DATA] §2.9's resolver-layer rule; keys define membership and are always required |
| Map entry value (schema in scope)         | conditional    | Permitted only when the map type is `voidable`, written `{K => V?}` (§5.3); the entry is then present with a void value ([TSON-DATA] §2.9) and counts toward the size bounds |
| Field-type position (type-definition grammar) | no         | Parse error — use type parameters (§5.10)                                                        |
| Type-ref position (type-definition grammar) | no           | Parse error                                                                                      |
| Type-def body (declaration right-hand side) | no             | Parse error — use `{}` for an empty record                                                       |
| Field modifier value (`~`/`=`)            | no             | Resolver error — `~ _` and `= _` are refused; "may be missing or written `_`, and nothing else" is the type slot's to say, `a?: void?` (§5.2) |

When a schema is in scope, a void element requires the governing container type to permit it. Void elements occupy positional slots, and size constraints count all slots. A tuple's voidable position requires an explicit `_` — the tuple's length is fixed by its type, and short tuples are validation errors (§5.3).


### 7.7 Resolver Behaviours at Typed Positions

**Field names in the name type's form.** A document's field name is matched against the record's declared names in the record's name type's `normalization` form (§5.2, §5.5): under a case-folding name type `NAME` is the field `name`, two spellings of one name in one record are a duplicate field, and a name that is no value of the name type can match no declared field and is unrecognised (§7.2). The schema's own references to its fields match the declared spelling exactly; only a document's names are matched in the type's form.

**Typed key equality.** [TSON-DATA] §2.6 defines layered duplicate-key identity: textual identity as the parser's minimum, decoded-value identity for any processor that decodes, and declared-type identity — which can only make *more* keys equal — on top. A schema-aware processor decodes keys by the declared key type's contract (§7.4), so two keys equal under that contract are one key, and a duplicate that only the declared key type relates (`1` and `1.0` under a `number`-keyed map, two spellings of one octet string under a `bytes`-keyed one, `Content-Type` and `content-type` under a text key type whose `normalization` folds case — the textual and decoded-value duplicates of [TSON-DATA] §2.6 are already resolver errors below this layer) is a **Class 2 validation error** at the repeated occurrence, consistent with the set rule (§7.5): identity under a declared *atom* key type is over the key type's value space (§5.5), and under a compound key type — a record, array, map or choice — it is the processor's host equality over what it built, at least the textual identity of [TSON-DATA] §2.6 in a tree the processor builds itself, on §7.5's terms. Which host representation the decoded keys take afterwards is the implementation's concern; the identity contract for an atom key is not. A key typed by an identifier family is a name as well as a key, and the keys of one such map are a look-alike scope (§11.4).

**Empty braces.** [TSON-DATA] §2.8 defers empty-brace disambiguation to declared type information. Under a schema, the expected type at the position supplies it: the resolver transforms an empty-brace value into an empty record or an empty map per the expected type, defaulting to an empty record when the position is untyped. The rule covers exactly the two containers that share the brace form and cannot be told apart when empty; an empty map then faces the map's size bounds like any other. At a position whose expected type is anything else — an array, a tuple, an atom, a choice with no brace-class variant — an empty brace is a **validation error**, reported as a value of the wrong form: an array or tuple has its own empty spelling (`[]`), and admitting `{}` there would let a brace-class value conform to a bracket-class type (§5.4).


### 7.8 Cross-Schema Type References

Under a schema a value's type comes from a namespace, and two questions are independent at every position: whether the **governing namespace** — the schema's own declarations and its imports (§2.2.3) — is admitted, and which **foreign schemas** are. The `scoped` constructor (defined in `meta.tn`) is the sum type that answers both: its instance names the namespaces a value's type may be drawn from, and the value names its own type.

```
scope_kind => !enum [LOCAL EXTERN]
scope_set  => !set_type { element_type: scope_kind  min_items: 1 }
scoped     => sum & {
  scope:    scope_set
  schemas?: {schema_identity => [type_name; 1..]?; 1..}
}
```

`scope` says which namespaces are admitted; `schemas` narrows the foreign ones — missing is any foreign schema, a keyed map is those schemas, and a key's void value (`{K => V?}`, §5.3) is every type that schema declares where a list is those types. The map is keyed by schema identity, so one schema cannot be listed twice, and keys are compared by canonical identity ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1) — a key in any spelling of an identity read to its canonical form — so a pinned key and an unpinned `!!schema` in the data match, as do an A-label and a U-label spelling of one host, each pin verified by the loader on its own (§10.2). The state admitting nothing is unspellable rather than refused — both collections are non-empty by construction — and one coherence rule, of the family a resolver already runs over bound pairs, ties the fields together: `schemas` requires `EXTERN` in `scope`. Where `choice` is the *closed* sum, enumerating its variants, `scoped` is the *open* one, naming where variants are drawn from; both are sums in which the value names its own type, and `disjoint` is absent on `scoped` as on every non-choice sum (§8.1).

Core declares the three named subsets and two applications over them (§9):

| Instance | `scope` | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `declared` | `[LOCAL]` | a type this schema declares or imports, chosen by the value |
| `extern` | `[EXTERN]` | a type from any foreign schema, which the value names for itself |
| `dynamic` | `[LOCAL EXTERN]` | either, from anywhere — the successor to what earlier revisions called `unknown`, under a narrower meaning: the *data* decides the type, and the value is validated in full against the type it names, which is why it is not called `any` |
| `extern_of<S>` | `[EXTERN]`, `schemas: { S => _ }` | any type of one named foreign schema |
| `extern_type<S, T>` | `[EXTERN]`, `schemas: { S => [T] }` | one named type of one named foreign schema |

The two templates are ordinary §5.10 partial applications over a meta constructor: `attachment: extern_type<"https://…/claim.tn", claim>` declares nothing, `S` standing in a key typed by meta's `schema_identity` and `T` inside `[type_name]` — both atoms, so both value parameters, recorded as `S: schema_identity` and `T: type_name` and checked at the application (§5.10), where `schema_identity` admits any identity a document may carry and no fragment (§5.5) — and reaching one schema and one type per application; the multi-type and multi-schema rows keep the instance form or a named declaration in the user's schema. An application's identity is the argument as written (§8.2), so a pinned and an unpinned `S` are two applications where the data-side match is canonical.

**The data rule.** At a position whose type resolves to a `scoped` instance, the value's own shape picks the cell:

- a value carrying `!!schema` is **EXTERN**: its identity MUST be a key of `schemas`, or any schema if `schemas` is missing, and its `!type` MUST be in that key's list, or any of that schema's types if the key's value is void;
- a value carrying `!type` alone is **LOCAL**, resolved in the governing namespace exactly as at any other position;
- a value carrying neither is a **validation error** — *a value at a scoped position must name its type* — in every mode, since the position promises no type for an untagged value to be read as (§7.1).

A cell the instance's `scope` does not hold refuses the value it would have taken, as a validation error: nothing failed to resolve, and [TSON-DATA] §8.1's categories make that of it at both cells — a value carrying `!!schema` at a `declared` position included, which is this rule's case and not the restriction's below. The read of an EXTERN value is the scope push below: load the named schema through the ordinary library (§10.1), so a schema this processor will not supply is a refusal and one it could not obtain leaves the value undetermined ([TSON-DATA] §8.1), never a verdict on the value; push the scope; resolve `!type` in it; validate in full; pop.

**Values carry their scope.** A value matched at an EXTERN cell MUST carry its own `!!schema` directive identifying the foreign schema and a `!type` annotation identifying the type within it — schema scope changes are always visible in the data, never implicit. A `!!schema` directive on a scoped value pushes the new schema scope for that value; when the value ends, the scope reverts:

```
!!schema:"https://tson.io/2026/medical/patient.tn?sha256=a4f2e8d1c3b5a7f9e2d4c6b8a0f1e3d5c7b9a2f4e6d8c0b3a5f7e9d1c4b6a8f0"
!patient_record {
  patient: "1234"
  attachments: [
    !!schema:"https://tson.io/2026/insurance/claim.tn?sha256=f8b2a1d3c5e7f9a1b3d5e7f9a2b4d6e8f0a3b5d7e9f1a4b6d8e0f2a5b7d9e1f3"
    !insurance_claim { claim_id: CLM-5678  amount: 450.00  provider: "City Medical" }
    !!schema:"https://tson.io/2026/radiology/report.tn?sha256=d4e9c7f1a3b5d7e9f2a4b6d8e0f3a5b7d9e1f4a6b8d0e2f5a7b9d1e3f6a8b0d2"
    !radiology_report { study_id: RAD-9012  modality: MRI  findings: Normal }
  ]
}
```

Here `attachments` is declared `[extern]` — any foreign schema per element — or, where the schema means exactly those two, a narrowed instance listing both: `attachments: [claim_or_report]` over `claim_or_report => !scoped { scope: [EXTERN]  schemas: { "https://…/claim.tn" => _  "https://…/report.tn" => _ } }`, which is the case the example exists to show and which no single-schema form could spell. Each directive binds to the single array element it prefixes ([TSON-DATA] §2.3, §2.7); the example follows the encoder recommendation of one directive-carrying element per line.

**Composition order under a scoped element.** Within a scoped value the order is fixed by the grammar: directive, then annotations, then the optional type annotation, then the core value ([TSON-DATA] §2.3, §3.1). The directive opens its scope before the element's own augmentation resolves: annotations on a scoped element resolve against the newly scoped schema's namespace (§3.3.3), and the type annotation resolves against its type-name namespace.

**Typed-position restriction, derived.** A nested `!!schema` directive is admitted at a position exactly when the position's type resolves to a `scoped` instance whose `scope` holds `EXTERN`, containers descending as above — for a record field or map entry value the field or entry type applies; for an array element, the element type applies. Where it is not admitted, which rule refuses it depends on the position, and the two never overlap. At a position whose type is a `scoped` instance whose `scope` does not hold `EXTERN`, the cell rule above decides and the directive is a **validation error**: it is well-formed, the position is one that reads a scope's cells, and nothing failed to resolve. At a position whose own type is **not** a `scoped` instance — a record, a choice, a `value`, or a container, whose elements and values are positions of their own — it is a **resolver error**, a push having no cell there to be refused by. The rule is no longer a list of permissive names: a position admits a scope push because its type says so, the way `disjoint` is a fact of a choice's declarations (§8.1). A `value`-typed position in particular admits none — a single token is not a scope, and a pushed schema's namespace is consulted only by the `!name` annotations a token position does not carry. The outer schema opts in to receiving foreign values at each position, by naming a type that admits them: cross-schema acceptance is authored intent, not accident.

**A schemaless document opens no schema scope.** A nested `!!schema` in a document with no `!!schema` of its own is a validation error naming the directive. The restriction above exists so that an outer schema can opt in, and a schemaless document has no outer schema to do the opting: admitting the push there would make a Class 1 document a Class 2 document halfway down, on the strength of the same absence that makes the check impossible, would give the one directive with no header-visible cost ceiling to a document nothing agreed to validate, and would produce a verdict on one value in a document that is otherwise not being validated at all. [TSON-DATA] §1.5's classes stay decidable from the header: a reader knows from the first two lines which obligations it has taken on. A processor that meets such a directive reports it once, consumes it, and reads the value it prefixed schemalessly.


## 8. Resolver Output

The resolver's output for a schema is a value of the kernel's `schema` — a namespace from each name to the value its entry resolves to (§4.1): the output records and their conventions (§8.1), template instantiation and identity (§8.2), and references (§8.3). Each construct's mapping to its value is defined with the construct in §4–§5. Resolved output and source are one grammar: output is source with sugar expanded, bodies flattened and chains computed, and an entry written as a constructor application resolves to itself.


### 8.1 Output Records: `type_ref` and `type_argument`

**An entry is its value.** A schema map's entry resolves to the value its declaration denotes, a `top`, with no wrapper around it: `person => !record { fields: { … } }`, `integer => !integer_type {}`, `age => !integer_type { chain: { supertypes: [integer] }  min: 0 }`, `type_name => !reference { target: identifier }`, `vec => !template { … }`, and a namespace entry `orders => !interface { … }`. Values are annotated with the structurally appropriate constructor: `!record` for vocabulary bodies (constructors, §4.2) and for every record, open or closed; a binding record headed by the applied constructor for every closed non-reference definition (§5.6); `!reference { target: T }` for reference-kind entries; `!template { … }` for an open entry that is no record (§5.10); and a namespace type's tag for a namespace value (§4.1). `schema`'s `value_type` is `top`, and the subsumption rule of §7.2 admits every one with no exception: every annotation head carries `top` in its chain — constructors through `type` and their base kinds, a namespace type through `namespace`, `reference` and `template` by direct composition (§4.1) — and `top` being abstract, each entry is read by its tag among `top`'s subtypes.

Four things a reader might expect are deliberately not on the entry. There is no `constructor` field: whether an entry is a constructor is whether it is `top` or IS-A it, derivable from its chain (§4.2). There is no `kind` field: an entry's kind is derived (below). There is no `source` field: a construction's constructor is the value's tag, a refinement's origin is the chain's nearest member, and the one fact a wrapper once held that nothing else states — the application an entry is the instantiation of — rides on the entry's key as `@application` (§8.2). And there is no `disjoint` or `members` on the entry: each derived index lives in the body it is derived over.

**A type carries its chain.** Every type body — every value whose constructor IS-A `type` — carries `type`'s one field, `chain`, a `type_chain` (§4.1): `supertypes`, the closed IS-A chain, every element a bare name, complete to a fixed point through applied parents (§5.8) and sorted; `subtypes`, the inverse over the entries of this map; and, on a record template alone, `parents`, the parents as written, one of which may be an application (§5.10). An empty chain is omitted. A composition's fields are flattened and its `chain` stands among them first. `reference`, `template` and a namespace value compose `top` and carry no chain — a hop, an open body and a map carry no IS-A, which is the rule. A declaration never writes `chain` and a constructor never declares a field of that name (§4.1).

**Open entries.** An open entry takes one of two forms (§5.10).
