A **record template** is `!record { fields: { … }  parameters: [ … ] }`: an ordinary record body read as the vocabulary, with its `parameters` in place, a parameter standing in a field's `type` or `value` or in an argument of an application, its `discriminators` stated as the family's, its `extension` each application's, and `chain.parents` the parents as written. Every other open entry is an instance of the kernel's `template` constructor — `template => top & { parameters: [template_param]  template: text }`, so `set => !template { parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind }]  template: "!set_type { element_type: T }" }`. The parameters are a list of `template_param` records, in declaration order — the arity, order and types an application binds against: `template_param => { name: param_name  type: type_kind  bound?: type_kind }`, where `type` is the type an argument is read as, derived from the body and narrowed by a written type, and `bound` is present only where `type` is `type_kind` (§5.10). It is a list of records rather than a map, which would lose the order arguments are positional in, or two parallel lists, which would be free to drift. `type` is a derived fact that may also carry what an author wrote — `<N: int8>` lives nowhere else — so ingest verifies it rather than recomputing it (below). A `template`'s application is **text**, the held body as written under §5.10's one-spelling rule, since a body carrying a parameter in a value slot (`min_items: N`) is not a value of any constructor's record shape and cannot be typed as one; what is compared — for identity, on ingest, and between two resolvers' outputs — is the **parsed form** of the text, never the text, so whitespace is free (§5.10). A `!template` carries no chain, `extension` or `discriminators`: it is not a type, and an application of it is its expansion (§8.2). A consumer of closed entries never meets a `!template` (§1.3), and dispatches on a record template's stated facts without reading a field that carries a parameter; a consumer that ingests a `!template` needs a schema parser, which ingest already requires (below). `template` is resolver vocabulary: nothing is ever typed by it, and a source declaration applying it directly is a resolver error — the parameter list `<…>` is the authored spelling of an open entry (§5.10).

**Kind is derived.** A consumer that needs an entry's kind (§4.1) derives it from the entry's value, in this order, and every conforming resolver derives the same answer: **(1)** the value is a `!template` → TEMPLATE, an open entry with no kind of its own; **(2)** else the value's constructor head is `reference` → REFERENCE — the head lookup would say PRODUCT, since the kernel's `reference` is itself a product, and §4.1 makes REFERENCE a kind the alias form confers rather than a base kind; **(3)** else the entry is `top` or IS-A it — it is a constructor — → the base-kind name among its own `chain.supertypes` (`atom`, `sum`, `namespace`), each counted only as the kernel's own entry, or PRODUCT if none: a constructor's kind states what its *instances* are, not what its own body is (`integer_type` has a `!record` body and is ATOM-kinded); **(4)** else → the kind of the entry the value's constructor head names: ATOM, PRODUCT or SUM for an instance of a type constructor, NAMESPACE for a value of a namespace type. Branch (4) resolves the head through the structure namespace (§3.3.1), which a consumer able to read the body at all already holds. A kind is judged by origin, not spelling: a schema's own record named `namespace` reaches none of the kernel's entries, so a composition onto it is PRODUCT.

**The `type_ref` and `type_argument` records.** A slot that names the type of a value is typed `type_kind` (§4.2) and, in a closed entry, holds a bare name — `record_field.type`, `tuple_element.element_type`, `array.element_type`, `map.key_type` and `value_type`, `choice.variants`, `template_param.type` and `bound` — a type's name, judged a member of the kind when the schema links. The structured reference `type_ref`, which carries arguments, is the *application* form and stands only where an application may: `type_chain.parents`, an open record's `record_field.type` (§5.10), `type_argument.name`, the `@application` annotation, held text, and `reference.target`, which may name a constructor (§3.3.1):

```
type_ref => {
  name:       type_name
  arguments?: [type_argument]
}

type_argument => {
  ( name: type_ref | value: value )
}
```

A `type_ref`'s `name` is the referenced type or, within a template, a parameter, and `arguments` carries the application's positional arguments — present only where an application is still open or is an entry's identity (§5.3): a use-site application resolves to a bare reference to its expansion, so `arguments` in output means "an application" and appears inside template bodies, in a record template's fields and parents, in an open alias's `target` and in `@application` only. A `type_argument`'s `name` member holds every *reference* — a type, an entry, or (in template bodies) a parameter of either kind — while its `value` member holds concrete literals only; exactly one member is present (the group is not optional and its two options hold one member each, §5.11). The reference/literal split is therefore structural: a token in a `name` member is always a reference, so value-typed token literals (enum members) can never be mistaken for parameter names, and member population is the open/closed signal — substitution replaces `{ name: P }` with the bound argument (`{ name: text }`, `{ value: 1920 }`) as parameters close. In a record template a parameter stands at a `type_kind` slot as written, resolved output included; in any other template it sits inside the `template.template` text.

**The `reference`, `record_field` and `field_map` records.** An alias body is `!reference { target: T }`, where `target` is a `type_ref` — a bare name for every closed alias, and an application with its arguments, in `type_ref`'s record form, where the alias is still open (`<B> !reference { target: { name: pair  arguments: [ { name: uuid }  { name: B } ] } }`, §5.10), so that a partial application states the arguments it binds. A reference is a hop: `target` names the immediate referent, and a use site elsewhere in the output that names the alias names the alias (§8.3). A record's `fields` is a **`field_map`** — the namespace from each field's name to its `record_field` (§4.1, §5.2): the name is the key, written once by its spelling, and each `record_field` carries its `type` (a `type_kind` value) and the four facts a reader consults — `optional` (the name's `?`, default `false`), `voidable` (the type's `?`, default `false`), `role` (`FREE`, `DEFAULT` or `FIXED`, default `FREE`) and `value` — each default omitted in output, and `value` present exactly when `role` is not `FREE`. Three invariants hold over them: `value` is present exactly when `role` is not `FREE`; `DEFAULT` requires `optional: true`, a default on a key that is always written being a value nothing can reach; and `FIXED` requires `voidable: false`, since the `_` decision is taken before the pin is consulted. There is no separate parameter channel, and there is no stored omission answer: a reader derives it — a field that is not optional is missing, an optional one with a value injects it, except at a field group's member, which the group governs and never supplies (§5.2, §5.11). In a closed entry `value` holds a concrete fixed or default value, read against the field's declared type (§5.2); in a record template it may hold a parameter, unlabelled, since a token there is a parameter exactly when its text resolves into the enclosing record's `parameters` (§5.10), and a closed entry has no parameters for one to resolve into. A `record` body also carries `extension` (omitted at `OPEN`), `discriminators` (present where the record is a member-dispatched base), `name_type` (present exactly where it was stated, §5.2), `groups`, and `parameters` where the record is a template. The `field_map`'s key family is the record's name type — `field_name` where neither the record nor a supertype states one — and two keys that are one value under it are a resolver error (§5.2).

**`optional` and `voidable` in output.** The series' two kinds of nothing have one name each wherever a record states them (§5.2). `optional` — the slot may be missing — is a field of `record_field` and of `field_group`, where it is the group's own `?`, beside `optional_members` for the members that may be missing once their option is chosen (§5.11). `voidable` — the slot's value may be void — is a field of `record_field`, `tuple_element`, `array` and `map`, where it speaks of the field's value, the position, the element and the entry's value. A container's part is never missing, so a container has no `optional`; a map's key is never void, so `map.voidable` is its value's alone; both are structural, and no rule has to refuse them. Each is a boolean defaulting to `false` and omitted there. There is no `element_state` and no `state` field: a binding record carrying one is refused as a member its vocabulary does not declare (§5.5), never read as the other fact.

**Positional form.** `name` is `type_ref`'s only unmarked-name field, so the general positional form (§5.6) applies at every `type_ref`-typed position: a bare name token fills `name` directly, and a braced record is the explicit form. Canonical output MUST use the bare token whenever `arguments` is absent; an explicit record without `arguments` is valid data but non-canonical. A `type_kind` slot of a closed entry holds a bare name and nothing else. `type_argument` deliberately has *no* unmarked-name field and hence no positional form: a bare token cannot self-classify as reference or literal, so its braced record is load-bearing, not ceremony. The `name<args>` sugar is schema syntax and not data syntax (§5.6): a data document at a `type_ref` slot writes an application as the explicit record, `{ name: page  arguments: [ { name: order } ] }`, and the spelling that names one is an alias in the schema (§8.2). `record_field.type` is `type_kind`, an atom, so a `record_field` has a positional form and a document may write `fields: { x => text }` for `fields: { x => { type: text } }`. Canonical output writes the explicit record, tagged: `fields: { x => !record_field { type: text } }`. The untagged record and the positional form are read as the same value, `field_map`'s `value_type` typing the position.

**Reading parameter references.** **A parameter reference appears only inside an open entry's own body** — a held text, or a record template's fields and parents — and every reference outside one names a type and is read directly against the schema. Within a held body, and within a record template's fields and parents, parameters and type names share the `identifier` type, so a `name` — in any `type_ref`, at any depth, including `type_argument` name members and `reference.target`, and at any `type_kind` slot — resolves against the entry's own `parameters` list first, then the schema's type-name namespace, the same precedence used during source-level parsing (§5.10); a name at a constructor-channel position — the held body's head, or a body-annotation head — resolves through the structure namespace (§3.3.1), the same channel that resolved it at source level. The two channels are consulted at disjoint positions and never mix (§2.2.3): type-name tokens are data scoped to the schema being described, while annotation and constructor heads belong to the governing chain. Inside a held body a parameter may also stand in a value slot — a constructor's scalar field, an enum's member list — and in a record template in `record_field.value`, read by the same precedence (§5.10). A token in a closed body that would resolve only as a parameter is an unresolved reference, the verdict every other reference form gives it.

**`@application` is the one fact the value does not state.** An entry that closes a template application carries, on its schema-map key, `@application:{ name: box  arguments: [{ name: int32 }] }` — the **canonical** application, arguments with reference chains followed to their terminal entries and nested applications resolved to entry names (§8.2, §8.3). It is the entry's identity as an instantiation, and an entry of any kind can carry it: a record template's declared member, a namespace entry closed from a templated instance, the alias a template's application closes to, a self-reaching application's instantiation entry (§8.2). It is a fact about the entry and not about a type, so it lives beside the entry as `@synthetic` does, is derived — an author's `@application` is a resolver error, as a written `chain` is — and is **read as stated** on ingest, not recomputed (§6, §10.1), which is what lets an imported schema's instantiations unify with a later schema's. Nothing else records provenance: which operator a refinement used, and what a subtracted record was subtracted from, are what was written, nothing reads them after resolution, and the source document has them.

**The derived indexes: `subtypes`, `members`, `disjoint`.** Three indexes are computed by the resolver and never set by a declaration, each living in the body it is derived over. `chain.subtypes` is the transitive inverse of `supertypes` across the schema's namespace — "what is in family X?" — and `supertypes` answers "is this in family X?" by name; a parent written as an application contributes, once the composing entry closes, the name of the entry its arguments name — `ok<text>` indexes `result<text>`, never a head name (§5.8) — and a record template is indexed by its own name, its members being the applications a declaration names (§5.10). `supertypes`' standing differs from `subtypes`': `subtypes` is a cache, fully derivable and never trusted, while `supertypes` is part of a type's serialized meaning — the chain and a flat body cannot be recomputed from each other, and for the atom family the hop lives only there, since desugaring erases the surface distinction between refining an instance (`age => !integer ^ { … }`, IS-A `integer`) and constructing a fresh sibling (`port => !integer_type { … }`, IS-A nothing), both serializing under the `!integer_type` tag with an identical body shape. Construction and expansion are not IS-A: a sugar construction (`ids => [order]`) carries no supertypes (§5.6), and a template's expansion carries whatever chain the expansion's own `^` or `&` gives it — a record template's declared member IS-A the template (§5.10) — but a minted record takes no edge to a parent application nothing declares, and is a member of nothing (§8.2). **`members`** is `kind_type`'s index (§4.2): the names of every entry in the governing closure built with `of`, computed at link over resolved entries and sorted as `subtypes` is, so `identifier_kind => !kind_type { of: identifier_type  members: [field_name identifier param_name scheme_name type_name] }`. **`disjoint`** is `choice`'s: `choice => sum & { variants: [type_kind]  disjoint?: boolean }`, and for every choice, declared or synthetic, the resolver writes `true` or `false` beside the variants it is derived over — discrimination-class distinctness, a total, two-valued derivation (§5.4). It sits there rather than on the entry because a variant list is the only thing it is a fact *about*, and §7.2's closed-record rule refuses `disjoint` on any other body, so the non-choice sums (the `scoped` instances, §7.8) need no carve-out; the `@disjoint` annotation (§5.4), when present, is the author's assertion checked against this derived value at schema load, and does not appear in the value itself. Like every name in resolver output, the names in these indexes are tokens in the described schema's own namespace (§2.2.3): an index never reaches outside the resolution that wrote it, and a consumer follows the entries in the defining schema's output.

**Ingest.** When resolved output is ingested as data (§10.1): the resolved document is a *data* document, and its header admits only `!!id` and `!!schema` ([TSON-DATA] §2.2, §3.3) — it cannot carry `!!import`, and its `!!schema` names its own governing chain, not the schema it describes. The described schema's identity, and the import list that constitutes the namespace in which the map's type-name tokens are interpreted (§2.2.3), are therefore not recoverable from the resolved document and MUST be supplied to ingest explicitly; the source schema document's header, held in the library (§10.1), is the sole authority for both. Then: `chain.subtypes`, every kind's `members`, `disjoint` in every choice body, and the derived annotation `@synthetic` on synthetic entries' keys (§8.2) MUST be discarded and recomputed, so none can be forged into a document to change how it reads; an entry's kind is derived on the same terms and was never in the document to trust (above). `chain.supertypes` and `chain.parents` are **read as stated**, never recomputed, with integrity verified — every listed supertype must exist, the list must be closed to a fixed point, an atom's supertypes must share the entry's constructor tag, and a record's must be consistent with the flattened `fields` — because a flat body and its chain cannot be recomputed from each other (§4.1). `@application` is read as stated (above). Author-written annotations, key- and value-position alike (§6), are preserved as data, a restated field's in the order §5.8 fixes. The closed-entry rule (§5.10) MUST be verified — no token inside a closed body that resolves only as a parameter (§5.6); a `!template`'s held text is parsed and **re-resolved as source** against the supplied namespace, since a held body is never read against a vocabulary until it closes, and the parsed form MUST equal the one-spelling form §5.10 requires; the text is never compared as text. Each parameter's recorded `type` and `bound` are **verified, not recomputed**: ingest derives them again and checks that a recorded `type` IS-A the derived one and a recorded `bound` IS-A every inherited one — the rule a written type already met at the declaration (§5.10) — so that a written narrowing survives and a stale or altered one is refused, where discarding and recomputing would silently drop every type an author wrote. Every entry carrying `@application` MUST equal the recomputation by substitution from it, and every synthetic entry MUST carry the body its constructor's vocabulary admits — the self-describing checks. A checked annotation (§6) is re-checked on ingest as it was at load: `@disjoint` against the recomputed `disjoint`. Every record's and enum's names are re-judged against its name type and every kind-typed slot's name against its kind (§4.2, §5.2). The family checks of §5.2 — every member of a member-dispatched base pinning each selector, pins distinct as values, nothing composed onto a FINAL record, no minted record a member of anything — are re-run over the ingested map and the closure that holds it (§3.3.4), the declaration rules of §5.11 are verified over every `field_group`, and the three `record_field` invariants above are verified per field. Canonical-form violations (an explicit `type_ref` record without `arguments`, a positional `record_field`, a chain written where none is admitted) are resolver errors at ingest. Within-family retargeting — a document claiming an entry refines a different sibling of the same constructor — is internally consistent and undetectable from the document alone; this residual gap is one reason resolved-form documents are never schema sources (§10.1) and ingest is an explicit, opt-in act.

**Body patterns** (informative — the construct sections govern):

| Source form | Resolved value | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Root record `abstract {}` | PRODUCT; `!record { chain: { subtypes: [...] }  fields: {}  extension: ABSTRACT }`; no supertypes | `top` |
| `type`, a base kind, `namespace` | PRODUCT; `!record { chain: { supertypes: [top …] … }  …  extension: ABSTRACT }` | `type`, `atom`, `sum`, `namespace` |
| Fresh record `{ fields }` | PRODUCT; `!record { fields: { … } }`; no supertypes | `person`, `record_field` |
| Composition `A & B & { ... }` | `!record { chain: { supertypes: [A B …transitive] }  fields: { …flattened } }`; kind per §5.5 | `employee` |
| Refinement `T ^ { ... }` | `chain.supertypes: [T …]`; materialised body | `production` |
| Subtraction `T - { f }` | `!record { fields: { … } }`; no supertypes (§5.9) | `account_public` |
| Atom constructor `atom & { ... }` | ATOM; IS-A `top`; `chain.supertypes: [atom top type]` | `integer_type`, `enum_type`, `kind_type` |
| Product constructor `product & { ... }` | PRODUCT; IS-A `top`; `chain.supertypes: [product top type]` | `record`, `array` |
| Sum constructor `sum & { ... }` | SUM; IS-A `top`; `chain.supertypes: [sum top type]` | `choice`, `scoped` |
| Namespace type `namespace & { key_type: = K  value_type: = V }` | NAMESPACE; IS-A `top`; `chain.supertypes: [namespace top]`; a record of its pinned facets | `schema`, `field_map`, `interface` |
| Constructor refinement `T ^ { ... }` in a meta-schema | IS-A `top` through `T`; `chain.supertypes: [T …]` | `set_type`, `enum` |
| Instance template `<T, N> !C { ... }` | `!template { parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind } { name: N  type: … }]  template: "!C { ... }" }` (§5.10) | `vector`, `set`, `kind`, `ref`, `extern_of` |
| Constructor instance `!T {}` | kind from `T`'s family; `!T {}`; no supertypes | `integer`, `value`, `dynamic` |
| Kind instance `!kind_type { of: C }` | ATOM; `!kind_type { of: C  members: [...] }`, `members` derived (§4.2) | `identifier_kind`, `type_kind` |
| Atom refinement `!I ^ { v }` | `!ctor { chain: { supertypes: [I …] }  v }` | `age`, `non_negative_integer` |
| Sugar or constructor-application body | construction in place; binding-record value; no supertypes (§5.6) | `lookup => {text => integer}` |
| Record template `<T> { ... }` | `!record { fields: { … }  parameters: [{ name: T  type: type_kind }] }`, a family base nameable at a type position, ABSTRACT by construction, `chain.parents` as written (§5.10) | `container`, `pair` |
| Sugar form at a use site | lifts to a synthetic entry — closed (constructor body) or open (`!template`) per §5.3; the use site holds a bare reference | `[text]`, `{text => integer}`, `[T; N]` in a template body |
| Template application | the declaration naming it, with `@application` on its key; else its expansion, a closed synthetic, beside which a self-reaching application keeps an instantiation entry, a `!reference` to the synthetic carrying `@application` (§8.2) | `string_triple => vector<text, 3>`, a use site's `set<text>` |
| Namespace value `!N { k => v … }` | NAMESPACE; the map as written, the type's tag on it; no chain (§4.1) | `orders => !interface { … }` |
| Choice `(A \| B)` | SUM; `!choice { variants: [...]  disjoint: … }`, the fact always recorded in the body (§5.4) | `contact_method` |
| Field group `( a: T \| b: U )` | members flattened into `fields` as `optional: true`; grouping in `record.groups`, one list of members per option (§5.11) | `integer_type`, `type_argument` |
| Abstract base with selectors `abstract { a: T =?  … }` | PRODUCT; `!record { … extension: ABSTRACT  discriminators: [a] }`; members in `subtypes` pin `a` (§5.2) | `pet` |
| Reference `name` | REFERENCE; `!reference { target: name }`; no chain — a bare name, never a fully-bound application, which is an instantiation in place | `id => uuid` (§8.3) |

The `schema` type is a namespace from type names to values; schema lookup is by name. Declared entries are keyed by their declared names; synthetic entries and self-reaching instantiation entries are keyed by their internal names, each synthetic entry's key carrying the derived `@synthetic` marker (§6, §8.2).


### 8.2 Instantiation and Synthetic Entries

Resolver output contains internally named entries beyond the declared ones, and one identity discipline covers them all. **Every application materialises, as its expansion**: a sugar form or constructor application at a use site lifts to a **synthetic entry** — closed or open per §5.3's lift rule — and a fully-bound application of a template materialises its **expansion**, the closed synthetic entry the substituted form lands on, keyed on its content (§5.10). Nothing is carried structurally in place: a use site holds a bare reference to its entry, and `type_ref.arguments` in output means an open application inside a template body or a record template's fields and parents, an open alias's target, or an `@application`, nothing else (§8.1). A declaration's own body resolves in place as a construction (§5.6) and is the one thing that never lifts.

*Trigger positions.* Lifting and materialisation fire wherever an application is **named at a type position**: record field types, group member types, tuple element types, array and map element positions, choice variants, and arguments at any depth. A top-level type-def body that is a fully-bound template application makes the declaration the entry itself (below). Composition targets and refinement sources remain restricted to named type references, optionally with arguments; inline structural forms there are resolver errors (§5.7, §5.8, §12.1), and an application there is subsumed as an operand and mints nothing (§5.8). An *open* application inside a template body does not materialise — it closes when its template does (§5.10).

*An application nothing declares is its expansion.* A use site naming `set<text>` names the synthetic `!set_type { element_type: text }` lands on — the entry a `[text]`-style sugar or another template expanding to the same form also lands on; a use site naming `box<int32>` names a closed synthetic record, keyed on its content, which `crate<int32>` over a template with the same field list lands on too. No instantiation entry is minted and nothing records the application: the expansion records nothing a value can tell apart, since it admits exactly the synthetic's values. The one exception is a **self-reaching expansion**: `nested => <T> ( T | [nested<T>] )` closed at `nested<text>` names itself inside its own body, so its content cannot be written without a name, and the knot is tied on the application, which keeps an **instantiation entry** — a `!reference` to the synthetic, keyed on the canonical application, carrying `@application` (§5.10.1). The rule is total: whether closing reached the application again decides it. **A minted record is a member of nothing.** A minted record whose chain would name a declared type — `k: dog_of<text>` over `dog_of => <T> pet & { … }`, or a parent application a declaration owns — would be a family member with no name a read, a tag or a binding could use, and is a resolver error at the declaration that wrote the application (§5.2). A parent application nothing declares gives a minted record no chain edge: `u: labelled<text>` over `labelled => <T> box<T> & { label: text }`, beside `w: box<text>`, loads, the minted record IS-A nothing. A minted record gains no IS-A edge later; a declaration's operand still reaches it — `vip => customer & box<int32>`, beside a use site's `box<int32>`, is IS-A that synthetic and stands where `crate<int32>` is expected, the two applications being one type. **A declaration names only itself.** An unnamed form with a declaration's content is not that declaration: beside `text_list => [text]`, a field written `t: [text]` names the synthetic, and `!text_list [a]` there is refused by §7.2; collecting would need a new rule for every form kind and would make two declarations sharing a body ask whether they interchange, and they are two types. **A diagnostic names what the author wrote**, and nothing for a form with no spelling: a minted atom instance (`b<10>` over `b => <N> !integer_type { min: N  max: 100 }`) has no sugar spelling and no author-written name, so a message about it is the family's own (`'5' is less than the minimum 10`), located by its position, never the entry's content-derived key; a minted record is named as the body a record is written with (`unknown field 'nope' on '{ v: int32 }'`).

*Two identities.* **A declared entry's identity is its name**: two declarations are two types however alike their bodies — `stock_id => !uuid_type {}` and `other_id => !uuid_type {}` are two atoms, and a purely structural rule would collapse them and destroy a distinction §7.2 enforces (§5.5). **A declaration whose body denotes a type is that type's entry, in both lift channels**: `text_list => [text]` is the construction in place (§5.6), and `bx => box<text>` is the instantiation in place — the substituted binding record its value, closed under the author's name, `@application` on its key, with nothing minted beside it and no `!reference` hop. A use-site application resolves to a declaration that owns it where one exists, so "two fully-bound applications denote the same entry" holds within a schema. Two declarations naming one application are **two entries**, neither privileged and neither collapsed — what `a => { v: text }` beside `b => { v: text }` already is, and so what `t => <T> { v: T }` with `a => t<text>` and `b => t<text>` has to be; where the pair is a discriminated family §5.2's pin-distinctness rule refuses it, exactly as it refuses the hand-written pair. What this gives up is stated plainly: a content-derived name is a function of the form alone, which is what let two independently resolved namespaces agree on it across the import merge (below); a declared name cannot carry that, so a schema writing `box<text>` that has never seen the schema declaring `bx` lands on the content-keyed expansion while that schema calls it `bx`, and the merge sees two names for one form. That costs nothing a value can tell — both entries admit exactly the same values, and a use site in the importer resolves to the declaration owning the form — and it cannot reach a family, a family member being declared. **A minted entry's identity is its canonical content**, since it has no declared name to be its identity; "identity is structural" is true of the minted half only, one rule per family:

- **Self-reaching instantiation entries** — two fully-bound applications denote the same entry if and only if their **canonical applications are structurally equal**: the application with every argument's reference chain followed to its terminal entry and every nested application resolved to its entry's name (§8.3), compared member-by-member — heads, argument order, `name` against `name`, `value` against `value` under the rule below. Following the chain is what makes a *rename* transparent: `box<user_id>` over `user_id => uuid` denotes the same type as `box<uuid>`, because a reference is the same type under another name (§5.7's table) — where `box<!uuid ^ {}>` and `box<!uuid_type {}>`, a refinement and a fresh instance, keep entries of their own. The canonical application is exactly what `@application` records, so identity is a single-level structural comparison of it, and spelling variance never forks an entry; the name the author wrote is provenance, not identity, and survives at the use site that wrote it. A declared instantiation's identity is its name (above); a use site reaches it by the canonical application, compared on these terms.
- **Value arguments compare as values, and are recorded as written.** A literal argument is recorded in `@application` (and substituted into the body) as the token the author wrote, so resolver output round-trips the spelling; identity, however, compares the value the token denotes under [TSON-DATA] §4 — exactly the equivalence §4.3 states and no wider. Radix, digit separators, and a redundant sign fall away (`255`, `0xFF`, `0b1111_1111`, `0o377`, and `+255` are one argument), a float's written scale falls away (`.5`/`0.5`, `1.0`/`1.00`/`1e0`), and `.inf`/`.infinity` are one value; quoted strings compare by text. What does **not** fall away is the base type: §4 resolves `1` to an integer and `1.0` to a float, so those remain two arguments even though one magnitude covers both. `vector<float32, 255>` and `vector<float32, 0xFF>` therefore denote one entry, and a choice listing `[float32; 255]` and `[float32; 0xFF]` as two variants is the same-variant-twice error of §5.4, not an accepted non-disjoint choice.
- **Closed synthetic entries** — structural equality of the resolved binding record: one entry per distinct concrete form schema-wide, whichever spelling or template produced it. A closed synthetic carries the **constructor** it builds as its tag, never the application that produced it, which is what lets every channel land on it.
- **Open synthetic entries** — structural equality of the **parsed** held body **up to consistent renaming of parameters** (`<T, N>` and `<A, B>` over the same shape are the same template), normalised by renaming parameters positionally (`p0`, `p1`, …) before comparing; the text a `!template` carries is never compared as text (§5.10). This is what "one spelling" exists for: `<T> { a: [T] }` written twice mints one entry, and an entry's content-derived name is computed over the renamed parsed form.

The channels dedupe against each other's products: `[order; 1..]` written directly in a plain declaration and the same form arising inside a materialised template land on **one** closed synthetic entry, because both comparisons occur after names have meaning, over resolved structure. The moment is normative: desugar-time lifting *creates* a synthetic entry, but its identity settles **after Pass 2**, when references have resolved — eagerly-lifted synthetics that become structurally identical under resolution merge into one entry, so the one-entry-per-form rule holds schema-wide regardless of which moment produced each candidate. **The merge pass is required**, not an optimisation: a closed lift hashes its binding record at desugar, before its inner applications are rewritten to entry names, while an open lift hashes the closed record at materialisation, so without a pass that re-derives every synthetic's identity from its resolved record, `[box<text>]` written directly and `[box<T>]` closed with `T := text` land on two entries for one type (§5.10). The first occurrence materialises; later occurrences reuse; recursive references resolve to the entry's own name (§5.10.1). Closure consults templates and never modifies them: closing `grid<pixel, 4>` after `grid<pixel, 3>` reuses the template entry and both open synthetics untouched while producing fresh closed entries.

*Synthetic entries are an implementation's own.* Their names are resolver-chosen and their shape is unconstrained beyond producing the required resolved output: a processor is free to mint one per schema, share them, alpha-normalise their parameters, or keep them however its own resolver requires, and the lift targets are never kernel-declared (§5.3). An author never writes one and never references one; the one thing an author does is name an application they want to reuse, and the spelling for that is an alias — `order_page => page<order>` — which is one line, gives the application an identity and a diagnostic located at the author's own declaration, and is RECOMMENDED over repeating the application or the explicit record form (§8.1).

*Entry names are internal.* Minted entries are named by the resolver, and the names are **not part of this specification's conformance surface**: an implementation chooses them freely, subject to two rules and one recommendation. Freshness (MUST): an internal name is a valid `identifier` and collides with no declared entry and no other internal entry. Stability within a resolution (MUST): all references to one entry use one name. Determinism (SHOULD): names SHOULD be derived from the entry's identity-bearing content — the canonical application for a self-reaching instantiation, the resolved body structure for a closed synthetic, the parameter-normalised body for an open one — a readable head plus a structural hash, for instance. Content-derived names keep re-resolution output diff-stable, and they are what makes independently-resolved namespaces agree on their internal names wherever their structures agree — which is what lets the import merge (below) unify rather than collide. Internal names do not exist at source level (there is nothing to author), MUST NOT be relied on across resolutions or implementations, and resolved-form comparison between implementations is structural — equality up to renaming of internal entries, with set-typed fields compared as written, in source declaration order (§7.5). Two consequences for a **consumer** follow and are stated because a wrong pick is invisible until someone writes a configuration: **no consumer contract may require a derived name** — a binding map, a generated class table, a configuration file keys on a name some declaration gave the application, and a processor holding both SHOULD show and accept the declared name wherever it shows or accepts one, falling back to the entry's own only for an entry no declaration names; and **the declared applications of one template are recoverable from `@application`** — a declared instantiation records the head and arguments on its key, so the family a host language spells `Result<T>` is a walk over entries and needs no index. A derived name written in data (`!box_text_04117bb4`) is a valid identifier in the namespace and is not refused; such a document was written by reading one processor's output and fails at the next, whose spelling differs.

*Entry shape.* A declared instantiation: `@application` on its key, the canonical fully-bound application; its value the substituted binding record, headed by the applied constructor (§5.6) — closed, with the expansion's kind by derivation and whatever chain the expansion's own `^` or `&` gives it: for a record template's member, `chain.supertypes` holding the template and everything it IS-A, plus the closed parent an open `parents` application named (§5.8), its `extension` the record's own (OPEN unless the template wrote `abstract`), never the base's ABSTRACT, and no `discriminators` — closing pins each selector a parameter pins to its argument, and a `=?` selector is pinned by no argument, so closing does not pin it (§5.10). A synthetic entry resolves under the top-level-construction rule: a binding-record value headed by the constructor (a `!template` while open), no supertypes, `@synthetic` at its key (§6, §8.1). Materialisation also runs the value-level checks that the held body deferred: **every family coherence rule that §5.3 and §5.5 state for a literally written body applies again at materialisation, over the operands that were parameters** — `min_items ≤ max_items` for the containers, `min ≤ max` and `min_length ≤ max_length` for the atom families, `min_prefix ≤ max_prefix` and the prefix range for the network families, and any rule a family states that is not a bound pair — together with the conformance of every substituted value to the slot it fills (§5.2, §5.10). A resolver needs no list: the rule an application closes onto is the rule the family already owns for a literal body, asked once more of the closed record. A violation is a resolver error located per §5.10's rule — at the declaration whose text wrote the offending name, with the materialising application as context. For the §5.10 template `vector => <T, N> !array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: N }`, a declaration `hd_row => vector<pixel, 1920>` resolves to

```
@application:{ name: vector  arguments: [ { name: pixel }  { value: 1920 } ] }
hd_row => !array { element_type: pixel  min_items: 1920  max_items: 1920 }
```

while `vector<pixel, 1920>` written at a use site lands on `hd_row`, the declaration that owns it. Where no declaration names it, the use-site application and the structurally identical form written directly as `[pixel; 1920]` anywhere in the schema both land on one *closed synthetic* entry — the same body, `@synthetic` at its key, no `@application` — which is what §5.3's "a use-site sugar form mints where a declared application does not" comes to: the sugar and the application are two spellings of one expansion. `[pixel; 1920]` lands on that synthetic beside `hd_row` too, a declaration naming only itself (above).

*Non-exposure and the import merge.* Minted entries are resolver-materialised, not declared: they cannot be named from any schema source, and authors reach a type by writing the application (reusing the entry) or by declaring a name for it, which is then the entry (above). Under the transitive import merge (§2.2.3) the internal entries travel with their namespace — an imported entry's body references them, so they are part of what the import delivers — and they merge by the same structural identities as within a schema: an importing schema whose own application derives an identical entry denotes the merged entry, never a duplicate, and internal names never collide with declared names by construction (§3.4.1). A later schema's application of a template an imported library declared and applied is unified with the library's declared instantiation through the `@application` the library's output carries (§8.1).

*Diagnostics and cross-schema identity.* Internal names MUST NOT be the primary form shown to users: diagnostics surface the source application or form — recoverable from the originating source position, and from `@application` where an entry carries one — located at the declaration whose text wrote the offending name (§5.10). Cross-schema identity of *declared* types is through named declarations or not at all; internal entries carry structural identity only.


### 8.3 References

A type-def body that is purely a type *reference* — a bare name or a template application, with no body record, no `&`, and no construction — produces a REFERENCE-kind entry whose value is `!reference { target: T }`, where `target` names an entry (§8.1):

- **Simple leaf** (`id => uuid`): `id => !reference { target: uuid }`. The `target` is the immediate referent, not the ultimate type — reference chains (`user_id → id → uuid`, over `id => uuid` and `user_id => id`) appear as distinct entries, each pointing one hop forward.
- **Fully-bound template application** (`string_triple => vector<text, 3>`) is **not** a reference: the declaration is the instantiation entry itself, closed in place with the application as `@application` on its key (§5.10, §8.2), exactly as a sugar-form body — `string_triple => [text; 3]` — is a construction in place (§5.3, §5.6). A `!reference` body is a bare name or a still-open application, never a closed one — with the one exception of a self-reaching expansion's instantiation entry, which is a `!reference` to its own synthetic (§8.2).
- **Open template application** (`same_tree => <T> tree<T>`, `uuid_pair => <B> pair<uuid, B>` — an application of a named template with at least one parameter still open): the declaration is itself a template (§5.10); `target` is the application itself, arguments included — `reference.target` is a `type_ref`, so the alias states what it already binds. Reference-kind templates compose during substitution without materialising intermediate entries.
- **An alias of a constructor** (`my_record => record`, in a meta-schema): `target` may name a constructor, the one slot beside `kind_type.of` and a `!` head that may (§3.3.1), and the alias is applied as its target is.

A top-level *constructor* application is not a reference — it resolves as a construction in place (§5.6). `!reference { target: X }` is the explicit spelling of the alias `X` and denotes exactly that (§5.5).

The correction is worth its sentence: an earlier revision filed a declared application by its syntax — a parameterised *reference* — and made the declaration a hop to something else's identity, so that `pet.subtypes` read a minted name where the author had written `dogs`, and every surface that showed a name had to follow the hop back. Filed by what it is — a construction — the declared name is the entry, which is what a family's `subtypes` index, a tag, and a diagnostic all key on.

**A reference is a hop, not a rewrite.** Resolved output states a reference chain **as the author wrote it**: a use site — a field type, tuple element, choice variant, argument, or binding-record type slot — that names a REFERENCE entry names that entry, and `target` names the next. Nothing is flattened, and no annotation records a source-level name, because the chain itself is the record: an earlier revision rewrote each use site to the chain's terminal entry and attached an `@alias` naming the first hop, which was a second, lossy representation of a walk that happens anyway — a chain of two hops kept the name of the one that carried nothing — and every pass that needs the terminal entry (reader compilation, discrimination-class classification, inhabitance, variant distinctness, kind membership) walks the chain regardless. A processor MAY collapse a chain **after linking, when it compiles for reading** — a REFERENCE entry's reader is its target's, named for the entry doing the referring, which is what gives a diagnostic the alias the author wrote for free — and MUST NOT collapse it in resolved output, which is what two conforming processors compare. The walk stops *at* an argument-bearing target — that is an application, not a hop to another entry, and there is no entry at the end of it until materialisation produces one.

**Where a chain is followed, and where it is not.** Identity of an application follows its arguments' chains to their terminal entries, because a reference is the same type under another name (§8.2); subsumption at a typed position follows both chains (§7.2); the source of a refinement or composition, a choice variant judged for `void`, a discrimination class, a kind's membership and a qualified name's first segment are all read after following the chain (§4.2, §4.3, §5.4, §5.7, §5.12). What the chain *never* does is carry meaning of its own: nothing that changes how a value reads may live on a reference, and the meta layer declares no annotation that does — a fact that decides reading, such as an alphabet, lives in the type (§5.5), where every position already names it. That is what makes a rename safe to see through.

**References are not supertypes.** REFERENCE-kind entries carry no chain and do not contribute to the `supertypes`/`subtypes` graph: `uuid.subtypes` does not list `id`; `id` has no `supertypes`. The three ways to name a type after another, and what each buys, are tabled in §5.7.

**Instantiation entries are not references.** A declared instantiation (§8.2) carries its expansion's kind and a binding-record value, not a `!reference` record; a REFERENCE entry may *point at* one by name (`alias => string_triple`), and the instantiation itself is a closed, concrete definition. The self-reaching instantiation entry of §8.2, and an alias a template's application closes to (`my => id<text>` over `id => <T> T`), are the `!reference` entries that carry `@application`.


## 9. The Meta Layer

Two pre-loaded schemas form the meta layer, and one library beside core instances meta's network constructors. Implementations MUST pre-load the kernel and meta (§3.4, §10.1). The normative source for all three is carried in the companion artifacts (§1.5), pinned by content hash at publication; the pre-loaded in-memory model is authoritative and the documents are descriptions of it.

| Schema | Role | Declares |
|--------|------|----------|
| `2026/38/m/meta-kernel.tn` | Self-referencing bootstrap; its `!!id` and `!!meta` reference its own URL | `top`, abstract; `type => abstract top & { chain?: type_chain }`, the kind of the kinds that describe data values, with `type_chain => { parents?: [type_ref]  supertypes?: [type_name]  subtypes?: [type_name] }` (§4.1); the base kinds `atom`/`product`/`sum`, abstract, each composing `type`; `namespace => abstract top & { key_type: identifier_kind  value_type: type_kind  ordered?: boolean ~ false }`, the constructor whose instances are maps keyed by names, with its two compositions `schema => namespace & { key_type: = type_name  value_type: = top }` and `field_map => namespace & { key_type?: ~ field_name  value_type: = record_field  ordered?: = true }` (§4.1, §5.2, §8.1); `reference` (§8.3); the parameterless `record`/`array`/`set_type`/`map`/`tuple`/`choice` constructors (§4.2) — every type slot typed `type_kind`, `array` and `map` carrying `voidable` and `ordered`, `set_type` refining `array` with `voidable`, `ordered` and `unique_items` pinned and no bound of its own (§5.3); `record` carrying `fields: field_map`, `extension?: record_extension_type ~ OPEN` over `record_extension_type => !enum [ABSTRACT FINAL OPEN]`, `discriminators?: [text; 1..]`, `name_type?: identifier_kind` and `parameters?: [template_param]` (§5.2, §5.10); the `record_field` supporting record with its `type` and four facts `optional`/`voidable`/`role`/`value` over `field_role => !enum [FREE DEFAULT FIXED]`, carrying no name (§5.2, §8.1); `tuple_element` with `voidable`, and `field_group` with `members` as a list of options of `text`, `optional_members` and `optional` (§5.11); the `type_ref`/`type_argument` supporting records (§8.1), with `choice` carrying `disjoint`; the `template` constructor (`top & { parameters: [template_param]  template: text }`) whose instances are held bodies, with `template_param => { name  type: type_kind  bound?: type_kind }` (§5.10); the kinds `kind_type => atom & { of: type_name  members?: [type_name] }`, its inline spelling `kind => <T> !kind_type { of: T }`, and the three named kinds `identifier_kind`, `atom_kind` and `type_kind` (§4.2); the reference atom `ref_type => atom & { of: type_name }` and its inline spelling `ref => <T> !ref_type { of: T }` (§4.2, §5.12); the enum constructor `enum_type` (`name_type: identifier_kind`, `members: enum_set`) and its tightening `enum` (§7.4); the non-empty member sets `enum_set`, `integer_member_set`, `text_member_set` and `scheme_set`; the atom-constructor/instance pairs `value_type`/`value` and `void_type`/`void` (§4.2), `integer_type`/`integer` (with the `integer_size` supporting record, `size`, the bound groups, `multiple_of` and `members`), `text_type`/`text` (with `pattern` and `members`, each settable once, and `normalization` over the `normalization` enum, §5.5, §5.7), `identifier_type`/`identifier` (the UAX #31 profile facets over `identifier_base => !enum [XID ID ASCII NONE]`, §7.4) with its second instance `scheme_name` over the `ASCII` base, `iri_type`/`iri` (RFC 3987: `schemes`, `allow_relative`, `allow_fragment`, the kernel's `iri` requiring a scheme and typing `atom_specification.spec`, §5.5) and `regex_type`/`regex`; the identifier roles `type_name`/`field_name`/`param_name` (naming positions over `identifier`); `non_negative_integer` (`!integer ^ { min: 0 }`, the type of every counting facet); `boolean`; `atom_specification`; the internal enums; the annotation types `annotation`, `doc`, and the resolver's derived `synthetic` and `application => @annotation type_ref` (§6, §8.2) |
| `2026/38/m/meta.tn` | Canonical meta-schema; chains to the kernel and imports it | `set` (the `set<T>` template over `set_type`, §5.3); `bytes_type` (with `bytes_encoding`, §5.5); `scoped` (with `scope_kind`, the non-empty `scope_set`, and `schema_identity => !iri_type { allow_fragment: false }` typing its `schemas` keys, §5.5, §7.8); the constraint-vocabulary constructors for numeric (`float_type` with `ieee_format`, `decimal_type` with `members` over `decimal_member_set`, `rational_type`, `complex_type` with `complex_component` — a partial order under refinement, §5.7), temporal (`date_type`, `time_type`, `datetime_type`, `duration_type`, `period_type` — all five with the exclusive bound groups, the last three of `time`/`datetime`/`duration` carrying `precision`, `duration` and `period` carrying `multiple_of`, §5.5), identifier (`uuid_type`, and `uri_type` with the kernel's `iri_type`'s facets over RFC 3986, §5.5), network (`ipv4_type`, `ipv6_type`, `cidr4_type`, `cidr6_type`, `mac_type`, `hostname_type` with `allow_idn ~ true` over RFC 5890, `host_type` over RFC 3987's `ihost`, and `media_type_type` with `allow_parameters ~ false`, `types` and `suffixes` over RFC 6838 — each constructor's instance living in `net.tn`), and text (`email_type`) families; the annotation types — the classifying markers `ordering`, `bounded`, `exact`, `numeric`; the checked `disjoint`; the documentary `deprecated`, `title`, `comment`, `examples`, `read_only`, `write_only` (§6) — and no representation directive (§6) |
| `2026/38/m/net.tn` | The network library; governed by meta, importing nothing | The instances of meta's network constructors, one each: `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `cidr4`, `cidr6`, `mac`, `hostname`, `host` and `media_type`, each the type [TSON-DATA] §5.5's row of the same name defines; the schemaless annotations `!ipv4` … `!media_type` denote these entries ([TSON-DATA] §5.5) |

Each atom family is a constructor/instance pair (§4.2): the constructor lists the family's constraint fields, and the canonical instance lives in the kernel, in core or in net (`value`, `void`, `integer`, `text`, `identifier`, `iri`, `regex`, the three kinds in the kernel; `number`, `float32`, `float64`, `rational`, `complex`, `date`, `time`, `datetime`, `duration`, `period`, `bytes`, `uri`, `uuid`, `email` and the rest in `core.tn`; `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `cidr4`, `cidr6`, `mac`, `hostname`, `host` and `media_type` in `net.tn`). Two rules hold across every family and are stated once in meta's header: `multiple_of`, wherever it appears, is strictly positive, tests the value's magnitude with the sign ignored, tightens only to an integer multiple, and composes with every other facet; and `members`, wherever it appears, has every member satisfying the body's other facets — `text_type`'s pattern included — and a refinement shrinks the set on the numeric tiers and sets it once on `text_type` (§5.7, §7.4). Every set-typed field is emitted in source declaration order (§7.5). Meta's kernel import is doubly load-bearing: it supplies the kernel types meta's own declarations use and delivers the kernel's structural vocabulary to every meta-governed schema (§3.3.1). Annotation types live in the chain because of the one-hop rule (§3.3.3, §6).

**Core holds only what a schema cannot do without.** §2.2.3 makes it a resolver error for a local declaration to reuse a name the import closure binds, with no hiding, so every name core declares is one no importing schema may declare, and a name core added in a later revision would break every schema already using it. Core therefore declares the types a schema cannot reasonably write for itself: the atoms — `number`, `rational`, `complex`, `float32`, `float64`, `bytes`, the five temporal families, `uuid`, `email`, `uri_reference` and `uri`, `iri_reference` and `iri` — the integer widths `int8`–`int256` and `uint8`–`uint256`, the `scoped` instances `declared`, `extern` and `dynamic` with the templates `extern_of` and `extern_type` (§7.8), and the templates `set`, `kind`, `tuple1` and `voidable_tuple1` (§4.2, §5.3). It leaves to the schema that wants one any name that stands for a single line it can write itself: a sign-bounded integer or a non-empty text is a refinement declared where it is wanted (`count => !integer ^ { min: 0 }`), and a schema that types a value or a map key as a name constructs its own `identifier` (§7.4). **A family most schemas never use belongs in a library outside core**, where its names cost only the schemas that import it: the network families — `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `cidr4`, `cidr6`, `mac`, `hostname`, `host` and `media_type` — are `net.tn`'s, so a schema importing core alone may declare a `mac` or a `hostname` of its own, and one that wants the network types imports `net.tn` as well. Core declares fresh siblings of kernel and meta entries under the same names — `void`, `boolean`, `integer`, `text`, `regex`, `iri`, `set` and `kind`, and one annotation type, `doc`: the same constructions and contracts, distinct type entities (§3.3.5) — so that data documents and user schemas governed by or importing core can use them. Its `iri` is the one sibling written differently from its original — as a refinement of `iri_reference`, so that every `iri` IS-A `iri_reference` — and its resolved body is the kernel's. Core declares no `ref` (§4.2): a reference names an entry that holds a value, which only a kernel-governed schema has. There is one `bytes`, with no spelled siblings (§5.5).

**JSON.** A JSON document is read through the JSON encoding of [TSON-JSON], a second encoding of this model and not a mode of the TSON notation ([TSON-DATA] §6): it maps a JSON number to `number`, JSON `null` to void — admitted where the position is voidable (§7.6) — and a JSON object to a record where its keys are names its record admits, identifiers by default (§5.2). Every type doc in the artifacts describes its value space without reference to any encoding and names TSON text where it means it; the family facts of §5.2 and the `disjoint` fact of §5.4 are the model's, and both encodings read them.

Users normally chain to `meta.tn`. Schemas that chain to `meta-kernel.tn` directly are alternative type vocabularies replacing meta, or extensions of the meta layer — the format's sanctioned extension point (§2.2.2).

**Guidance for extension meta-schemas.** Three rules keep extended vocabularies coherent with the kernel's. First, **a slot that names a type is typed by a kind, a slot that names a value by a reference, and `type_ref` is for a slot that holds an application** (§4.2, §5.12): a field whose value is the name of a type a value can have is `type_kind`, or a narrower kind — `request?: kind<record>` admits the names of the record types and refuses `uuid`'s — which is what makes the slot judged a member when the schema links and visible to every reference mechanism (§8.1, §8.3), where a `type_name`-typed slot would carry a bare token the resolver treats as data; a field whose value is a value declared elsewhere, written by its name, is `ref<T>`, which declares the spelling and the judgement at once; and `type_ref` stands only where an application may. The kernel's own `enum_type.name_type` and `record.name_type` are `identifier_kind`, and `kind_type.of` is typed `type_name`, since its values are constructors' names, which no kind covers (§4.2); so is `ref_type.of`, whose parameter `T` is thereby a value parameter whose argument is a name resolved in the type-name namespace (§4.2, §5.10), and so are the resolver-derived name lists `kind_type.members`, `type_chain.supertypes` and `subtypes` (§4.1, §4.2). Second, a meta layer's own vocabulary — the records its constructors' instances hold — is declared beside them, as a **namespace type** declares the map a governed schema holds a value of (`interface => namespace & { key_type: = method_name  value_type: = method }`, §4.1), and never imported from an ordinary library (§2.2.3): a meta layer declares the `text`, `boolean` or `uri` its vocabulary needs, or reaches the kernel's through its import. Third, the kernel's `regex` atom is pinned to a specification (`atom_specification`, §4.2), and the pin is a strict gate: an implementation MUST implement the pinned dialect as specified — not a host library's near-relative — and MUST document any divergence it cannot avoid; a pattern using a feature outside the pinned dialect is a resolver error at schema load, not a silently host-dependent behaviour. The same discipline applies to every spec-pinned atom an extension declares: the `spec` pin is the contract, and "whatever the platform regex engine does" is never a conforming reading.


## 10. Schema Resolution Model

Schema URLs are **logical identifiers first**: a URL names a schema, and resolving it does not by itself require a network request. Implementations resolve schema references through a **schema library** — a local store mapping canonical identities ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1) to schema content. Fetching is a permitted but opt-in way to *populate* the library (§10.1, §11.2), not the meaning of a reference.


### 10.1 The Schema Library

When the resolver encounters a `!!schema`, `!!meta`, or `!!import` directive, it looks the reference up in the library — the reference read to its canonical identity in any spelling ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1); if not found, the resolver does not attempt to fetch unless fetching is enabled (§11.2), and reports one of two outcomes, neither a finding about validity ([TSON-DATA] §8.1). A schema this processor **will not** supply — one it does not hold and will not fetch, a host off its allowlist, an illegal identity, a missing required pin, or one past its size limit (§11.2) — is a **refusal**: a decision of this deployment's configuration, as deployment-specific as an identifier policy, which a processor configured otherwise does not make and no rerun changes, so the document is rejected *here* and the sender's fix is to name a schema this deployment will supply. A closed library's miss is a refusal, never "not found": the library is the deployment's whole configuration, so a schema outside it is one this deployment will not supply. A schema this processor **could not** obtain — a source that looks beyond its configuration (an origin, a directory) and found nothing at the reference, a host that did not answer, a fetch that timed out — leaves the document **undetermined**: nothing has read the schema, so nothing is known about whether the document conforms, and whoever holds the fix, for nothing at the reference the sender most likely does, a reference with nothing behind it being most often a typo. Both are distinguishable from the four categories, in the same report, located at the reference, and name their reason (not held, not permitted, too large; not found, unreachable, timed out), since a sender acting on a report needs to tell "your document is wrong" from "I will not look here" from "I could not look". A document with both an undetermined import and an ordinary error reports both, and a rejection settles the summary whatever else went unjudged ([TSON-DATA] §8.1). The library is populated through three mechanisms, in order of precedence:

- **Pre-loaded schemas.** The implementation ships the meta-kernel and meta-schema as pre-loaded entries and SHOULD ship the core type library and the network library. Pre-loaded schemas are authoritative — their in-memory representation takes precedence over any external source (§3.4).
- **Registered schemas.** Applications register schemas before parsing documents that reference them, from local files, embedded resources, or any application-specific source.
- **Fetched schemas (optional).** Implementations MAY support fetching by URL; fetching MUST be explicitly enabled by the application — never the default — and is subject to §11.2. Production systems SHOULD register all required schemas at startup rather than fetch at runtime.

**Identity agreement.** Registration whose target identity differs from the canonical identity of the content's declared `!!id` is an error — the library MUST reject it as identity confusion. The comparison is over canonical identities, so scheme variance agrees; a differing host or path does not. Content with no `!!id` MAY be registered under an application-supplied identity (a development-mode convenience, §2.2.1); content with an `!!id` is registered under that identity and no other.

**Schema sources are schema documents.** Whenever the library is populated from a document — registered or fetched, and for the targets of `!!schema`, `!!meta`, and `!!import` alike — that document MUST classify as a schema document ([TSON-DATA] §2.2). A resolved-schema data document (resolver output, §8) is not a valid schema source: its derived fields cannot be verified from the document alone; it is non-canonical and not hash-pinnable; and, being a data document, its header cannot carry `!!import` — the described schema's namespace is not expressible in it, only in the source schema's own header (§8.1's ingest rule). An implementation MUST reject a data document supplied as the content of a schema URL, with a categorized diagnostic. Resolved-form documents MAY enter the library only through the explicit ingest path (§8.1), which does not take derived indexes on trust: the derived annotation `@synthetic` and the derived indexes `subtypes`, `members` and `disjoint` are discarded and recomputed; `chain.supertypes`, `chain.parents` and `@application` are read as stated and verified; author-written key and value annotations are preserved as data; and a namespace entry (§4.1) ingests like any other entry — its value is the map its type describes, each value ordinary data of the meta-schema's vocabulary validated against it, with the rule that no type position names it (§4.1) re-run on the ingested map.


### 10.2 Hash-Pinned References

The hash-parameter URI convention — the algorithm-named query parameter, lowercase full-length hex, the query-is-hash-parameters-only rule, canonical identity, and the mismatch rule — is defined in [TSON-DATA] §2.2.1. This section defines how the library applies it.

Library keys are canonical identities: a plain reference, its `http`/`https` variant, and its hash-pinned form all name the same entry — the pinned form additionally demands verification. When a hashed reference resolves, the implementation MUST verify the library's content against the declared hash before use; a mismatch is a **resolver error** — a verdict, unlike a refused or undetermined schema (§10.1), because the schema *was* obtained, its bytes were read, and they are not the bytes the document's reference commits to, which is a finding about the reference the document carries — and the library MUST NOT silently substitute mismatched content. When registration supplies a document whose declared `!!id` itself carries a hash, the implementation SHOULD verify at registration time.

**Hashes attach to the canonical entry.** Verification is a property of the canonical identity, not of each reference. Within a single resolution's reference closure (§3.4.1), the digests declared across all references to one identity form a set, and the set determines the identity's treatment. An empty set resolves unverified. A single digest is verified once against the entry's content, before any reference to the identity resolves; every reference — pinned and plain alike — then resolves to the verified entry. A plain reference alongside a pinned one is therefore not a conflict but an inheritance: it rides the pin, and it inherits the pin's failure — a verification failure applies to the identity for the whole resolution, and a plain reference MUST NOT proceed with content a declared digest has rejected. Two distinct digests for one identity is a resolver error: at most one describes the real bytes, and the library MUST NOT choose between them. A verified entry, once cached under its canonical identity, is immutable. A *failed* verification SHOULD NOT be cached under the canonical identity; an implementation MAY maintain a separate negative cache keyed on the full reference string.

**Pins to pre-loaded identities.** Pre-loaded schemas resolve to in-memory structures (§3.4, §10.1), but a pinned reference to a pre-loaded identity is an ordinary pinned reference and MUST be verifiable: an implementation MUST hold, for each pre-loaded schema, the published content digest (or the canonical document bytes) of the artifact its structures implement, and MUST verify declared pins against it — a mismatch is the ordinary verification error. One reference in the series can never carry a pin: the meta-kernel's self-referencing `!!meta`, whose hash input would contain the pin itself. It needs none — it resolves by pre-loading (§3.4) — and under the per-identity rule its plain spelling composes without conflict with pinned references to the kernel elsewhere in a closure.

An implementation MAY offer a strict resolution mode that rejects any closure containing an identity whose digest set is empty (pre-loaded identities excepted), for deployments requiring a fully verified chain; the per-identity rule above is the default and the interchange baseline.

When no digest is declared for an identity anywhere in the closure, it resolves without integrity verification — appropriate for development, NOT RECOMMENDED for production interchange. References that cross a trust boundary SHOULD be hash-pinned: a schema's `!!meta` and `!!import` values pin its contract and dependencies; a data document's `!!schema` pins its vocabulary. Because pinning is per-identity, one pinned reference raises the guarantee for every reference to that identity in the closure, and a published plain reference never prevents downstream pinning. Pinning composes into the verification chain of [TSON-DATA] §2.2.1 — a consumer holding a single hashed reference can verify a document together with its schema, that schema's meta-schema, and the kernel, provided each rung's own references are pinned; published schemas SHOULD pin every reference they carry, the kernel's self-reference being the sole exception.

```
!!schema:"https://example.com/people.tn?sha256=9f86d081884c7d659a2feaa0c55ad015a3bf4f1b2b0b822cd15d6c15b0f00a08"
```


### 10.3 Canonical Identity

Reference identity is defined by [TSON-DATA] §2.2.1: two references name the same document if and only if their canonical identities — the host's canonical text plus the path, scheme and query removed — are byte-for-byte identical. The library applies this as its key rule:

- `http://tson.io/2026/38/m/core.tn` and `https://tson.io/2026/38/m/core.tn` — **same** entry; the scheme is a transport hint.
- `https://tson.io/2026/38/m/core.tn` and the same URL with `?sha256=…` — same entry; the pinned form additionally requires verification (§10.2).
- `https://tson.io/2026/38/m/core.tn` and `https://elsewhere.example/2026/38/m/core.tn` — **different** entries; the host is load-bearing, so a fetch-endpoint change cannot silently redirect a name.
- `https://xn--bcher-kva.example/sch%c3%a9mas/x.tn` and `https://bücher.example/schémas/x.tn` — **same** entry; a reference in any spelling of an identity is read to the canonical form, the host as a `host` value and the path's percent-encoded UTF-8 read back ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1).

The identity profile holds a document's own `!!id` to one spelling — the canonical form, with no userinfo, port, percent-encoded unreserved character, dot-segment or fragment — and reads a *reference* in any spelling of the same identity to that form before comparison; what has no IRI form is an error, never a candidate for normalization ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1). A fetcher that reaches a host beyond US-ASCII does so in the URI spelling, and a report of such a fetch SHOULD name both the identity and the URI requested. The pin rides in the query so that it reaches the origin and the cache, and the fragment is reserved for a later intra-document reference ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1). Content addressing is over bytes, and every identity rule above a digest is over value spaces (§5.5): two spellings of one octet string hash the same document to one identity and are one map key, and a content-addressed reference is never encoding-dependent.

**Confusable identities.** Because identity selects a document's entire type vocabulary, a look-alike identity is a higher-value spoof than a confusable field name. Implementations processing untrusted references SHOULD surface, and MAY reject, two registered identities that differ only in ways the profile does not already forbid — e.g. visually confusable host labels (UTS #39; [TSON-DATA] §9.4).


## 11. Security Considerations

The security considerations of [TSON-DATA] §9 apply; this section adds the schema layer's.


### 11.1 Schema Validation

Documents without `!!schema` carry no type guarantees — only base type resolution applies. Applications processing untrusted TSON input SHOULD validate against a schema before use.


### 11.2 External References

Schema URLs are logical identifiers resolved through the schema library (§10), not fetch instructions; no network access occurs unless the application explicitly enables it. Implementations that support optional fetching MUST treat it as opt-in, disabled by default, and when enabled SHOULD enforce:

- **Transport security.** Fetched references MUST use an authenticated, encrypted transport (in practice, `https`); a fetcher MUST NOT downgrade to a cleartext scheme for an unpinned reference.
- **Allowlists.** Restrict fetchable references to approved hosts or path prefixes, matched on canonical identity (§10.3) so scheme variance cannot evade the list; a host is matched as a `host` value ([TSON-DATA] §5.5), so an allowlist entry in either label form admits an identity's host in either, and a look-alike host (`schemаs.example.com` with a Cyrillic `а`) is refused unless the operator listed it, the comparison being by name. `!!schema` and `!!meta` references are particularly sensitive: a malicious reference could load an untrusted schema that redefines expected types. A reference the allowlist refuses is reported as a refusal ([TSON-DATA] §8.1; §10.1), and the report SHOULD NOT disclose the allowlist: telling an untrusted sender which hosts this processor will fetch from is reconnaissance for request forgery, where a name policy's statement serves the sender and costs nothing.
- **Content hash verification.** Require hashes on fetched references and reject mismatches — preventing tampering and silent schema drift; a reference whose pin is required and missing is a refusal.
- **Size limits** on fetched content; a schema past the limit is a refusal.
- **No recursive fetching.** A fetched schema's own `!!import` directives MUST NOT trigger further fetches; transitive dependencies must be pre-registered or pre-fetched.
- **Caching.** Cache verified schemas locally, keyed on canonical identity; a verified hash-matching schema is immutable. A failed verification MUST NOT overwrite or poison the canonical-identity entry (§10.2).

Production systems SHOULD pre-register all required schemas at startup and disable runtime fetching entirely.


### 11.3 Directive Security

Directives are a control channel that affects interpretation. Applications processing untrusted input SHOULD restrict which directives are accepted; `!!meta` and `!!import` are particularly sensitive because they select and extend the type vocabulary — production systems MAY restrict which meta and import URLs are permitted. See [TSON-DATA] §9.3.


### 11.4 Name Hygiene at the Schema Layer

[TSON-DATA] §8.2 defines the name-hygiene mechanisms — skeleton distinctness within a scope, `Identifier_Status`, and a restriction level — as policy that every conforming processor implements and enforces by default, never as validity. This section supplies the schema layer's **scopes** and the one rule the scope structure adds.

The named scopes at this layer are: the members of one enum (§7.4), under its `name_type`'s profile — all three mechanisms, every enum's name type being an identifier family; the field names of one record definition (member labels of its groups included, §5.11), under the record's `name_type`'s profile (§5.2), which is the same scope the data layer applies to a schemaless record ([TSON-DATA] §2.5, §8.2) — a profile's own added characters (`json_name`'s `@` and `$`, a medial space) are exempt from mechanism 2 and read by mechanisms 1 and 3, and the floor on what a profile may add is §7.4's; the declared names of one schema; and **the merged namespace at `!!import`** — the sharpest, because §2.2.3 decides collisions by exact identity, which a confusable pair passes by construction: two entries a reviewer reads as one name are, to the resolver, two names. Choice variants are not a scope: a variant is a reference to a declared name, so two confusable variants are two confusable entries in the namespace and are reported there. A template's parameter list is not a scope either (§5.10): one author writes it whole on one line, and the list stays short so that it stays reviewable. Mechanisms 2 and 3 are per-name and reach every identifier position without a scope ([TSON-DATA] §8.2); only mechanism 1 needs the list above.

**Values that are names.** A value whose declared type is an identifier family (§7.4) is a name wherever it stands, and the schema layer is what makes it one. Mechanisms 2 and 3 reach every such value, in every encoding, under the identifier policy and under the family's own profile: a character the profile adds meets no `Identifier_Status` rule, and a per-segment level divides the value at the profile's own separators ([TSON-DATA] §8.2). Mechanism 1 gains two **data scopes**, each a set the type itself delimits: the key set of one map whose key type is an identifier family, and the element set of one array whose elements are unique — a `set_type` instance, or any array stating `unique_items` — and whose element type is one. An array that admits repetition is not a scope, since nothing says two of its elements name two things. A pair is refused at the second key or element, as a pair of field names is, and like every other scope these are skipped where the identifier policy switches skeleton distinctness off. A value a *schema* writes is judged the same way, under the same policy: a field's default or fixed value of such a type when the schema loads, since a default reaches every document that omits the field, and a value in a payload the scopes above do not cover — the keys of a namespace entry's map (`orders => !interface { place_order => … }`, whose `key_type` is an identifier family, §4.1), a qualified name's segments (§5.12), or an annotation's value. A kernel constructor's own identifier-typed values are the scopes listed above, or references to a declaration, and are judged there; a kind's or a reference's value is a name of a declared entry and is judged where the entry is declared. A map keyed by `text` remains data: `admin` and `аdmin` may be two legitimately distinct keys, and nothing about a map position says they are names — the key's type is the schema saying so.

Because identity selects a document's entire type vocabulary, the import merge is exactly where the compositional hazard bites — two independently published, independently pinned schemas, each clean alone, can collide on a skeleton when one imports the other (`list_item` beside `Iist_item`), and the fix is a rename in a document the importing author may neither control nor republish. That is the strongest single reason the mechanism is policy rather than validity: as a validity rule it would make such an import impossible to write; as a policy rule the operator relaxes the check, visibly and in code, and proceeds. A refusal at the merge is reported at the second arrival with both declaring schemas named, in the manner of §2.2.3's collision diagnostic, as [TSON-DATA] §8.1's refused outcome — in the same report as the four categories, distinguishable from them — and the report carries the UCD version and the identifier and token policies it was judged under ([TSON-DATA] §8.2). The policy is not a property of a schema and no schema carries one: the merged namespace is what the check runs over, and only the importing site knows it.
